# 2023 — Prologue: The Cloth Nobody Owned
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · NONCANON literary draft · proposed secondary-world staging*

**Source boundary:** The names and scores that will appear in Week 1 come from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json` (Flaim / ESPN league 1417621 archival extract, source blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`). The team names are 2023 banners, not verified identities of their human owners. Jake's appearance is a **proposed literary continuity for the user-attested Team 1 association**, not provider-authenticated historical ownership. The awning, repair bench, unnamed settlement, tradespeople, cloth, conversations and choices in this story are **PROPOSED FICTION**, not league events or Atlas-accepted geography. Principal interiority is restricted to `CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`; other people are externally observed, not historical-owner POVs. No result after the opening week is known to the characters. This prologue precedes the six certified Week 1 results.

---

The cloth above the waiting bench had six bad knots. Everybody who passed under it could count them. Nobody had counted the people who came there when it rained.

Jake had watched the sixth knot fail from the dry side of the street. A small cracking noise, scarcely audible over wheels and conversation, and then a corner of the awning folded inward. The water that had gathered in its belly came down all at once. Three travelers stood up with their blankets over their heads. An old woman picked a wet loaf from the stones. A boy clapped because he had never seen so much water fall in such a neat square.

For a moment Jake was tempted to laugh with him. The boy had no share in the awning's failure. Neither did he.

Then the old woman placed the loaf on the bench, looked at the water running off it, and began to break away the soaked crust. Jake crossed the street.

The waiting place belonged to no banner. That was what the notice at the end of the bench said, though somebody had added in a thinner hand that its upkeep belonged to whoever was willing. A roof without an owner was one of the few things everyone praised and nobody repaired.

Two workers were already trying to gather the fallen corner. One stood on a stool with a strip of rope between his teeth. The other kept the stool steady with both hands. Neither wore a crest. The awning was too heavy for the man above to lift and too wet for him to let go.

"Put your foot where the wood is sound," Jake said.

The man looked down at him. "Would you show me which part is sound?"

Jake examined the stool. There was a split through the nearest rung. If the worker climbed higher it would probably give. Jake could imagine the fall with awful precision: the man, the wet cloth, the stool collapsing beneath them, and the crowd afterward insisting that somebody should have known.

He put his shoulder beneath the canvas instead. Its weight surprised him.

"That helps," the worker said, and began to free the rope from the broken eyelet.

For a little while there was only the ordinary awkward labor of three people who had not practiced together. Jake shifted his feet. The worker on the stool drew the rope out, discovered it was frayed nearly through, and tossed it down. The other worker caught it without releasing the stool. Jake could not turn his head to see how she managed that.

"Somebody paid for these last year," he said.

"Somebody paid for the knots," said the worker on the stool. "Not the hands to tie them again."

The sentence annoyed him, partly because it made sense. A great many things in the Commonwealth were easy to buy once and expensive to keep. He knew that from the Contest; success was never the same as the maintenance of success. But the thought sounded foolish beside an old woman eating the edible edges of a wet loaf.

When the corner was drawn back to its support, the workers tied a temporary loop. The fabric hung crooked. Water still ran along the seam and dripped from a low point directly onto the middle of the bench.

"Six places," the woman holding the stool said. "Six lengths of clean cord, six patches, six people who won't disappear before dark. We can make it hold."

"Why six?"

"Because I have already counted five failures, and you are standing under the sixth."

She had counted more carefully than he had.

Across the road, two children were arguing over a scrap of colored fabric. One pulled it toward his chest; the other said the color belonged to his people. Their mother took the scrap away from both of them and wrapped it around the handle of a carrying basket. Jake watched this small confiscation become a useful binding.

The Contest would soon supply more colors than the street could hold. Banners would be carried in triumph, and banners would be folded away. The victors would call their cloth a sign of right, and the defeated would protect their last decent lengths from the rain. This season, too, would begin with people certain they knew which material was worth keeping.

He asked the worker how much cord was needed.

"Enough for six knots that don't come loose when they're wet."

"I meant the measure."

"I gave you the measure."

Jake reached into his coat and brought out the narrow strip of ribbon he had planned to wear when the Benedict Kenobi banner went up. He had bought it for the opening, a private extravagance. It was not long enough to close even one of the broken seams.

The woman regarded it without reaching for it.

"Pretty," she said.

"It's strong."

"Then pull."

He held one end while she took the other. The thread gave a little and returned. She tested the edges, ran a thumb along the weave and handed it back.

"You could spare that," she said.

He disliked the way she had phrased it. He had brought the ribbon as a promise to himself, and she had transformed it into something that could be given away. For a moment he felt the irrational resentment of a man asked for too little to make refusal respectable.

He untied the knot around his wrist.

"One," he said.

"Not yet." The worker took the ribbon and laid it beside the wet rope. "It has to be put where it can hold."

By evening the awning was no longer falling. It was not repaired, either. The worker who had climbed the stool went away carrying the broken eyelet. The woman folded the loose corner and weighted it with two bricks. Travelers returned under the portion that still offered shelter, each of them choosing a seat by a calculation of drops.

Jake left without his ribbon. He walked half a street before realizing he was looking backward for the flash of its color.

The next morning would bring the first public reckonings of a new season. There would be names above numbers and numbers beside names. It was possible, he thought, to spend an entire life learning how to read those columns and never once learn what the person beside you required.

He went home with rain in his collar and the uncomfortable knowledge that he had not repaired anything. He had only promised one knot.

There were five more to find.

# 2023 — Week 1: Six Knots
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six certified matchups · original NONCANON prose*

**Historical evidence:** `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, 2023 Week 1, six matches, twelve distinct archived slots (Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`). The numerical results and the home/away field are archival competition facts. **Home/away is not a verified physical venue.** Names refer only to season-specific team banners; human owners, travel, military events and motives are not provider-authenticated. No transaction is asserted. All awning, bench, cord, work parties, dialogue, spectators, weather and material consequences are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. Only Jake has interior focalization, as a proposed `CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER` Team 1 literary candidate; the other five encounters use external, non-focalized chronicle narration. Non-POV civilian actions are invented and do not establish new principal viewpoints. El Niño is treated as a storm-name/banner, never a speaking person. Each scene knows only the opening week's result, never future results.

---

## I. Benedict Arnold and Two Dreaded Snake — The Sound of a Rope

**VERIFIED:** Slot 8, **Benedict Arnold (home), 130.94**, defeated slot 6, **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 79.68**. Margin **51.26**. The archival name `Two Dreaded Snake` is preserved exactly and is not retroactively rewritten as the 2026 banner or visual morphology.

A cord could be judged in two ways: by the weight it held and by the sound it made when it broke.

Beneath the public awning the worker who had counted the failing knots brought out a length of dark rope. She put it across two trestles and invited the people who had gathered to pull it. There was no reward for winning the test, only the knowledge that a canvas roof could be left hanging above strangers without endangering them. That ought to have made the task simple. Instead several spectators wore the colors of competing houses, and every strain in the rope became a demonstration of somebody's strength.

When the first public result was delivered, it carried Benedict Arnold well ahead of Two Dreaded Snake. The herald read **130.94 against 79.68** and repeated the latter figure when a watcher insisted he had heard it incorrectly. The gap was too large to be explained away by the corner of a sheet or an uncertain mark.

A supporter of the winning banner offered a dyed cord. It had been prepared for a celebratory standard; its knots were ornamental and its ends were bound with bright wire. The worker tested one end, then cut off the wire before allowing the rope near the awning.

"That's our crest," the supporter objected.

"It's a roof," she replied.

He demanded the piece back. The worker held the severed decoration in her palm until he took it. The useful cord remained on the trestle. Nobody applauded; the division of property was too small to deserve ceremony and too exact to permit misunderstanding.

Across from them a spectator wrapped in the losing colors untied a plain length of binding from his bundle. He laid it beside the dyed one. The two ropes were nearly equal when stretched taut.

"Which is stronger?" a child asked.

The woman fastened both lengths to the same cracked eyelet and invited the child to pull. One held, then the other held. Rainwater dripped from the canvas above onto all three of them. She could not declare a victor from the test, and for a time the public watched a test with no winner.

The certified contest already had one. The roof did not care.

A knot was made and dressed with a blunt wooden pin. The worker pulled it twice. The first repaired corner did not rise very far, but its downward sag ceased to deepen.

The strip of colored wire went away in a closed fist. The cord stayed behind.

## II. The KING of the Octagon Village and Benedict Kenobi — What the Cloth Could Bear

**VERIFIED:** Slot 3, **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 190.74**, defeated slot 1, **Benedict Kenobi (away), 113.02**. Margin **77.72**. Jake's focal participation is an expressly noncanonical, user-attested literary candidate, not verified historical human-owner binding.

Jake had gone to the repair bench for his ribbon.

The woman from the previous evening had promised to keep it dry. He intended only to make sure she had done so before the day's announcements began, because he had begun to worry that a promise made while holding up a falling roof had been mistaken for a gift. He found the ribbon stretched between two nails, holding a loose seam flat while the worker measured it.

"That isn't where you said you would put it," he told her.

"Not yet," she said. "I said we would put it where it holds."

He would have answered, but a runner came down the street carrying a folded results sheet. A crowd followed the runner as if the sheet itself were on fire. Jake knew the names written on it. In the opening encounter, his Benedict Kenobi banner had faced The KING of the Octagon Village.

He expected a contest. What the paper delivered was a distance.

**190.74** for The KING. **113.02** for Benedict Kenobi.

A margin of **77.72**.

Jake read the numbers until their shapes became the same pale marks on the page. He had prepared, in the private chamber of his imagination, a dozen speeches for close defeat. He had even prepared a dignified silence for a loss by ten or fifteen. He had not prepared to see such a difference written in public ink while someone measured how little ribbon he had offered a roof.

The runner moved on. The crowd followed. A child stepped from the bench into a puddle left by the broken awning, and the woman caught him by the collar before he could slip.

"Will that cloth help?" Jake asked.

"It can keep one tear from traveling. It won't hold the whole weight."

He looked again at the ribbon. It had seemed sufficient when he wore it alone. Its inadequacy was obvious when laid against the great sodden acreage above them.

"Then I'll find more."

"Where?"

He had already opened his mouth. The question stopped him.

The marketplace sold cord, but his purse was light. A claimant might ask others to contribute, but after such a loss he could imagine the answer being spoken in laughter. He could cut cloth from a banner, but then there would be a quarrel about whose banner had been cut. All his choices became arguments before they became repairs.

The worker lifted the ribbon and handed it to him. "You can take it back. Nobody has stitched it yet."

It would have been easy. He could put the strip in his pocket, leave the six knots to people who worked with their hands, and retreat to the private world where the figures still belonged to him alone.

Instead he gave it back.

"Keep the first piece," he said. "I can carry water or hold a ladder. Tell me which."

She pointed to a bucket and an empty basin beneath the leaking seam. "Empty that before it pulls the whole thing down."

His defeat did not teach him a great truth. He would mistrust any story that said it had. It left him doing work he could measure in small journeys: basin to street, street to basin, the water always returning faster than he could carry it. By dusk his hands were sore, and the bench was still not dry.

But the third traveler who came seeking shelter no longer had to stand in the falling water.

Jake watched her sit. Nobody asked whose banner he had served.

## III. Benedict Coomhersnatch and Arm Chair GM — The Shorter Plank

**VERIFIED:** Slot 5, **Benedict Coomhersnatch (away), 144.28**, defeated slot 11, **Arm Chair GM (home), 119.34**. Margin **24.94**. These banners do not authenticate a shared historical owner with Benedict Kenobi, Benedict Arnold, or Eggs Benedict.

The plank brought to the awning was too short.

It was a handsome plank, rubbed smooth by some earlier use, and the man who carried it was proud enough to ask that it be placed above the central bench. He represented no verified owner in this chronicle; the plank and its bearer belong wholly to the proposed story. He stood it against the wall while the second public reckoning was read.

The paper gave Benedict Coomhersnatch **144.28** and Arm Chair GM **119.34**. A listener repeated "Benedict" before the herald had finished the name, and somebody else answered as though the word referred to the same banner already heard that morning. The herald stopped, returned to the first syllable, and read the complete name again.

"Not the other one," he said.

"Which other one?"

"The one you have in mind."

Nobody could agree which that was. A boy pressed his finger against the roster of names and showed his grandmother that the letters had different endings. She nodded as though that settled the matter. It did not settle the argument among the adults.

At the repair bench, the plank bearer lifted his gift toward the broken support. The wood would not span the gap. He shifted it left; the right edge hung in the air. He shifted it right; the left edge abandoned its peg. He proposed trimming the damaged support so that the plank would fit.

The woman with the tools refused. To make the gap smaller, she said, they would have to cut away material that still bore the awning's weight.

The man set the plank down hard enough to raise dust. "It's good wood."

"Yes," she said. "For a shorter place."

An older repairer took the plank to the side bench, where a leg had been failing for weeks. He braced the leg and drove two wooden wedges through the new support. Someone sat on it to test the fit. The bench ceased to rock.

The donor returned to inspect the work. His plank had been saved from embarrassment by being put somewhere he had not intended. He ran a hand along its grain. Then he took away his measuring string, leaving the wood.

The result of the public contest remained unchanged. So did the obligation to say each of the four Benedict-named banners in full, however inconvenient that was for a crowded mouth.

Above them the awning still needed its third knot.

## IV. Red Leopards and The LLC — The Price on the Nail

**VERIFIED:** Slot 2, **Red Leopards (away), 149.76**, defeated slot 4, **The LLC (home), 121.06**. Margin **28.70**. The 2026 identities of Kevin and David are not asserted to own the 2023 slots.

At the edge of the shelter a little board appeared bearing three prices. One for standing in the dry, another for sitting, and the third for putting a bundle beneath the bench.

The board was clean. The figures had been painted by somebody who knew how to keep his brush from shaking. A hired attendant with ink on his thumb stood beside it collecting coins into a lidded tin. For the first hour the arrangement worked, at least for those who had money. The people who did not moved beyond the drip line and stood with their bundles against their ankles.

Then the fourth result was brought.

Red Leopards had taken **149.76** against The LLC's **121.06**. Some watchers turned the margin into a jest about whether prices were evidence of strength. Others protested that a ledger of earnings could not be reduced to a single competitive defeat. In the telling proposed here, none of them could establish who had actually authorized the board. The name on it was a word for the service, not a certified claim on the wood beneath it.

The repair woman came from the far support carrying two wet lengths of cord. She stopped at the board.

"Whose bench?" she asked.

"Nobody's," said the attendant.

"Then who granted you permission to rent it?"

He lifted his tin. "People have been paying."

"That is not the same answer."

The old woman whose bread had been soaked the day before arrived under the edge of the cloth. She examined the board, counted coins in her hand, and stepped away. There was enough for the dry place, but not enough to keep the bundle of clean clothes from getting wet. She could leave the bundle outside, or sit with it in the rain. The attendant offered no third arrangement.

The worker set the cords down. She took the board off its nail, turned it over, and wrote something on the unpainted wood with a piece of charcoal. The attendant began to object. She handed him back the tin.

"It is still your money," she said. "The board is not yours."

On the reverse she wrote: KEEP THE WALK CLEAR. DO NOT CUT THE SUPPORTS.

The attendant stood for a while with the prices facing his chest, holding a commercial rule nobody would now read. Then he went away.

No official order had been issued. No source claims the Red Leopards' victory broke a real business or altered Commonwealth property law. In this fiction, one improvised toll had failed because the people under the awning refused to let it endure.

The old woman sat, her bundle beside her, and watched the workers pull the next wet rope into place.

## V. El Niño and The Blew Waffle's — A Name for Weather

**VERIFIED:** Slot 9, **El Niño (home), 178.70**, defeated slot 7, **The Blew Waffle's (away), 86.86**. Margin **91.84**. The historical banner is not treated as a speaking humanoid or an established meteorological report.

In the afternoon the wind changed.

That is a statement about the imagined weather of this draft, not a fact derived from the competitive record. The people under the awning felt the new gust first in their sleeves and then in the fabric above them. A corner that had held steady began to shudder. A loose pole knocked against stone three times.

When the next tally came, its names made the change in the air difficult to ignore. **El Niño, 178.70. The Blew Waffle's, 86.86.** A difference of **91.84**.

A man near the awning said the storm had won. Another said the name was enough to bring bad weather. A third insisted the gust had arrived before the herald and could not reasonably be credited to a document. Their arguments were loud enough that they failed to hear the pole knock a fourth time.

The repair woman heard it. She set her basket down and called for the men at the nearer trestle to lower the canvas. One argued that the new knots had been carefully tested. She answered that a knot tested under ordinary load was not a roof tested under every possible wind.

They lowered it together. At first the canvas came easily. Then a pocket of air caught beneath it and hauled one edge above the people's heads. Several hands were lifted to catch the hem. For an instant the great sheet looked like a sail with no vessel attached.

The repair woman told them to release the edge.

They obeyed slowly. The canvas struck the stones. Water ran out from its folds. There were angry cries from people who had been promised shelter, but nobody had been pulled from the bench or thrown by the pole.

The worker knelt beside the flattened cloth, testing the third knot between thumb and forefinger. Its cord was intact. The failure was in their assumption that the awning always had to remain raised.

A few travelers collected their bundles and sought more solid walls. Others remained, helping fold the fabric into a length that would not take the wind.

The name on the public sheet remained a name. The storm, in this telling, did not give a speech, claim a throne, or issue commands. It merely made ordinary people change how they held a piece of cloth.

## VI. Mixon it up for a week and Eggs Benedict — The Wrong End of a Promise

**VERIFIED:** Slot 10, **Mixon it up for a week (away), 129.24**, defeated slot 12, **Eggs Benedict (home), 96.19**. Margin **33.05**. The Week 1 participants do not possess knowledge of who will ultimately win the 2023 championship.

The last result arrived after the awning had been lowered.

It was read to people standing among folded canvas and loose supports: **Mixon it up for a week, 129.24; Eggs Benedict, 96.19**. For several moments there was no reaction. The two names had to compete with the problem of where to place a dozen heavy bundles of cloth until the wind passed.

A carpenter proposed cutting the cords. They could unwind the canvas quickly, he said, and use the rope again elsewhere. Another worker reminded him that every cord had been made for a particular damaged eyelet. If they cut it now, nobody would know its length or where it belonged.

"Then mark the ends," the carpenter said.

"With what?"

He held up the last public score sheet.

The herald refused to surrender it. The figures were needed for the season's record, and the herald was right to say so. A result could not be made less true because its paper was useful to somebody else. The carpenter found another scrap, the blank reverse of the discarded price board, and began writing small directions against each knot.

What had appeared to be a petty dispute became an hour of physical work. The bundles had to be separated, the cords measured and wound, the eyelets counted, and the six repaired points remembered. The man who had offered the too-short plank returned with a tool and drove small notches into the supporting wood. The woman who had tested the first rope held each loop while it was bound.

The supporters of the winning banner were present. So were people whose colors had lost. The story does not place particular historical owners among them. The work took all their hands without establishing anyone's true motives.

At the end, the awning lay safe against the wall. Its promise of shelter had not been fulfilled that day. But it could be lifted again, and whoever lifted it would know which cord belonged to which point. That was a smaller achievement than victory and, for the waiting travelers, a more useful one.

Somebody began to predict the season from its first six outcomes. Another listener asked him to say whether the next week's wind would turn. He admitted that he did not know.

The woman with the tools closed the box of wooden pins. "Good," she said. "Then there's something left for us to learn."

## After the Six — What Remains Under Cover

Jake returned when the last of the spectators were leaving. He had emptied the basin so often that his wrists were raw. The awning was gone from above the bench. Without it the street looked wider and the bench lonelier, an ordinary thing exposed to a weather it had never promised to withstand alone.

The woman was checking the cords against the marks in the carpenter's board. Jake saw his ribbon wound with the other binding. He could have found it immediately; one end still carried the faint crease left by his wrist.

"Does the roof go back up tomorrow?" he asked.

"When the wind lets us."

"And if it doesn't?"

"Then it stays down."

He had spent the day measuring himself against a number and the night watching people prepare for a question the numbers could not answer. He wanted to know whether keeping the ribbon had changed anything. The bench remained exposed. His banner had lost badly. Most of the people he had helped were already gone.

The woman placed the last pin in its box and offered him the handle.

"Take this inside," she said.

"Where?"

"Somewhere the wood will be dry."

He carried the box beyond the reach of the rain. It was a tiny and unceremonious act. No one attached a banner to it. The workers had six knots ready and no certainty about the next weather. The city's imagined shelter was not a sovereign claim. A recovered strip of ribbon did not repay a defeat. A victory on a public sheet was not a deed to the ground.

But the cord was measured, the pegs were sound, and the bench would be waiting when the cloth could rise again.

Before leaving, Jake put a hand against the bare support. He felt the shallow marks the carpenter had made for the six repairs. They were not beautiful. They were exact.

Somewhere else, other victories and defeats had already begun to produce their own stories. He had not witnessed those encounters, only their certified notices and the ordinary things people did afterward. He would be foolish to mistake his view from one bench for the whole Commonwealth.

Tomorrow he would learn whether the next question demanded more than six knots.

---

# 2023 — Week 2: The Weight of the Dry Place
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six certified matchups · original NONCANON prose*

**Source and time boundary:** `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, **2023 Week 2** (archival Flaim / ESPN 1417621, blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`). Scores, result, slot identity and home/away are archive evidence; home does not prove physical venue. The banners do not prove contemporary owners occupied the same 2023 slots. Jake is the only character with interiority, and even his slot-1 continuity is **proposed, user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. Other encounters are externally narrated civilian reactions, without assigning a 2026 owner to a historical team name. The awning, drying racks, workroom, tools, street, conversations and wind are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. The 2023 `Two Dreaded Snake` archival label is not a license to apply later three-headed morphology. El Niño is never a speaking humanoid. No characters know outcomes after Week 2.

---

The awning could be raised again, but not for everybody.

That was the first hard lesson of the following week. The workers had saved the fabric; they had not increased the length of the shelter. Three dry places could be made by lifting the strongest section. Five people were already sitting there when the cloth rose, and several more arrived before the knots were pulled tight.

The woman who kept the tools refused to draw a line between those who deserved the dry places and those who did not.

"Then somebody else will," a spectator said.

He was right.

## I. The Blew Waffle's and Two Dreaded Snake — A Second Use for the Cord

**VERIFIED:** Slot 6, **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 159.18**, defeated slot 7, **The Blew Waffle's (home), 116.68**. Margin **42.50**.

The first argument began over a piece of cord no longer than a forearm.

It lay beside the awning in a box of discarded fastenings. An apprentice had removed it because the knot had bitten so deeply that the strands could not be straightened. The fiber was still sound away from the knot. He wanted to cut it loose and carry it home for a hinge. The worker with the tools wanted it kept for smaller repairs.

The second week's first result arrived while they were pulling in opposite directions. The sheet put **159.18** beside Two Dreaded Snake and **116.68** beside The Blew Waffle's. Someone in the crowd remembered that Two Dreaded Snake had been beaten in the opening week and declared a resurrection. Someone else pointed out that its opponents this time were different. The word resurrection was taken back, but not the impulse behind it.

Two men in different colors began arguing about whether a beaten banner had learned a lesson or merely found a weaker rival. It was the sort of argument that could go on without any of its participants lifting a hand.

The apprentice, still holding the rope, asked the woman what a cord was worth after it had failed.

"It held somewhere once," she said. "Now we find the smaller place."

He cut away the crushed knot. The remaining length was too short to suspend canvas. It did fit around a loose fastening on the tool chest. He wrapped it twice, secured the lid, and lifted the chest to demonstrate.

The woman tested the lid. It held.

One of the spectators offered to buy the cord, newly useful, for the colors it might wear in a celebration. The apprentice shook his head. There was no glory in a fastening and no shame in using a thing for less than one had hoped.

The result on the sheet stood. It changed no physical ownership of the box and proved nothing about the private life of any historical human owner. In this proposed episode, it did arrive at the same moment that a worker discovered how not to throw away something merely because it could no longer perform its former task.

## II. Red Leopards and Benedict Arnold — The Counterweight

**VERIFIED:** Slot 2, **Red Leopards (home), 157.80**, defeated slot 8, **Benedict Arnold (away), 137.14**. Margin **20.66**.

A stone can make a rope easier to raise. It can also drag the whole arrangement down.

The repairers tried the second method first. They hung a smooth counterweight from the higher support, meaning to let the stone balance the thickest part of the awning. The canvas rose without the usual struggle. People cheered at the ingenuity; somebody offered to paint the stone with a red mark in honor of the week's newly announced Red Leopards victory over Benedict Arnold, **157.80 to 137.14**.

The person holding the rope did not answer. She was watching the other end of the line.

The weight had lifted the roof, but it had also pulled one support sideways. The wooden pin at the base crept out by the width of a fingernail. Then another. A man climbed onto the bench and pressed it back; his hand could not remain there forever.

Two spectators began to argue about whether the favorite should always retain advantage after an opening triumph. The carpenter called to them for a wedge. They did not hear him. Only when the pin struck stone did they turn.

He caught the support against his shoulder, sending a tremor through the fabric. A young worker pushed the wedge beneath the base. The counterweight stopped moving. For a time nobody knew whether they had saved the roof or only postponed its fall.

The woman with the tools took the weight down.

That provoked more protest than the danger had. The people under the dry section had enjoyed their hour of shelter. The stone was efficient, and efficiency looked like success to anyone who had not watched the pin leave the wood.

The carpenter took a chisel and cut a proper seat for the support. The work required lowering the cloth, making everyone wet again. Nobody thanked him while he worked. When the support was set in its new place and the stone could be raised without pulling the base, they lifted the canvas a second time.

It held.

The red mark was never painted. The scoreboard's certified outcome needed no symbolic confirmation. A sturdy roof did.

## III. Benedict Kenobi and Mixon it up for a week — How Much Help Is Enough

**VERIFIED:** Slot 10, **Mixon it up for a week (away), 127.86**, defeated slot 1, **Benedict Kenobi (home), 113.35**. Margin **14.51**. Jake's fictional response is not a statement of his real 2023 motives or ownership.

Jake had promised himself that the first result would not follow him into the second week.

He made the promise while carrying the carpenter's wedges to the workroom. The wedges were heavy in an unremarkable way. They did not demand strategy; they needed only a dry place and someone willing to carry them. That was the virtue of the work. It asked nothing of him that could be measured against another man's triumph.

But the next sheet found him there anyway.

**Mixon it up for a week: 127.86. Benedict Kenobi: 113.35.**

He stared at the margin, **14.51**, which was so much smaller than the previous defeat that he felt a brief, absurd gratitude. Then he became angry with himself for feeling grateful at all.

He set down the wedges. The workroom smelled of damp wood and metal worn smooth by use. Through the open door he could see the awning being raised, slowly this time, with the stone counterweight removed. A dozen people watched. Half of them would fit beneath the cloth; the rest would stand outside.

The woman who kept the tools came to the doorway.

"Will you carry the box back?" she asked.

"I'll do more than that."

"Carry it back first."

He took the box. At the awning he began to lay out pins and lengths of cord according to a pattern he had seen the carpenter use. He was trying to make himself useful before anyone could think to ask whether usefulness was merely another kind of competition.

The carpenter came down from the ladder, glanced at the arrangement, and moved three of the pins.

"Those are for the high side," he said.

"I thought they went here."

"Then ask."

He had spoken without cruelty. That made Jake's embarrassment harder to dismiss.

The carpenter showed him the difference between the pins. The high-side pegs had been worn into long, narrow shapes by years of being turned under pressure. The shorter ones locked the low brackets. If the two were confused, the cloth would rise crooked even though every peg seemed to fit.

Jake picked up the wrong pin and held it between two fingers. It was much easier, he realized, to offer all of one's strength than to admit one had not learned the name of a tool.

"Will you show me again?" he asked.

The carpenter did.

When the awning was raised, Jake had contributed no brilliant design. He had carried a box and placed the right pins where he was told. Several travelers came under the cloth and did not know him. That anonymity irritated him less than it had the week before.

After dark the second defeat would be written into the season's history with complete accuracy. The repair would be remembered, if at all, by the person whose parcel had stayed dry. Jake wanted both events to mean something. He did not yet know how to hold them together.

## IV. Arm Chair GM and El Niño — No Agreement with the Wind

**VERIFIED:** Slot 9, **El Niño (away), 188.92**, defeated slot 11, **Arm Chair GM (home), 138.84**. Margin **50.08**. The score does not certify any real meteorological event.

A second gust struck while the repair woman was knotting the high side.

The people who had taken dry seats began at once to discuss the meaning of the name El Niño. Some called it a warning. One man insisted that the weather was being controlled by somebody who knew more than he would say. Another, who had been watching the sky for hours, asked which of them intended to hold a conversation with a wind.

The competitive announcement added fuel to their certainty: **El Niño, 188.92; Arm Chair GM, 138.84**.

The worker with the tools tied off the knot and told them to move their bundles from the nearest post. The cloth could remain raised, but the post was under strain. No one obeyed immediately; the spectators had begun to divide into those who said the storm would grow worse and those who claimed it had already spent itself.

A cup fell from the bench. It struck the stones and broke, leaving a crescent of pale ceramic beneath the shelter.

A traveler reached down to gather the pieces. As he did, a length of loose canvas slapped against the post above his head. The repair woman caught his collar and drew him clear. The cup could wait.

She set a basin beyond the drip line and loosened one corner of the cloth, making a narrow opening through which the gust could pass. The awning no longer swelled into a sail. It fluttered in the gap, breathing out pressure instead of holding it.

The traveler collected the broken ceramic and put it in his bag. He had lost something small. The spectators had won nothing by predicting the wind.

No storm was granted a voice or a body; nobody here made a treaty with it. This was the older discipline of bending a fabric roof to withstand a force indifferent to who might have prevailed in the week's Contest.

## V. The LLC and Eggs Benedict — The Borrowed Hammer

**VERIFIED:** Slot 12, **Eggs Benedict (away), 151.94**, defeated slot 4, **The LLC (home), 110.15**. Margin **41.79**. Neither archived team is bound here to a 2026 human owner.

The hammer had been left at the repair bench without a name.

Its handle was scarred by old teeth marks, the kind left when a tool was gripped under one arm while both hands carried something heavier. The head was bright where the iron had struck pegs. Beside it lay a folded claim ticket, written in the sort of careful handwriting that usually accompanied an exchange of money.

The ticket promised payment for the hammer's use.

Nobody knew who had written it. The bench was a place where people left tools in haste and came back expecting to find them. One man wanted the ticket honored because an agreement was an agreement. Another pointed out that no one had agreed to it, and that paper without a maker was not enough to bind the hands of those who needed a tool.

The next competitive score arrived: Eggs Benedict **151.94**, The LLC **110.15**. Some onlookers connected the margin to the argument over payment. In the proposed account, this was an argument made by spectators, not evidence of a business transaction or an allegory of a real owner.

A carpenter came, asked for the hammer, and found the ticket wrapped around its handle.

"Who owns this?" he said.

Nobody answered.

He unfolded the paper, read it, and placed it beneath a stone so the wind would not carry it off. Then he used the hammer to drive three pegs into a warped crosspiece. He worked carefully and stopped when the wood began to split.

A traveler approached to ask whether he was paying for the hammer.

"Not yet," he said. "I haven't found anyone to pay."

The traveler pointed to the writing on the ticket. "It says you owe."

"It says someone wants to be paid. Those are not always the same sentence."

When he was finished, the carpenter cleaned the hammerhead, set the tool on the bench and placed the ticket beneath it. The rightful owner, if there was one, could take both. The disputed promise had not been destroyed; neither had it been accepted without question.

The awning held another hour.

## VI. Benedict Coomhersnatch and The KING of the Octagon Village — A Measure Kept

**VERIFIED:** Slot 5, **Benedict Coomhersnatch (home), 182.46**, defeated slot 3, **The KING of the Octagon Village (away), 132.00**. Margin **50.46**. Four distinct 2023 banners containing "Benedict" must not be merged into a single assumed owner.

At the end of the week someone brought a length of measuring tape to the workroom and asked that it be preserved.

It was old and had been stretched so often that its marks no longer agreed with the straight rule kept by the carpenter. A worker had used it to cut two cords. One had come out short, the other long. Neither mistake was dangerous on its own. Together they made a knot whose weight was carried by the wrong eyelet.

The final announcement put Benedict Coomhersnatch at **182.46** over The KING of the Octagon Village at **132.00**. The same KING banner that had won so decisively in the opening week had lost this one. A spectator tried to describe this as proof that every triumph contained its opposite. The carpenter asked him to hold the tape against a rule.

The discrepancy could be seen at once.

"Whose is it?" the spectator asked.

"Does it matter?"

"It matters if we're to blame somebody."

"It matters if we're to fix something."

The carpenter kept the shorter rule and discarded the tape's old marks. He cut a fresh strip from plain cloth and measured it twice against the sound rule. When he finished, he folded it at the midpoint and placed it in the tool chest.

No one could write a new measure by praising the last one. Nor could a public defeat erase a former victory already recorded. The repairers had to live among both truths, just as the competing banners now did.

The woman with the tools tested all six knots, pulling each in turn. One gave a little. She untied it and began again.

The work lasted beyond the last cheer of the evening. As people departed, a child who had watched both weeks asked why the woman would not leave the one slack knot alone.

She held out the rope for him to touch.

"Because someone who hasn't come yet will stand under it."

## After the Second Reckoning

The roof had become a place where people argued over rules nobody had written and over customs everyone had assumed. It had also become a place that remained dry longer than it had the week before.

Jake came to help lower the canvas for the night. The carpenter trusted him with the high pegs. He took them out in the proper order and set them in their marked places. No one praised him. The work was becoming familiar, which was a different kind of reward.

Beyond the bench, the wet streets shone in the late light. A woman with a bundle asked whether the awning would be open in the morning. Jake said the workers meant to try. She thanked him and went on, not asking which colors he wore.

He could remember **113.35** with more clarity than he wanted. He could also remember the feel of the correct peg in his hand. Neither memory made the other false.

"Will you be here tomorrow?" asked the repair woman.

He thought of his banner and the next public reckoning, of the arguments that would follow any result, of the many things he still had not repaired. A poor answer would be to promise everything.

"I can come in the morning," he said. "Tell me which task is mine."

She nodded.

The woman with the bundle had gone beyond the corner. The awning was lowered. The six knots were quiet in the dark, holding no load until they were asked to bear it again.

# 2023 — Week 3: The Road With No Owner
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six archived matchups · original NONCANON literary draft*

**Evidence and authority.** The six Week 3 results below come from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, archived Flaim / ESPN league 1417621 evidence (Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`). Scores, weekly pairings, historical team banners, and home/away competition fields are historical source assertions. **Home/away does not identify a physical venue.** Human-owner continuity, real actions, trades and motives are not authenticated by these rows. The passage from the first two weeks' invented awning to a river landing, public amphitheater, hearing room, market, observatory and library is **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not a settled map. Only the proposed `CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER` / team-slot-1 candidate receives interior viewpoint; the five other encounters have external chronicle narration, without entering another owner's thoughts. Historical owner mapping of slot 1 is user-attested, not provider-authenticated. El Niño remains a storm-name/banner without speech or humanoid form; Two Dreaded Snake is the exact 2023 archival banner, not an assignment of later visual morphology. Four separate 2023 Benedict team labels do not establish a common owner. No character knows a Week 4+ result. This independent weekly chronicle does not replace the separately preserved causal novel manuscript *The Fourth Address*.

The little city, whose real place on any approved map remained undecided, had a road drawn through it in red ink.

The road was not red. It was gray in the morning and white when the dust rose. There were wet places where a cart-wheel sank, and stretches of hard stone that injured horses. The red line appeared only on the public diagram: a single assured stroke between two squares, one marked LANDING, the other GATE.

For two weeks the citizens had gathered beneath a repaired awning to argue over whose hands might keep something standing. Now someone had drawn a route and asked who had the right to send people down it.

A child's charcoal mark crossed the red line halfway along.

Nobody had erased it.

## I. The KING of the Octagon Village and Two Dreaded Snake — A Chorus Without a Captain

**VERIFIED:** Slot 3, **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 164.48**, defeated slot 6, **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 109.34**. Margin **55.14**. These are competition banners, not evidence that either historical owner occupied the invented amphitheater.

At the stone amphitheater, six singers refused to begin until a seventh was allowed onto the platform.

They had been hired for the opening of the road, a pageant that would send the first ceremonial wagon from the landing to the gate. The seventh singer was a ferryman's daughter. Her father could take a boat downriver but could not afford the new toll proposed for wheeled traffic. The committee had offered her a place in the audience, not in the chorus.

"She knows the last verse," said the oldest singer.

"Then teach it to someone on the platform," replied the steward.

"The last verse belongs to her."

The steward pointed to the program. Its arrangement had already been copied a hundred times. Every name fit the column. Seven names would not.

The seventh singer climbed onto the first step and stayed there, neither performing nor yielding. The oldest singer began the melody alone. He reached the end of the second line and stopped. Another voice answered from behind him, then another, not with the intended words but with the sounds the ferrymen called across water when the current was hard to judge.

The ceremonial march became an improvised warning. It had rhythm enough to carry farther than the steward's orders.

A herald eventually announced the weekly verdict: **The KING of the Octagon Village 164.48**, **Two Dreaded Snake 109.34**. The KING banner's victory after the previous week's defeat drew a roar from the upper benches. In the proposed fiction, the singers' quarrel is neither caused by that score nor the act of either competitor. Their choice belongs to the unnamed townspeople.

The steward tried again to introduce the procession. Nobody heard his opening words. People had begun singing the warning-call, which was a poor anthem but an excellent instruction: wait for an answer before you cross.

At last the ferryman's daughter walked onto the platform. She took no one else's place. The six singers moved apart.

"That spoils the program," said the steward.

The oldest singer folded the printed sheet. "Then there is one thing the program has taught us."

"What's that?"

"Not everything worth hearing has been admitted to the column."

They sang the last verse properly. Some listeners knew its words and some did not. At the edge of the amphitheater, the steward sent an apprentice to ask whether the road committee had counted the people who would travel without wagons.

The apprentice was not told to bring an answer by sunset. He was told to bring a true one.

## II. Benedict Kenobi and Red Leopards — The Passenger Who Stayed

**VERIFIED:** Slot 2, **Red Leopards (away), 184.75**, defeated slot 1, **Benedict Kenobi (home), 183.46**. Margin **1.29**. This is the third consecutive defeat for the proposed historical slot-1 / Jake candidate, not proof of the real person's feelings or actions.

At the landing, Jake had been given a strip of blue chalk and asked to mark the stones above the waterline.

The task sounded small enough. Mark a place for the carts to stop; mark another where pedestrians could wait; mark the point beyond which the ferryman would not accept a horse. No one had told him what to do when the landing filled with people who did not fit neatly into those divisions.

He watched a woman lead her exhausted father down the incline. The old man carried a box against his ribs. When he stopped to rest, the box gave a faint glass rattle. Others passed on either side, careful not to touch him. Jake knew that the ferry had room for one more passenger if the wheelbarrow was left behind.

A merchant argued that the wheelbarrow held medicines. The father said the box contained the lenses with which he had made spectacles for thirty years. Neither cargo could be weighed by its owner without becoming an argument about whose future mattered.

Jake stared at the chalk in his fingers. He wanted a principle large enough to spare him the decision. A marked stone, a queue, a rule printed in the square—anything that could do the choosing in his place.

The weekly results were carried down to the shore as the ferryman began boarding. **Red Leopards 184.75**. **Benedict Kenobi 183.46**. The loss was only **1.29**, close enough that some spectators described it as a victory missed by a breath. Jake could feel the old reflex return: examine the smallest failure, find the fraction that might have been different, and rehearse it until the next contest arrived. That interior response is proposed literary invention, not authenticated owner testimony.

The merchant appealed to the ferryman. The ferryman appealed to the chalk marks. Both looked to Jake.

He turned the blue chalk over in his palm. The rules would let the barrow board first; it had arrived sooner. The rules said nothing about the old man's legs, which trembled under him, or the way he had slipped one arm around his box to keep its glass from knocking against the stone.

"How many can you take if we carry the barrow empty?" Jake asked.

The ferryman measured the space with his eyes. "Two more, if no one moves."

"And the medicine?"

"On the next crossing. The river won't shut itself for the night."

Jake looked at the merchant. "Does any of it spoil in an hour?"

"Not the medicine," the merchant said. "My arrangements."

Jake asked for two volunteers to carry the wheelbarrow back up the slope. The merchant protested and then, seeing that the people behind him were listening, offered to help. The father boarded with the box. His daughter stepped aboard after him. No law had been overturned. A rule had been read alongside the people it was meant to serve.

When the boat pushed free, Jake's chalk had snapped into two pieces. He kept both. The margin of the contest did not widen because a passenger crossed safely. The third defeat remained a third defeat.

He was relieved that the old man had not asked which banner he served. He was less pleased to discover how badly he had wanted him to ask.

## III. Benedict Arnold and The LLC — Nine Measures of Silence

**VERIFIED:** Slot 8, **Benedict Arnold (home), 165.56**, defeated slot 4, **The LLC (away), 156.46**. Margin **9.10**. The hearing, performers and decisions are invented civilian events and cannot be attributed to the historical team owners.

A hearing about noise ought to have been simple. The people of the upper street said the bell struck too early. The people near the market said it struck too late. Both accused the bellkeeper of changing the hour to favor the other.

The bellkeeper arrived with no defense except the bell's clapper.

He set the iron tongue on the hearing-room table. Its surface was blackened, its broad end worn bright by contact with the metal around it. He asked every accuser to hold it in turn. Some refused. Those who did hold it found it heavier than they had imagined.

"I cannot strike the bell without this," he told the hearing. "And I cannot strike it for both streets at the same moment."

A violinist from the upper street said the bell woke her younger brother, who worked through the night. A fishseller from the market said the bell was their only signal to bring in the river boats before the tide changed. The two had been shouting at each other in the corridor. Inside the room, they stood close enough to notice the wear on one another's hands.

A runner crossed the threshold with the new result: **Benedict Arnold 165.56**, **The LLC 156.46**. The difference, **9.10**, briefly gave the listeners another subject to argue over. One onlooker declared that a narrow victory showed the value of decisive leadership. Another said that a narrow defeat exposed the danger of putting any clock in one person's care. Their interpretations were not evidence of what either competitor intended.

The magistrate asked what the bellkeeper proposed.

"Nothing," he said. "I was told to ring at dawn. If dawn has two meanings, I have two orders."

The violinist surprised everyone by requesting a later first bell, not to silence the fishsellers but to let her brother sleep. The fishseller replied that he would send two boys along the quay with lanterns before the tide changed. The magistrate offered to pay the boys from the public coffers, and the fishseller shook his head.

"They should be paid," he said. "But not as a favor. Put it in the rule."

The hearing did not agree on the new hour. The magistrate called a recess and left the clapper on the table as evidence. When the room emptied, the violinist and the fishseller were still arguing, but their voices had grown quieter. They were no longer speaking about who owned the bell. They were asking how far a sound needed to travel.

The bellkeeper lifted the clapper to carry it home and discovered that the violinist had wrapped a square of cloth around its sharpest edge so it would not cut his hand.

There had been no verdict about the hour. There was, however, a better question than the one with which the hearing had begun.

## IV. Mixon it up for a week and The Blew Waffle's — The Feast That Refused a Table

**VERIFIED:** Slot 7, **The Blew Waffle's (away), 170.38**, defeated slot 10, **Mixon it up for a week (home), 121.52**. Margin **48.86**. Historical spelling and spacing are retained from the archive; the food market below is fictional and does not imply an owner-operated business or real transaction.

There was going to be a feast.

This was the first fact on which the kitchen committee agreed. They could not agree about who was invited, who should pay, which dishes counted as traditional, or whether a feast held beside the unfinished road could be called a road-opening feast at all.

The cook who had proposed it brought a pot of onions to the market and set it over a brazier. People who could not attend the committee meeting came to see whether the smell meant the quarrel was finished.

It did not. The argument emerged from the market hall in stages, growing louder each time someone opened the door.

"Seats for the sponsors," said a committee member.

"Seats for anyone hungry," said the cook.

"Then who provides the food?"

"Someone who intends to feed people."

The cook was not wealthy. Her sleeves were flour-marked and she had been buying bruised vegetables at closing time. She knew the limitations of her plan better than the committee did. What troubled her was the new diagram on the wall: a long table divided into named boxes, with a section of benches beyond the last box labeled GENERAL.

An apprentice baker arrived bearing the weekly announcement: **The Blew Waffle's 170.38** over **Mixon it up for a week 121.52**. Some in the market laughed at the food-adjacent banner appearing just as the committee's own feast fell apart. The coincidence became a joke, not an asserted causal link to an actual competitor or commercial deal.

The committee offered the cook the first table, entirely to herself, if she would let the sponsors choose their guests.

She took the diagram down, rolled it tightly and passed it to the baker.

"Here," she said. "Light the fire with it."

The baker stared at her. "It has their names."

"Then they will be warm before the rest of us."

This scandalized the committee sufficiently that several members stopped speaking. The cook set more onions in the pot. She told the baker to keep the paper, unburned, until the sponsors had a chance to revise it. Then she dragged the first table out into the market and set it across the path so that everyone, arriving from either direction, would have to decide whether to help move it.

Three people did. One was a sponsor. One was a porter. One was a woman carrying a child and a cracked bowl. Together they turned the table sideways, leaving room for two lines to meet.

By evening there were no boxes on the new seating plan. There were still arguments about who would pay, how many pots they could fill and whether a feast without music was an insult. Those arguments could be answered, imperfectly, with work. The earlier diagram had turned hunger into a privilege before anyone had eaten.

The cook tasted the onions, found them too sweet and added salt.

"Now," she said, "we have a problem worth discussing."

## V. Benedict Coomhersnatch and El Niño — Instruments for an Uncertain Sky

**VERIFIED:** Slot 5, **Benedict Coomhersnatch (home), 211.24**, defeated slot 9, **El Niño (away), 101.08**. Margin **110.16**. The large score difference is a certified competition result; it is not proof of a meteorological event, physical conquest of a storm, or identity-sharing among the Benedict banners.

From the observatory's western window, the public road appeared as a line interrupted by roofs.

An old observer had been ordered to paint a favorable forecast on a board outside the tower. The road opening needed fair weather, the committee explained. A doubtful forecast would keep the guests away. The observer did not object to guests. She objected to being told what the wind would do before the instruments had been read.

Her assistant carried the barometer from one end of the table to the other, looking for a place where the needle would climb. He had learned to perform that useless ritual under a previous employer, who could bear bad weather more easily than a disappointing number.

The observer took the instrument away from him.

"You are not measuring the sky," she said. "You are measuring the table."

Outside, a flock crossed the tower's shadow. The observer watched their formation until they passed beyond the roofs. The birds, unlike the guests, had made no demand for certainty.

The day's competitive result soon arrived: **Benedict Coomhersnatch 211.24**, **El Niño 101.08**. The difference, **110.16**, was the greatest of this week's six listed contests. Some spectators celebrated the victor as though a storm itself had been subdued. In this account El Niño is a historical team banner and weather-associated imaginative symbol, not a speaking creature and not a claim that any storm actually crossed the town that day.

The observer heard the celebration and asked the assistant what it meant to defeat a prediction.

"To be right after someone else was wrong," he said.

"And what if everyone is wrong?"

He did not answer.

She opened the tower door to let the apprentices enter. One had been studying cloud shapes. Another had watched the river. A third had counted birds settling low in the reeds. Their reports contradicted one another. She wrote all three on the slate.

The assistant pointed out that the committee wanted one answer.

"It will have one," she said, "when there is one to give."

At dusk she sent the board down to the gate with a message: there could be clear hours, there could be rain, and travelers should make preparations for both. The assistant protested that such words would offend the committee.

The observer put on her coat.

"If they want obedience, they can hire a herald. If they want warning, they must allow the warning to be uncertain."

The road opening went ahead as planned in the proposed story. Nobody could say afterwards that the sky had promised them anything. The observer considered this a modest triumph, though no weekly verdict carried her name.

## VI. Eggs Benedict and Arm Chair GM — The Chair Beside the Door

**VERIFIED:** Slot 12, **Eggs Benedict (home), 150.96**, defeated slot 11, **Arm Chair GM (away), 140.66**. Margin **10.30**. Neither banner authenticates a real owner at the fictional public library or fixes a historical site on the Atlas.

The reading room possessed thirteen chairs and a table built for twelve.

For years the thirteenth chair had stood beside the entrance, reserved for a custodian who never sat. On the morning of the road ceremony a traveler moved it to the table so he could read the public diagram. The custodian moved it back. The traveler moved it forward again. By noon this had become a dispute the librarians could not contain.

A woman carrying a parcel of books asked for the reason.

"The room was designed for twelve," the custodian replied.

"The room has thirteen chairs," she said.

"One does not belong at the table."

The woman placed the books down and drew up the contested chair. It was a poor fit. The arms struck the table edge, and she had to sit sideways to reach the paper. Her parcel was bound with a ribbon, not a cord, and contained accounts of people who had followed roads that were no longer on any official map.

She turned the first book to a drawing of a vanished crossing. In the margin someone had written, years before: WE WERE HERE BEFORE THE BRIDGE.

The custodian frowned. "That annotation damages the record."

"It adds to it," said the woman.

A messenger interrupted with the competitive score: **Eggs Benedict 150.96**, **Arm Chair GM 140.66**. A spectator joked that the losing banner ought to appreciate the merits of a well-placed seat. Nobody mistook the joke for source evidence. The week's real outcome settled the public contest, not the dispute about the library's furniture.

The librarian examined the drawing. The note was written in a different hand from the rest of the book. She could not certify the crossing had existed exactly as shown. She could certify that someone had wanted a later reader to know that a place had been lost.

"Would you cut the note out?" the woman asked.

"No," said the librarian. "I would mark who wrote it, if I could learn."

"And if you cannot?"

"Then I would leave enough room for the next person to ask."

They shifted the table six inches. The thirteenth chair fitted. No one proclaimed a reform; the room was still too small for everyone waiting outside. The custodian brought in a narrow bench from the corridor and discovered that the two chairs nearest the window could be moved without blocking the door.

When the woman left, she took her parcel but left a copy of the road diagram on the table. A child sat in the thirteenth chair and drew a charcoal line across the red route, at the place where the river actually turned.

The librarian did not rub it away. She wrote a date beside it and left a blank space for the child's name.

---

## After the Third Reckoning — What Cannot Be Drawn

The ceremonial wagon reached the gate after sunset. It had stopped twice: once for the ferry, once for a crowd outside the market that insisted on feeding the drivers before anyone made a speech.

At the awning, Jake found the apprentice who had been charged with taking messages between the six civic places. The apprentice had accumulated scraps of paper, each with an order that contradicted another. None of them showed the real shape of the road.

Jake's own banner had lost for the third time, **183.46 to 184.75**. He could not make that result less true by pointing to the people who had crossed the landing. Nor could a margin of **1.29** tell him whether he had done well by them. He wondered how many questions he had been trying to answer with a single number.

The apprentice handed him the original red-line diagram. At its center was a child's mark.

"Shall I make a clean copy?" the apprentice asked.

Jake studied the crossing, then the blank spaces that stood for the neighborhoods beyond the gate. He thought of the ferryman, of the woman and her father, and of the labor that would be needed when a traveler arrived whose name had never appeared on a committee program.

"Not clean," he said. "Accurate."

The apprentice turned the paper over. The back was empty, and there was enough room to begin again.

**Historical handoff:** Week 3 records six complete, non-overlapping archival matchups and twelve distinct slots. Winners: slot 3 KING, slot 2 Red Leopards, slot 8 Benedict Arnold, slot 7 The Blew Waffle's, slot 5 Benedict Coomhersnatch, slot 12 Eggs Benedict. Proposed slot-1 Jake/Benedict Kenobi is **0–3**, with Week 3 loss by **1.29**; these are slot-level results, not authenticated owner biography. No Week 4+ result is presumed. All civic events, characters, geography, inferred weather and dialogue are unapproved fiction. *The Fourth Address* remains a separate, unmodified NONCANON causal novel candidate.
