# 2023 — Week 4: The Price of the Other Bank
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six archived matchups · NONCANON literary draft*

**Source and viewpoint boundary.** The six results and their sequence are transcribed from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`, labeled Flaim / ESPN league 1417621. Historical human-owner identities remain unverified. The proposed Jake / slot-1 association is **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. **Home/away competition fields do not identify physical venues.** All tolls, roads, laws, civic officers, buildings, damage, journeys and dialogue are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`) alone carries limited interior viewpoint; the remaining actors are witnessed externally, never granted a new principal POV. Four distinct 2023 Benedict banners must not be mistaken for four identities belonging to one person. Two Dreaded Snake is the exact historical banner, without an imposed later appearance. El Niño is a storm-name/banner, **not a speaking or human-shaped being**. No Week 5+ outcome enters the characters' knowledge. The six weekly encounters constitute a historical companion, **not six mandatory novel chapters**; the separate noncanonical causal manuscript *The Fourth Address* remains unchanged.

The men came for the ferry before sunrise, carrying no tools that could mend it.

There were three of them, with clean hands and a long leather case. They drove a black post into the bank above the landing, where the blue chalk from the previous week could still be seen. On the post they nailed a small sheet of copper. In the gray light Jake could read only the largest words: **RIGHT OF PASSAGE — TEMPORARY ASSIGNMENT**.

The ferryman watched from his boat. His daughter stood beside the rope that kept the hull from drifting. Neither stepped forward until the youngest stranger took a folding rule from the leather case and began measuring the distance between the boat and the new post.

“You're measuring the wrong thing,” she said.

He looked up. “What should I be measuring?”

“How long a person can stand there before the people on the other side stop waiting.”

The men did not reply. They measured the stones, registered the rope, and left an iron ring at the foot of the post. By noon a chain could be passed through it. By evening the law would decide who held the key.

A week earlier the city had drawn a road through itself. Now it had found a way to sell the part that crossed water.

## I. Red Leopards and Benedict Coomhersnatch — The Lease of Ninety Days

**VERIFIED:** Slot 2, **Red Leopards (home), 166.78**, defeated slot 5, **Benedict Coomhersnatch (away), 152.06**. Margin **14.72**. This archival result does not authenticate the lease, the city, or the real-world identity or conduct of either owner.

Jake came to the weighing house with a bill for six lengths of cord. He had hoped to find the road steward there, but the queue was all strangers and nobody was weighing grain. They were weighing signatures. Two clerks at a broad table examined each petition and laid a stone beside its name: white for a supporter of the new passage lease, black for an objector. A man in a dark green coat stood at the window, reading the color of the stones from a distance.

The man introduced himself as Varn. He was not a magistrate. He held the city's unpaid repair notes, and the repairs had continued because he had lent the workers the cost of their iron and timber. Without his money, he explained, the road would remain a road only on paper.

The lease would last ninety days. During that time his firm could charge for carts, beasts, and freight crossing the public landing. Pedestrians without cargo would pass without payment. The terms sounded merciful until the ferryman's daughter asked whether a person carrying medicine counted as freight.

Varn asked what medicine weighed.

“Enough for someone to carry,” she answered.

A clerk moved a white stone toward a blank name. “We can exempt that particular kind.”

“And if it is bread?”

“Bread is merchandise.”

“And if somebody is hungry?”

The clerk put the stone down. He was young enough to be ashamed of the silence, though his shame did not change the form.

A cart outside the weighing house struck a rut. The axle broke, and a sheet of timber fell across the only route to the pump. Men shouted for help; the person driving the cart tried to lift the load before anyone could drag her free. Varn went out with the others. He did not stand aside like a villain from a street play. He got his green coat beneath a plank and lifted until the broken axle cleared the woman's leg. Jake saw the strain in his jaw and understood why the man could persuade people that no one else would do necessary work.

Afterward Varn washed his hands in a bucket. “The timber has to be paid for,” he told Jake, softly enough that only those beside the bucket heard. “That isn't a philosophy. It's what timber costs.”

A bell sounded from the upper square. The weekly board had been read: **Red Leopards 166.78; Benedict Coomhersnatch 152.06**. Several petitioners applauded the Red Leopards banner's victory. The tally did not decide the lease; it hung beside the day's business as a separate public reckoning. Even so, Jake noticed how quickly the white-stone supporters took the applause for encouragement.

Varn laid his washed hand on the lease. “Ninety days,” he said. “Then the city may buy back what it cannot repair.”

Jake searched the sheet for the price of buying back the landing. There was no price. There was a line where a price might later be written, and a smaller clause giving Varn the right to calculate its costs.

He left the bill for cord unsubmitted. At the door the injured driver was asking who would pay for the wheel.

No clerk had a stone for her question.

## II. Benedict Kenobi and The Blew Waffle's — Witness to a Closed Gate

**VERIFIED:** Slot 1, **Benedict Kenobi (home), 103.24**, lost to slot 7, **The Blew Waffle's (away), 160.64**. Margin **57.40**. The slot-1 fourth consecutive defeat supports a literary Jake continuity candidate only; it is not evidence of the actual owner's feelings.

The chain arrived before the vote was finished.

It was not especially heavy. Two men could carry it, and its links were small enough that a child might have lifted one end. Yet when they passed it through the ring on the bank, the landing changed. What had been a place where any person could wait became an entrance owned by a question: what are you carrying?

Jake was asked to witness the installation. A clerk brought a page, and the page listed the chain, the ring, the post, and the number of steps between high water and the nearest wall. He read it twice. None of the measurements was false.

He remembered the old spectacle-maker from the third week, the box of lenses rattling against his ribs, the weary father trying to board beside a medicine cart. The man's daughter had watched him from the upper stones and trusted the world to make room for one more person. Jake had drawn a blue line to show where they might stand. That line lay under the chain now.

“It's a receipt,” the clerk said. “Not a vote.”

Jake pointed to the final sentence. **Received on behalf of all persons served by the passage.**

“All?” he asked.

“Customary wording.”

“I haven't spoken to all of them.”

“You witnessed the ring. You weren't appointed to take their testimony.”

The weekly contest sheet passed along the queue. **The Blew Waffle's 160.64; Benedict Kenobi 103.24.** There was no narrow consolation in the difference this time. For the fourth straight week the slot-1 banner had lost; this result was **57.40** points apart. The figures did not command Jake to sign, but their appearance pressed against an already raw desire to prove himself useful.

The clerk held out a quill. “If you refuse, another witness can sign. The gate will close either way.”

Jake had believed until that moment that refusing a document could stop the act it described. The people behind him knew otherwise. A woman with two pails tried to pass, received a yellow token because the pails were empty, and was told that returning with them full might require a charge. She took the token as carefully as if it were made of glass.

Jake changed the last sentence. He drew a firm line through **all persons served** and wrote, in the narrow space remaining: **I witnessed the chain placed here. I did not witness agreement to it.**

The clerk stared at the alteration. It did not invalidate the lease. It made the receipt imperfect, which meant it would have to be copied and lodged separately. The clerk's pen hovered, then scratched a mark beside Jake's. No officer stopped the workmen. The chain remained.

The ferryman's daughter stood on the other side. She had one hand on the mooring rope and the other around a folded paper. She watched Jake leave the altered receipt at the table. He wanted her to understand what he had refused to say on behalf of everyone.

She lifted her paper. It was a list of passengers who had gone away rather than pay.

She did not smile at his careful wording.

## III. Two Dreaded Snake and Mixon it up for a week — Bread at the Chain

**VERIFIED:** Slot 6, **Two Dreaded Snake (home), 97.08**, lost to slot 10, **Mixon it up for a week (away), 173.50**. Margin **76.42**. These are historical competition banners, not a claim that an actual owner traded food or controlled the fictional granary.

By the third day the chain had learned what everyone ate.

Jake followed a pair of porters from the landing to the kitchen market. Their cart held sacks of flour, but their permit had been stamped for building supplies. The clerk at the chain declared the load wrongly classified and held it for inspection. The porters were told they might wait until evening; if it rained, they were told, the sacks could be covered at their own expense.

The cooks at the market sent a boy with a loaf and an empty pot. No one sent money. He sat on the cart-wheel, broke the loaf into pieces, and passed them around to the waiting drivers. What angered them was not hunger alone. It was that Varn's concession could make a cart stand for a whole day while a family on the far bank waited for the flour that had already been milled.

A cook named Sena took Jake inside the market. On the wall hung a board with two columns: food that would spoil if held, and food that might last the winter. She had begun moving sacks between columns without moving them from the room. Men who used to barter with her now demanded paper tokens. The tokens could be purchased in advance from the same office that held the chain.

“You can open the gate for the porters,” Jake said.

Sena shook her head. “I can open my door. They're different doors.”

Then she did something he could not mistake for a lesson in generosity. She sold the kitchen's stored oil to a broker who served Varn's road crew. With the payment she bought passage for the flour. It was a sensible bargain. It kept today's children fed. It also left the market without enough oil to cook tomorrow's meals, and the broker had purchased the oil for less than she had paid for it.

Jake asked whether the broker could be persuaded to return some.

“He would,” Sena said. “For more than he gave me.”

There was a quarrel at the far table over the week's posted result: **Mixon it up for a week 173.50; Two Dreaded Snake 97.08**. Someone had lost a small wager, and someone else insisted that the large margin proved a general truth about risk. Sena came out carrying the flour permit and snapped at them to move their boots from the food.

The porters were allowed through just as the market's ovens cooled. The kitchen sent one pot across the river with the sacks, a useful thing that could carry soup but not the oil needed to make it. The boy who had shared the loaf asked who would bring the pot home. No one had decided.

That evening Jake saw the ledger again. His amended receipt had been copied. The copied version preserved his objection in tiny letters, but it occupied the margin, while the printed sentence about universal receipt remained in the center.

The chain was not merely blocking bread. It was turning yesterday's exceptions into tomorrow's prices.

## IV. The LLC and Arm Chair GM — The Word Under the Seal

**VERIFIED:** Slot 4, **The LLC (home), 170.62**, lost to slot 11, **Arm Chair GM (away), 177.22**. Margin **6.60**. The narrow archival result does not certify any legal verdict or connect the team owners to the invented arbitration.

The hearing convened in a room meant for tax appeals, not for the river. Varn brought two witnesses, the clerk who had copied Jake's receipt, and a carpenter with the right to describe the new chain. The ferryman came alone. He had removed his cap and held it in both hands as if the cap were all the evidence he owned.

Jake waited beside the door. He had been summoned because of the line he had crossed out.

At the table sat three arbitrators. Behind them was a sealed copy of the charter, hung high enough to be visible and far enough to be unreadable. The chair in which an absent fourth arbitrator might have sat remained empty. No one asked who could fill it.

Varn's advocate read the term **temporary assignment**. He spoke of insolvency, public works, and orderly passage. For two sentences Jake almost forgot the chain itself. Then the ferryman asked whether his boat remained his boat if another man decided which passengers might board.

“It remains your property,” said the advocate. “The access is licensed.”

“And the rope?”

“The rope may be yours.”

“Then when I pull my rope, am I pulling my own boat or your gate?”

One arbitrator told him not to confuse property with permission. The ferryman looked down at the cap. He had not confused them. The charter had.

Jake was called forward to examine the receipt. Its ink had changed on the copy. The crossing-out survived, but the clause that had been struck was still legible beneath a fine line. His own replacement statement appeared at the bottom, almost touching the seal. The clerk swore that nothing material had been omitted.

“Then read the bottom,” Jake said.

The clerk read it. Slowly, because the room was silent, he pronounced every word: **I did not witness agreement to it.**

Varn did not interrupt. That made Jake more uneasy than anger would have. The man had written a concession that could survive an objection about the receipt. The signature mattered, but not in the way Jake had hoped.

A bell outside carried the fourth contest result: **Arm Chair GM 177.22; The LLC 170.62**, a **6.60** margin. The arbitrators waited for the bell to stop, then continued with the lease. One turned the suspended charter around so its back faced the room. A clause beneath the seal gave the concession-holder the right to suspend passage when a dispute threatened collection.

The hearing ended without a ruling on that clause. The arbitrators adjourned until they could inspect the original. Varn asked for the chain to remain while they considered it. Two arbitrators agreed. The third recorded dissent, but did not prevent the order.

The ferryman put on his cap outside.

“Did we win anything?” he asked.

Jake could see the chain through the window. There was nothing honest he could call a victory. He gave the ferryman the one page the clerks had permitted him to keep: the words of his objection, certified now as part of the hearing.

The ferryman folded it until only the word **did not** remained visible.

## V. The KING of the Octagon Village and El Niño — Seats Taken for Stone

**VERIFIED:** Slot 3, **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 90.92**, lost to slot 9, **El Niño (away), 189.06**. Margin **98.14**. El Niño is the archival name of a competing banner and the established elemental storm identity, not an actual humanoid participant at the invented demolition.

The first stone fell at the amphitheater while the audience was still filing in.

It was a deliberate fall. The city had authorized the removal of the outer steps to provide material for the new gatehouse. The foreman set ropes around a slab, pulled them taut, and told everyone below to stand back. What he had not calculated was the way the sound would travel. When the slab struck the ground, people in the narrow lane beyond the arena ran as if an attack had begun.

Jake reached the outer wall among them. Dust filled the air. The older singer who had defended the ferryman's daughter during the road ceremony stood on the remaining steps, holding the printed program from that day. It fluttered in his fist while the workmen unfastened the next slab.

The foreman said the steps could be rebuilt after the gatehouse paid for itself. The singer asked where people were supposed to gather in the meantime. Varn had not come, but a young accountant in the firm's green livery explained that the site was not part of the original lease.

“Then why take its stones?” Jake asked.

“Because the stones belong to the city.”

“And the city cannot keep them here?”

“The city has pledged them as security.”

The answer changed the meaning of the work. The men were not stealing. A city had promised part of itself away and left its laborers to collect it.

A messenger came with the public scoring roll. **El Niño 189.06; The KING of the Octagon Village 90.92**. The unequal result drew a shout that died quickly in the dust. The name El Niño appeared on the roll as a banner, not a speaking visitor or a figure advancing through the ruin. Jake watched the arithmetic settle into the ordinary noise of a day when stone, not a contest score, was deciding how people could live.

The foreman was under orders to finish before dark. He had already removed two slabs when the older singer climbed the remaining steps and began the warning-call from the week before. The workers paused, not because the song had legal force, but because one worker's brother was singing it from the road.

The foreman went up to speak with him. Jake followed. From the last intact tier he could see the road they had celebrated, the weigh-house roof, the landing and the small black post on the bank. The road was full of people carrying things back the way they had come.

“You'll break the edge if you take the next one,” Jake told the foreman. He had seen the hairline crack. It ran under the slab toward the inner wall.

The foreman tested it with his heel and cursed. He ordered a brace, then a second. The stone remained in place, but the slab already removed could not be returned before dusk. They left the staircase cut short, a drop at the end of a path that had once led all the way to the seats.

No proclamation was made. An evening gathering found there was nowhere safe to sit.

The accountant came back with an invoice for additional timber used in the bracing. It would be charged to the road, and the road's unpaid cost would increase.

Jake read the sum. He had learned something about a price that kept growing whenever anyone tried to keep a thing from falling.

## VI. Eggs Benedict and Benedict Arnold — The Second Copy

**VERIFIED:** Slot 12, **Eggs Benedict (home), 130.50**, defeated slot 8, **Benedict Arnold (away), 106.98**. Margin **23.52**. The two Benedict labels designate distinct 2023 competition slots; they do not establish a shared historical owner or family.

By the end of the week two versions of the charter had been placed in the library.

One was sealed and public. The other had no seal, but had been carried in by the apprentice who once ran messages between the market and the landing. He put it on the long table beside the thirteenth chair. The child who had marked the error on the red road diagram recognized the same table and moved aside to make room.

Jake had followed the apprentice through the open door. The second copy was older. Its edges were worn, and a corner had been patched with thin linen. Near the bottom a phrase had been written in a different hand: **Except the customary passage of persons and common necessities.**

The sealed charter had no such phrase.

The librarian did not declare either document authentic. She placed them beneath the window and asked the apprentice who had given him the old page. He answered that he had found it in a box of damaged account books that the council had sent away for disposal. He could identify the box, not the hand that had written the phrase.

Jake wanted it to be enough. A sentence about common necessities, if accepted, might undo the wrong he had watched at the chain. But a thing could be desperately useful and still be unproven. He had spent the week seeing people turn the usefulness of a document into proof that it belonged to them.

The evening board was brought in as the lamps were trimmed: **Eggs Benedict 130.50; Benedict Arnold 106.98**. The librarian entered the result among the week's other archival contests, separately from the disputed charter. Jake appreciated the separation. It made neither record less important. It stopped one from pretending to certify the other.

Varn came to inspect the papers. He had not hidden from the public, and he did not hide now. He bent over the unsealed copy, studied the handwriting, and said that the clause might have been a draft never adopted.

“Or it might have been removed,” the apprentice replied.

“Yes,” Varn said. “It might.”

The librarian asked what Varn wanted done.

“Keep both,” he answered. “But do not put an unproved sentence on the gate. People will cross without paying, and the repairs will stop. If the foundation goes, you'll blame me for that as readily as you blame me for the chain.”

Jake had watched him help lift the axle from the injured driver. He had watched the chain hold back flour and the amphitheater lose its steps. He knew that Varn was telling part of the truth. That was what made the next decision hard. A lie could be refused cleanly; a partial truth could collect the money that kept a ferry afloat.

The librarian offered to lodge the disputed copy in a closed drawer until the arbitrators ruled. The apprentice objected. “Then only the men who can afford a hearing will know there was another page.”

Jake took the old red road diagram from the table. Its blank reverse had held the child's first rough amendment. He placed it beside both charters and asked for three sheets of paper. He copied the sealed clause on one, the disputed clause on another, and on the third wrote only what the library could actually attest: **Two texts are preserved. Their relationship is unresolved. Public inspection is permitted.**

He signed the notice with his name, not as a representative of the road or its people, but as the one who had written it.

Varn told him that making uncertainty public would raise the city's borrowing cost. He might have been right. The librarian hung the notice anyway. People began to gather beneath it: porters, accountants, a farmer who had walked over the old road before it was mapped. They argued, and some argued badly. No decision came quickly enough to stop the evening toll.

The ferryman's daughter arrived with her list of passengers who had turned back. She did not look at Jake's signature. She laid the list under the disputed clause so that anyone examining the law would also have to examine the people it had already failed.

---

## After the Fourth Reckoning — A Road Still Open on Paper

There was no triumph at the landing that night. The chain was locked. The ferry lay on the far side, close enough for Jake to see the mooring rope and too far away for him to cross. Two families had bedded down on the near bank; another had started back toward the market with a cart of unsold vegetables. The amphitheater stood dark, with its outer steps cut away. The kitchen's ovens had no oil.

The fourth weekly result for the proposed Jake / slot-1 banner was certain: **Benedict Kenobi 103.24; The Blew Waffle's 160.64**. Four losses in four weeks. Nothing in that record established what the fictional man ought to do with the chain, and no description of his objection could reverse the contest.

He carried a copy of the library notice to the black post. He did not have a key, and he did not break the lock. He bound the notice above the copper plate with the last length of cord from his unsubmitted bill, where a traveler waiting to pay could read it before making a decision. Then he went back up the road to tell Sena that the porters might need food on the near bank tomorrow.

He had not freed the landing. Varn still held the concession. The city's debt remained. The partial truth on the gate might make matters worse before it made them better. But the notice changed what the people waiting could say when the clerk told them the law was settled.

A little past midnight the ferryman's daughter crossed in a small skiff with no passengers. She brought back neither freight nor a victory. She carried a bucket of fresh water to the two families under the wall, then returned before the toll-men noticed.

Jake watched the faint wake disappear. He had written two incompatible versions of a law into public view. She had crossed the river.

The city would have to decide which of those acts it could afford to call necessary.

**Historical handoff.** Archive Week 4 contains six complete matchups covering twelve distinct slots, with winners slot 2 Red Leopards, slot 7 The Blew Waffle's, slot 10 Mixon it up for a week, slot 11 Arm Chair GM, slot 9 El Niño, and slot 12 Eggs Benedict. Proposed historical Jake/slot-1 is **0–4**; Week 4 defeat margin **57.40**. All political, legal, culinary, architectural, transport, interpersonal and environmental events above are **unapproved literary invention**. Neither the physical setting nor any historical owner/banner binding is canonized. No future result is asserted.

# 2023 — Week 5: The Boat Without a Name
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six evidence-joined encounters · substantial NONCANON historical fiction*

**Evidence and authority.** Archival result assertions are from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json` (Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`), labeled Flaim / ESPN league 1417621. The original ESPN provider SHA and historical human-owner joins remain unavailable; the Jake/slot-1 link is **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. Home/away competition fields do not identify physical venues. Literary settings and actions are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not Atlas-certified geography or actual conduct of any owner. Only Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`) carries limited interior viewpoint; all other people are observed externally. Four separate 2023 Benedict banners do not imply one owner. Two Dreaded Snake is the exact 2023 archival banner; no later bodily morphology is projected into it. El Niño is an archive banner, **not a speaking or human-shaped being**. No Week 6+ outcome enters the characters' knowledge. These six separately narrated league encounters do **not** require six mandatory novel chapters. Earlier Chronicle Weeks 1–4 and the separate causal manuscript *The Fourth Address* remain unchanged.

The first thing to come loose was the road's promise.

The chain at the ferry had held for seven days. Its links had bruised the mooring post, but none had broken. The copper terms above it had remained readable even after the library posted a competing interpretation beneath them. People waited, paid, turned back, or found reasons to call themselves exempt. On the fourth evening, two women had cooked supper for the families stranded under the wall. On the fifth, there was no oil for the pots.

At dawn Jake found Sena kneeling over a cold oven with her hands buried in ashes. Beside her lay the little pot she had sent over the river with the flour. It had come home empty. The boy who had carried bread at the chain stood in the doorway and would not meet her eyes.

"Did they eat?" Jake asked.

"The far-bank children did." She lifted her hands out of the ash. "The ones here ate the crusts. I sent the pot because there was something useful to send. Now I have to decide which useful thing to sell to bring the oil back."

Outside, the market bells began their familiar announcement of the weekly contests, but a second sound cut across them: three blows on the ferry's warning bell, then one, then three again. It was the signal for an obstruction in the channel. No one had used it since the road ceremony. Jake ran toward the landing, knowing only that the ferryman had sounded a warning, not whether it was meant for him.

Rain had fallen upstream during the night. There was no storm overhead, only a brown seam in the water and branches traveling faster than the ferry could turn. A broken wagon wheel caught against the chain. Each swell pulled it sideways. The iron ring set into the bank had begun to move.

Varn stood at the black post without his green coat, his sleeves rolled above his elbows. He and the ferryman were pulling in opposite directions. They agreed about the danger. They did not agree about who was permitted to touch the lock.

For the first time since the lease, the gate was less secure than the people it kept waiting.

## I. Red Leopards and Mixon it up for a week — A Market that Would Not Move

**VERIFIED ARCHIVE, NOT FICTIONAL CAUSATION:** Slot 2, **Red Leopards (home), 141.44**, versus slot 10, **Mixon it up for a week (away), 149.96**. Winner: **Mixon it up for a week (slot 10)**. Margin **8.52**. The contest does not authenticate fictional actions, locations or owner identities.

The cartwright's forge stood on a lane too narrow for a wagon to turn. Jake found its owner, Mara, carrying one wheel at a time toward the market, her palms black with grease and her left shoulder wrapped in a strip of sailcloth. She had repaired the axle that broke outside the weighing house. The driver was alive, but the unpaid wheel had become a quarrel between the road office, Varn's firm, and the driver herself. Mara had been told to charge whichever of them eventually accepted responsibility.

"If I wait for that," she said, "the cart will outlive me."

She set the repaired wheel upright. It rolled across the stones and struck the shutter of a shuttered shop. For a moment it continued spinning in place, making a small, absurd sound. Sena, arriving behind Jake, laughed before she could stop herself. Then she looked at her empty oil flask and the laughter left her.

The street was full of goods no one could take across the river without paying first. Varn had offered reduced passage to anyone who bought a month's permits in advance. The richest carriers had done so. The rest were weighing their freight against the price of surrendering it. Mara had brought the wheel to the market because a ferryman's work was never finished without round things: rollers, pulleys, hubs, drums. She wanted to replace the groaning windlass at the landing before the flood snapped it.

The road office refused to release the wheel. It had been listed as compensation for the injured driver's claim, and moving it would disturb the assessment. Mara put both hands against the rim and refused to let the clerk take it back.

Jake had spent the previous week trying to make one printed sentence tell the truth. Here a sentence was holding a piece of timber hostage. He took the wheel by its axle and started pushing. The clerk shouted after them that the removal would be recorded. Jake heard the warning and kept walking. Sena brought the empty oil flask, not because it could help with a wheel, but because she could not endure being told to return empty-handed again.

At the landing, the swollen current was grinding sticks through the chain like fingers through a comb. The ferryman showed Mara where the winch had cracked. She examined it once, then stripped the old wheel off its mounting and fitted the repaired one in its place. The hub was a different size. It took a length of cord and three cuts from Jake's knife to bind it. The wheel turned awkwardly, but it turned.

When the contest board reached the crowd, the Red Leopards banner had fallen narrowly to Mixon it up for a week. The result mattered to the people who followed the league. It did not certify a cartwright's choice, a stolen hour of labor, or the price of the repaired wheel. A pair of porters argued about the smaller margin while Mara tested the windlass with both hands.

"Who pays me?" she asked.

Jake opened his mouth. Varn answered from the black post.

"I will," he said.

"For the broken axle, or the new windlass?"

"For what you've done today."

"Then you've bought a wheel that isn't yours."

Varn looked toward the chain, where the current had climbed another finger-width up the post. He did not insist on his bargain. Mara lashed a second cord into place and ordered them all to pull.

The wheel held. The bank did not yet. And Jake understood that taking a thing out of a ledger did not make it free; someone would arrive to count it eventually.

## II. Benedict Coomhersnatch and Benedict Arnold — Four Hands on One Stone

**VERIFIED ARCHIVE, NOT FICTIONAL CAUSATION:** Slot 5, **Benedict Coomhersnatch (home), 110.46**, versus slot 8, **Benedict Arnold (away), 106.12**. Winner: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (slot 5)**. Margin **4.34**. The contest does not authenticate fictional actions, locations or owner identities.

The apprentice from the archive came down the road carrying a piece of the amphitheater on his back.

It was no larger than a child's chair, but it had been cut from the same stone as the steps taken to build the toll station. Two men followed him with a narrow plank. They were arguing about whether the stone belonged to the lender who had ordered the dismantling, the council that had approved it, or the public whose songs had once filled the space around it. The apprentice said nothing. He was using his breath to keep the weight from slipping.

They needed the stone for the ferry's shoreward brace. The post had started to tilt toward the river, and the men at the landing were driving wooden pegs into mud that could no longer hold them. The apprentice told Jake he had seen one uncracked block among the damaged stairs. He took it before anyone could invoice it.

Sena intercepted them. "Put it down before his back gives way."

"If we do, it won't come up again," said one of the men.

"Then you ought to have carried something you could lift."

Jake stepped under the apprentice's right arm. The second man made room. With four pairs of hands they lowered the stone onto the plank and dragged it toward the landing, leaving a white scrape on the roadway. At every uneven paving joint the apprentice gasped and apologized as if he had personally made the road rough.

At the corner a toll constable stopped them. The stone bore a small painted number, indicating that it had been allocated to the concession works. Removing it without an order could carry a fine. The constable made a sour face at the rule, but he read it aloud all the same. His father had worked the quarry, he told them, and knew exactly what the block weighed.

The apprentice asked whether the constable might take hold of the plank while explaining the fine.

That made him angry enough to do it. He seized the heavy end, and the four of them walked together. No one in the party called the quarrel resolved. The constable kept repeating that he would report what had happened. By the time they reached the landing, he was too short of breath to repeat the part about the fine.

A herald shouted the week's second verdict: Benedict Coomhersnatch had prevailed over Benedict Arnold by a slender margin. The names hung over the street from a contest board; their similarity was no evidence that one historical owner held both banners. The spectators who heard the result had mixed reactions, but the apprentice scarcely looked up. He was watching for the brace to take the weight.

They wedged the stone at the base of the leaning post. Its first placement failed. The river tilted it away from the wood, and the constable's boot caught under the plank. Jake pulled him clear just before the stone dropped. Nobody spoke until the wheel at the windlass ceased to squeal.

"Again," Mara said.

This time they set the block crosswise. The apprentice drove a splinter into the gap. The old quarried stone held firm enough for the men to let go.

Sena bent to check the constable's foot. His boot was split, but the bone was sound. He stared at the stone as if it had betrayed his profession.

"Will you report yourself?" she asked.

"I suppose so."

"Make certain you mention whose back carried it here."

The apprentice looked almost frightened by the idea of his name going into an official account. He had stolen nothing for himself. Yet Jake had seen what the stone could do to a man given only the task of delivering it. He wished, suddenly and without much hope, that the archive could record weight without requiring someone to bear it.

## III. Benedict Kenobi and Two Dreaded Snake — The First Victory and the River

**VERIFIED ARCHIVE, NOT FICTIONAL CAUSATION:** Slot 1, **Benedict Kenobi (home), 182.48**, versus slot 6, **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 129.42**. Winner: **Benedict Kenobi (slot 1)**. Margin **53.06**. The contest does not authenticate fictional actions, locations or owner identities.

By noon the ferry could not land on either bank.

The new wheel had saved the shoreward windlass, but the rising water had dragged the boat against the suspended chain. A rope stretched from the bow to the post, taut as a bowstring. Every time the hull struck it, the people waiting above the landing flinched. The ferryman had gone aboard with a hooked pole and was trying to push the boat into the current. His daughter held the other end of the rope from a ledge that had already gone under twice.

Jake could reach her only by stepping over the chain.

Varn ordered him back. He was speaking as the concession-holder, not as a man watching the river. The contract prohibited passage across the secured landing without his key. The ferryman shouted that there would be no landing to secure if the bow struck once more. A branch hit the hull, rolled under it, and rose on the other side like the back of a dark animal.

Jake had no reason to believe he could swim that water. His mind ran through the useless alternatives: fetch the constable; summon a clerk; copy the agreement; ask the archive whether the iron ring was public property. The daughter slid half a step toward the edge.

He stepped over the chain.

The water struck him above the knees, colder than he had expected. He nearly fell. On the far side of the post he found an iron shackle joining the boat's line to the land rope, and the pressure of the current had driven its pin sideways. He pulled at it with bare hands. The metal would not move. His fingers slipped, and he heard Mara calling for the knife he had left on the wheel.

Varn came down the steps carrying a hammer. For a moment Jake thought the man meant to force him back behind the gate. Instead Varn planted himself on the wet stone and struck the pin, once, twice, then again. The third blow freed it. The mooring line snapped clear. The boat swung downriver and missed the chain by a hand's breadth.

The ferryman leaped for the nearest bench. His daughter caught his wrist. Jake reached them an instant later and pulled with both hands until the man's knees struck stone. Behind them the released boat spun into the flooded channel. It was the ferry's only sound hull.

The daughter tried to follow it. Jake caught the back of her coat. She twisted out of his grip with such fury that he stepped away. There was no second boat to rescue. They watched the first disappear around the bend, taking the means of ordinary passage with it.

A messenger from the upper square had arrived carrying the weekly contest sheet. The proposed Jake-associated historical banner, Benedict Kenobi, had defeated Two Dreaded Snake by a clear score. After four consecutive recorded defeats, the slot-one team had its first victory. Jake heard the names and the numbers as through a wall. Nothing about the contest established that he had saved anyone from a real river; the water, the ferry and every choice here belonged to the invented Commonwealth.

"You could have killed him," the daughter said to Varn.

Varn put the hammer down carefully. "The line was already killing the boat."

"You could have opened the lock before the rope tightened."

"I didn't know it would."

She picked up the hammer and carried it to Mara. She would not allow the creditor to claim it as his own.

Jake looked at his palm. A thin curl of skin had peeled away where the shackle bit him. The blood was a bright, small thing beside the vast brown water. He had crossed a line he had spent days describing, and the consequence was not freedom but a vanished ferry and a family with no way to earn its living.

"Will the passage remain closed?" he asked.

Varn raised his head. "There is no passage."

The daughter turned on them both. "There are people on the other bank."

Jake thought of Sena's pot, returned empty, and the market's cold ovens. For the first time that week he stopped looking at the lock. He began looking for another boat.

## IV. The Blew Waffle's and Arm Chair GM — The Cost of a Seat

**VERIFIED ARCHIVE, NOT FICTIONAL CAUSATION:** Slot 7, **The Blew Waffle's (home), 181.19**, versus slot 11, **Arm Chair GM (away), 186.06**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **4.87**. The contest does not authenticate fictional actions, locations or owner identities.

There was an old punt beneath the amphitheater, kept there by the singers who once used it to carry painted scenery along the river. It had not floated since the stairs were whole. The foreman said its planking was sound but the seams were not; one end had been crushed when the quarry crew removed a tier of seats. They could repair it if they had pitch, fresh timber, and time. The landing had none of these in the right quantities.

Mara sent the apprentice to find a kettle. Sena brought the last thick fat from the market pantry. The foreman protested that kitchen grease would spoil the seams when it cooled. "Then you will have to row quickly," she answered.

Jake helped lift the punt into the street. It dragged behind them like a coffin. The seventh singer, the ferryman's daughter, found them at the broken steps. She had barely spoken to Jake since he caught her coat, but she took the stern and directed them toward a stretch of bank where the current ran less fiercely. Her father followed without speaking. He had lost the boat that paid for their bread. His silence had a greater weight than any rebuke Jake could imagine.

At the half-demolished amphitheater, the oldest singer was trying to save a row of benches. Varn's contractors had promised to remove the remaining tiers in the morning. People had gathered on the intact ledge to watch the water. Some wanted the seats moved to the public square. Others needed their timber immediately for the punt. The argument began in low voices and became a shouting match when the foreman pointed at the legs of the best bench.

"Those belonged to the city before the road," the singer insisted.

"Tomorrow they will belong to the road," said the foreman.

"They belonged to the people who sat on them."

"Then the people can decide whether to sit or cross."

No vote was taken. The oldest singer went to the bench, pressed his hands to the wood, and broke the first slat loose. His grip trembled. The rest of the chorus followed, not because they accepted the foreman's argument, but because the ferryman was standing beside them with an empty rope.

Jake carried the boards down to the punt. When the first seam was packed, Sena heated the fat. The smell recalled supper more sharply than anything he had eaten that week. The apprentice rubbed a rag over the planks until his knuckles split. Mara cut the new crosspiece to fit the ruined bow. They worked under the painted competition banners strung above the square.

The week's close contest between The Blew Waffle's and Arm Chair GM had gone to Arm Chair GM by fewer than five points. Two men argued heatedly about the narrow result until the daughter asked one of them to take the heavy end of the punt. The argument stopped when he felt the weight. Another man kept talking, but he found a hammer as he did.

By dusk the punt would float in shallow water. The foreman would not certify it for the river. The ferryman tested the seams with a bare foot and gave no judgment. His daughter knelt to tie a fresh cord around the stern and put a second one into Jake's hand.

"Do not hold me this time," she said. "Hold the boat."

There was no clean way for Jake to tell her that he had been afraid she would die. He tied the cord where she showed him.

The remaining benches stood in the broken amphitheater like missing teeth. The singers would have nowhere to sit at their next rehearsal, and yet the light in the kitchen might return to the far bank. Jake had wanted the city to make a decision that harmed no one. The city had chosen wood over seating, and it had not chosen lightly.

## V. Eggs Benedict and The KING of the Octagon Village — A Song Taken Apart

**VERIFIED ARCHIVE, NOT FICTIONAL CAUSATION:** Slot 12, **Eggs Benedict (home), 158.76**, versus slot 3, **The KING of the Octagon Village (away), 161.84**. Winner: **The KING of the Octagon Village (slot 3)**. Margin **3.08**. The contest does not authenticate fictional actions, locations or owner identities.

The oldest singer came to the landing carrying the music for the road ceremony, folded into a tight rectangle.

He had written it when the amphitheater was whole. Its last verse had been intended for the ferryman's daughter, but the opening committee had refused her a place on the platform. In the week since, he had rewritten the verse three times. None satisfied him. He told Jake the new words would sound falsely triumphant while the daughter was trying to replace her father's livelihood with a painted punt.

"Then don't make it a victory song," Jake said.

"What would you have us sing?"

Jake did not know. It embarrassed him that the old man expected a suitable answer from someone whose main achievement had been to lose an argument with a chain.

The singer unfolded the paper. The refrain named every bridge and gate the city's founders claimed to have built. Several had never existed. Others had been raised by people whose names did not fit the rhythm. It was a magnificent piece of public music and a poor account of work. The singer admitted as much; he told Jake he had once been paid to make it sound otherwise.

He began crossing out the lines himself. Jake watched the old man reduce his own masterpiece to an unsteady sequence of verbs: carry, bind, hold, loosen, float. Sena joined them with a bundle of damp rags, and the oldest singer asked her how a cook knew when oil would last until morning.

"I don't," she said. "I know how much I have."

He wrote that down. When he asked the constable what a guard knew, the man said he knew who would be blamed when something went wrong, though that was seldom the same as knowing what was right. The singer folded the paper again and looked dissatisfied with every answer.

The fifth week had produced another narrow contest: Eggs Benedict had fallen to The KING of the Octagon Village. The little margin thrilled some onlookers, who repeated the numbers as if a boundary between success and failure could be measured with a ruler. The daughter paid them no mind. She was measuring rope for the punt, and the difference between enough and not enough had become immediate.

As the singer tried the new refrain, the apprentice approached with the stone-carriers' plank on his shoulder. The words gave him a place in the song, but he would not accept it.

"I didn't build the road," he said.

"You helped move the brace."

"And whose seat did I take apart to do it?"

The singer stared at his paper. He looked as surprised as if the accusation belonged to a different kind of song. The apprentice objected to how history would remember the dismantling. If the song said they had rescued the ferry, the listeners might forget that the ferry was gone and its debt still stood.

The singer took a knife and cut his page into seven strips. He handed one to each person nearest him. They were to add a line of what they had lost, not only what they had done. Jake received the narrowest strip. On it he wrote: I put my hand on the rope and let the boat go.

The daughter read his words and said, "You helped bring my father ashore."

"That isn't what I lost."

"No," she said, and looked toward the bend in the river. "It isn't."

By the time the singers gathered, the performance was neither hymn nor official apology. The voices entered at different times. People could understand the words only when they stopped trying to make them one grand account. Even then the old singer could not find a final line. The daughter left a space for it and returned to the punt.

A song had been taken apart to build another kind of boat. The city had not agreed on what it owed the people who remained.

## VI. El Niño and The LLC — The Boat Without a Name

**VERIFIED ARCHIVE, NOT FICTIONAL CAUSATION:** Slot 9, **El Niño (home), 97.02**, versus slot 4, **The LLC (away), 161.56**. Winner: **The LLC (slot 4)**. Margin **64.54**. The contest does not authenticate fictional actions, locations or owner identities.

The punt went into the water at dusk.

Mara wanted another hour to seal the bow. The ferryman said an hour would bring night and a faster current. Varn had stationed two constables at the concession post, not to bar the punt, but to record which goods it carried so that freight charges could still be assessed. The ferry's daughter listened until one of them asked her to declare the purpose of the crossing.

"People," she said.

"Passengers are a category."

"Then write the category."

"And the cargo?"

She pointed to Sena's empty pot, the wrapped bread, the oil flask, and the coils of rope. The constable began to read a tariff aloud. Jake felt the familiar impulse to argue about words. Instead he picked up the heaviest coil and carried it into the punt. His injured palm opened again. He closed his fingers around the rope and let the blood soak into it.

Varn came down the steps with a key.

"I can waive tonight's charge," he said. "The repairs will be counted against the concession."

Sena stepped between him and the boat. "If you add the punt to the debt, you'll own the thing we made because you lost the thing we had."

"I didn't lose the ferry."

"You made the old landing harder to use."

"The current took the boat."

Both were true in the narrow ways that could make a city impossible to govern. Jake watched Varn look at the new timber where amphitheater seats had been fitted to the punt's ribs. The creditor had no invoice for that. The seats had come from an institution he had ordered dismantled. If he valued the wood as private concession material, he would have to admit what his creditors had taken.

A runner reached the landing with the last weekly verdict. The LLC had defeated El Niño decisively. The latter name belonged to the archive's weather-named competition banner; no storm-shaped creature had fought in the city, and no human voice belonged to the banner. The river's fictional rising water had no evidentiary connection to the contest outcome. The runner waited for cheers and received none. Everyone was watching the punt.

Varn turned to the constables. "Record a temporary crossing."

"Under the lease?"

"Under my name."

That was more than the law required and less than Sena wanted. It made him personally responsible for any debt the office later refused to forgive. It did not abolish the concession or return the lost ferry. The first constable hesitated, then opened his ledger. The daughter climbed into the punt. Her father settled at the stern, testing the short pole in the shallows.

Jake asked for a place aboard.

"No," she said.

He was ready to argue, and she saw it.

"The river is taking too much already. We need you here. When we come back, you will have to pull us out."

She was right. He had been inside his own rescue all afternoon; the difficult work now was to remain on shore and trust someone else to finish it.

The punt pushed into the current. It caught hard against the brown seam. The ferryman used the pole to turn, and the daughter pulled on the rope Jake held from shore. Farther out she took the line round a cleat, using the tension to swing the bow away from the broken wheel still lodged against the chain. Once it passed the strongest pull, the punt settled. Its low hull disappeared behind a rise of water and returned.

There were people on the far bank. Jake could not count them, but he heard their voices when the punt touched stone. A single lantern moved down to the edge. Someone lifted Sena's pot aboard. Another handed the daughter a bundle. The voices grew quiet. The punt began its return.

The constables' ledger lay open beside Varn. A figure had been entered for the value of the crossing, then scored through. Jake saw Varn examine the crossed figure as if it were a wound. The concession-holder had saved a crossing for which he might now be held personally liable. Neither of them had arrived at that risk with innocent hands.

The daughter came back with three passengers and the pot full of broth. The boat leaked a little at the bow, but it came ashore. The ferryman did not celebrate. He set a plank under the keel, helped the oldest passenger onto dry ground, and told Mara which seam would need replacing.

Varn unlocked the chain and lowered it onto the stones. He did not throw it into the river. "At first light," he told the constables, "we have to decide what this becomes."

Jake looked at the iron links, suddenly ordinary and heavy. The river was still rising. The lease would still be read in the morning. A night of passage could make a permanent enemy of a man who had not yet chosen to be one.

Sena opened the pot and served the first bowl to the ferryman.

No one asked what it cost.

---

## After the Fifth Reckoning — What the River Kept

By midnight the punt had crossed twice more. Its seams were packed with rags, and Mara had set a lantern in the ruined bow so passengers could see the water before they stepped into it. Sena had found enough oil for one pot of stew, not for the entire kitchen. The old singer, whose music had been cut into seven pieces, sat on a stone from the demolished amphitheater and listened to the ferry's daughter call instructions from the river.

Jake had heard the weekly board read again: Benedict Kenobi's first recorded victory after an opening 0–4 season, **182.48–129.42** over Two Dreaded Snake. The number offered no absolution for the shackle he had opened or the ferry they had lost. He was glad the fictional man had finally seen his banner win, but the joy was interrupted whenever the punt struck a floating branch.

At the black post Varn nailed no new copper plate. He sat with his coat around his shoulders, counting the hours until the office opened. He had put his own name beneath one temporary crossing and would need to answer for it. The chain lay beside him on the stones. A child had woven a strand of river grass through one of its links, not as a symbol anyone had authorized, but because the child had been waiting a long time and wanted something to do.

The daughter returned at the third crossing with a broken oar. She gave it to Jake without ceremony and asked him to cut a new handle from the plank taken from the amphitheater seats. The work would take until dawn. He could not put the handle into a ledger, and nobody would applaud when he finished. Yet the person who needed it would know whether it was sound.

Jake carried the plank under the lantern. For now the ferry was gone, the public passage was improvised, and the city had neither settled its debts nor decided who owned the riverbank. Someone had taken a risk that could be punished; someone else had taken a risk that could drown them. All of them would need to remember the difference.

He set the knife to the wood.

**Historical handoff.** Week 5 archival winners are slot 10 Mixon it up for a week, slot 5 Benedict Coomhersnatch, slot 1 Benedict Kenobi, slot 11 Arm Chair GM, slot 3 The KING of the Octagon Village and slot 4 The LLC. Six historical fixtures encompass all twelve archival team slots. The proposed Jake/slot-1 historical literary association stands at **1–4** after its first win, **182.48–129.42** (margin **53.06**), contingent on an unauthenticated owner/slot link. All people, property, water, losses, laws, motives, actions, weather, geography and dialogue in the narrative are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. Week 6+ is outside the characters' knowledge.
