# 2023 — Week 6: The Hour No Boat Crossed
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six documented matchups · substantial NONCANON historical fiction*

**Evidence and authority.** Competitive results are transcribed from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`, labeled Flaim / ESPN league 1417621. The original ESPN provider SHA is unavailable. Historical human-owner associations are unverified: Jake's proposed association with archive slot 1 is **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. Home/away competition fields do not identify physical venues. The river, city, charter, citizens, songs, debts, ferry and punt are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. Only Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`) has limited interior viewpoint; supporting people have observable choices and speech, not separate interior narration. Four separate 2023 Benedict banners are not four verified identities. Two Dreaded Snake is an archived name, with no future morphology inferred. El Niño is an archival team banner and, in the broader authorized fiction, an environmental force, **not a speaking or human-shaped being**. Week 7+ results do not enter the characters' knowledge. These six source-anchored encounters are a companion Chronicle, not six mandatory novel chapters. The separate 2023 causal manuscript *The Fourth Address*, the 2023 Prologue and Weeks 1–5, and the protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III remain untouched.

The oar handle was too smooth.

Jake had planed it through the night, taking away every knot that might raise a blister. When he held it out to the ferryman's daughter, she turned it in her hands, rested its end against the stone landing, and tried to lift the weight of the empty blade. Her palms slipped at once.

"Your hand likes it," she said. "Mine won't, in the rain."

She put the handle back beside his feet.

Across the landing, the small punt rolled with each pull of the river. Three days earlier the ferry had been lost. The punt had made passages since then, but none had been free of labor. Its seams opened in the cold, its bow carried scars from the wheel that had caught the old boat, and the boards taken from the amphitheater creaked whenever two passengers stood on the same side. The ferryman used his short pole only in the shallows now. A long white bandage showed beneath his cuff.

At dawn there were twenty-three people waiting, as Jake counted them, and twice as many parcels. Sena had brought a kettle of thin broth to the wall. Mara, the cartwright, was crouched over the bow with a length of iron and a mallet. The oldest singer sat on a broken step, trying to make seven torn strips of music into a single page without hiding the losses their words named.

Varn arrived carrying the key to a chain that no longer blocked the landing. Behind him came a man Jake had never seen, wearing the unmarked brown coat of a traveling creditor. He had brought no guards. The constable walked with him anyway.

"This crossing cannot remain informal," the stranger said.

The daughter put the oar on her shoulder. "It isn't informal to the people who hold it above the water."

On the city wall the sixth set of competition verdicts had been posted. Men went to read them, shouted over close margins, and returned to the landing because the boat still held the greater question. The notices were certain about winners and numbers. The punt was certain only about weight.

Jake tried the handle once more. He had removed too much wood to make it pleasant. Repairing that mistake would require a strip of rough leather, if the city's remaining saddler would part with one. He went to find the saddler while the daughter began deciding who would cross.

## I. Benedict Kenobi and Eggs Benedict — Two Hands' Breadth

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; FICTIONAL EVENTS BELOW ARE NOT EVIDENCE OF CAUSATION.** **Benedict Kenobi (home), 132.20** versus **Eggs Benedict (away), 130.02**. Winner: **Benedict Kenobi (slot 1)**. Margin **2.18**.

The saddler had no leather to sell.

He held out a cracked harness and told Jake to take from it what he could without destroying the buckle. The harness had belonged to a delivery mule that had once brought flour along the road. Since the toll concession took effect, the mule's owner had stopped coming. The saddler had not seen the animal in a week.

Jake cut a strip from the underside. There was sweat dried into the grain and a smell of hay that outlasted the animal. He offered payment. The saddler asked instead that the daughter take a parcel across before sunset. Its contents were wrapped in cloth, and the man opened them without being asked: two metal hinges and a small whetstone, for his sister, whose oven door no longer closed.

"You can call those personal goods if that helps," the saddler said.

It did not help. That morning the crossing notice listed passengers separately from freight. The clerk at the black post had marked every parcel with a chalk sign and placed the hinges under the higher category. Jake asked who had given the clerk permission to classify something the public had built from public seats.

"The concession did," the clerk said.

"The concession lost its ferry."

"The concession did not lose its meaning."

There were four people ahead of the saddler's parcel. The daughter listened to the exchange without offering to settle the law. She tied a length of cord around the punt's middle rib and made Jake stand where the heaviest passenger would stand. The wet wood bowed. When he moved two hand-widths toward the stern, it recovered.

"That's how much room the argument has," she said. "Not as much as you want."

A runner was still repeating the narrow week's result from the wall: Benedict Kenobi had defeated Eggs Benedict by 2.18. It was the proposed slot-one banner's second win after four opening defeats, a standing of **2–4**. Jake had felt an unworthy lift of pride when he first heard it. He could imagine a crowd admiring a margin narrow enough to be called skill, though the result established no virtue and no right to command the shore.

He wrapped the leather round the oar. It made a grip rough enough to hold when damp. The daughter tested it again, first with one palm and then with both. This time the handle stayed where she intended.

At the gate a woman whose grandson waited on the far bank offered to carry the hinges inside her own coat. The clerk objected. The daughter asked whether he intended to search the woman. He went red but did not answer.

"I'll carry the woman," the daughter said. "What is in her coat is her burden until she asks for help."

The woman stepped aboard, her coat heavy and lopsided. The daughter took the stern and settled the oar into the new grip. Jake stood beside the painter, ready to pull. It was not a solution to the tariff. It was a crossing obtained by someone whom the tariff had failed to imagine.

As the punt moved out, the saddler looked down at the torn harness in his hands. He had helped his sister. He had also lost a piece of equipment he might never replace. Jake understood that the narrow sports victory on the wall belonged to its own tally; it could not price either of those facts.

The daughter returned without a passenger. She showed him the leather, dark where her palms had pressed. "That will last a few crossings," she said. "Find out what happens after."

## II. Two Dreaded Snake and Benedict Coomhersnatch — The Third Passenger

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; FICTIONAL EVENTS BELOW ARE NOT EVIDENCE OF CAUSATION.** **Two Dreaded Snake (home), 135.84** versus **Benedict Coomhersnatch (away), 136.14**. Winner: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (slot 5)**. Margin **0.30**.

By midmorning the wind had pushed a skin of foam against the stranded ferry wheel. The daughter drew the punt into the shelter of a stone step and ordered its passengers out. A porter accused her of inventing danger to keep the crossing for her friends. She asked him to place his hand under the middle bench. He felt the plank shift and withdrew his arm.

Mara came down with two braces. She set the first across the punt's ribs, then made the porter lift its outer end while she drove a wooden pin. He could not hold it steady. The apprentice took his place, braced one bare foot against the stone, and held until his face reddened. The porter watched and finally put his shoulder beneath the timber.

The ferryman offered his pole for the second brace. His daughter shook her head.

"It doesn't fit," she told him.

"It will if you cut it."

"Then when the river drops you will have no pole."

The old man touched the bandage below his sleeve. He said nothing. His daughter measured the pole against the hull a second time, and Jake saw that her first judgment had been right. They took a slat from a broken market trestle instead.

The bell from the competition wall rang again. Another result had been separated by almost nothing: Benedict Coomhersnatch had edged Two Dreaded Snake by 0.30. A boy at the landing repeated the margin three times, marveling that such a small difference could settle an official contest. The daughter's task involved differences even smaller: the thickness of a pin, the bend of a wet board, the moment a passenger chose to stand before being told.

When the brace was in place, the porter demanded that six adults be taken on the next passage. Two carried baskets; another carried a sleeping child. The punt had managed that many once in calm water, he said, and the work on the braces should make it stronger.

"Four," the daughter answered.

"You haven't even tried six."

"That's why I can still answer."

The porter turned to Jake. It was the first time that day anyone had invited him to overrule her. He had helped release the old ferry, had argued about the road's charter, and had been seen standing beside the black post when Varn lifted the chain. The porter expected those appearances to add up to authority.

Jake said, "She has the boat."

"She has our boat," the man replied.

The daughter climbed into it with her feet far apart to test the new braces. "You may call it yours when you've learned where it breaks. Until then you can help me keep it whole."

She sent four people across and left the other two waiting. The porter shouted that she was choosing who mattered. On the far bank, the first passengers stepped out, and the daughter turned back through the current without a reply. Jake did not know whether her silence made the waiting easier or harder. He knew the two people still on shore could see that she had returned for them.

When the second passage began, the sleeping child woke and cried. The porter, who had held the timber, stood on the bank with his arms idle. Mara handed him the mallet.

"There's another loose rib," she said.

He knelt without being asked again. Work was not agreement, and he had not withdrawn his accusation. The punt gained a brace; the queue gained a person who knew what the brace cost. Jake thought that might matter tomorrow, when there would be another question of who was allowed to decide.

## III. The KING of the Octagon Village and Mixon it up for a week — The Painted Timber

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; FICTIONAL EVENTS BELOW ARE NOT EVIDENCE OF CAUSATION.** **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 116.54** versus **Mixon it up for a week (away), 124.56**. Winner: **Mixon it up for a week (slot 10)**. Margin **8.02**.

At noon the apprentice discovered that the replacement bench had begun to split.

He found it by sound. As Mara drove the third pin, the plank spoke with a high dry crack, unlike the lower complaint of wood bending under a load. He grabbed her wrist before the mallet fell again. The point of the pin had entered the grain where an old nail hole ran unseen beneath the paint.

Mara examined the hole, then took her knife and widened it. The weakness ran nearly the length of the brace. Nothing could be saved by making the crack invisible.

"Put it back," said the singer, who had come to watch the repair. "The theater used that piece for fifty seasons."

"Then it has served long enough," Mara answered.

"That is not your season to spend."

She laid the split wood on the stones. It had come from the amphitheater seats, dismantled in the fourth week to keep a passage open. The singer picked out the carved initials of a dead performer and held the plank as though weight alone could repair it.

They needed a sound timber of that length. The market trestles had already been stripped, and the saddler's old stable had surrendered its door brace. Jake found himself calculating the interval before the next crossing, the number of people waiting, the amount of food Sena could still serve. The sums changed whenever another body arrived on the shore.

The apprentice pointed up the road toward the ceremonial arch erected for the opening of the crossing. Its painted beam stood over a lane nobody used now, because the lane led straight into the closed toll gate. The arch was thick enough. It bore the city's founding refrain in tall red letters.

The singer stepped between them and the arch. "That is the one thing we built before the debt," he said.

"We built the bench before it too," the apprentice replied. "And you let us take that."

"I helped take it," the singer said. "I know what that cost."

A clerk arrived with the third official verdict of the sixth scoring week, a victory by Mixon it up for a week over The KING of the Octagon Village. Jake heard the margin, 8.02, above the murmur of the argument. Two boys imitated the herald's flourish. The beams above them did not move. The contest had not fractured the punt or put the arch in the path of the carpenter's saw.

Mara found a ladder. She handed it to the singer.

He stared at it. "You want me to cut my own words down?"

"I want you to decide where the strong wood is," she said. "If you know another piece, name it."

He walked the arch from end to end and tapped it with his stick. At the third stroke the beam answered with the same dense note as a sound oar. The singer lowered his head, then held the ladder while the apprentice climbed.

Jake helped catch the beam. It fell more heavily than they expected, knocking the breath from his chest. One of the painted letters split against the paving. The apprentice carried the rest to the shore and planed away the end ruined by weather. The singer collected the broken letter and put it in his pocket.

When the new brace was bolted in place, it showed a fragment of the city's old refrain beneath the seat. Passengers stepping into the punt would see those red strokes near their feet without knowing where they had come from.

The apprentice asked whether the singer wanted the arch rebuilt.

"Not with rotten wood," he said. "Don't put a pretty lie above a road and call it memory."

He took the seven strips of his torn music from his coat. This time he did not rearrange them. He asked the apprentice to write the name of the arch on the reverse of one strip, along with the reason it had been taken. He would sing the loss if he could not display the beam.

Jake watched the apprentice hesitate over the word *needed*. He wrote *used* instead. The word left room for someone to argue. The next crossing began after the paint on the brace had already started to bleed into the water.

## IV. Benedict Arnold and Arm Chair GM — A Creditor Without a Boat

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; FICTIONAL EVENTS BELOW ARE NOT EVIDENCE OF CAUSATION.** **Benedict Arnold (home), 126.08** versus **Arm Chair GM (away), 127.50**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **1.42**.

The traveling creditor returned in the afternoon with a young clerk and a measuring chain.

He asked to examine the punt. His clerk counted the timbers, marking each against an inventory of assets belonging to the ferry concession. Mara stood in his way when he reached for the new brace.

"This came off the city's arch," she said.

"The arch stood on leased ground," the creditor answered.

"The arch didn't stand in the river."

"Rights to improvements can pass with the site."

He spoke like a man accustomed to being listened to. Jake could not tell whether he was lying, because the words might have been true under some version of the disputed charter. The library still held two. The creditor had apparently read only one.

Varn arrived without his key. His coat was damp at the hem, and a spot of ink darkened his fingers. The creditor greeted him by title and asked for the document authorizing his personal guarantee of the first improvised passage. Varn handed him a folded sheet. The creditor unfolded it but did not read it at once.

"How many passages since you signed?"

Varn told him.

"And how many charges collected?"

"None under my waiver."

The creditor looked toward the kitchen where Sena was serving broth. "Then the service consumes capital without servicing the debt."

"People are crossing," Varn said.

"People are not security."

That answer drove Sena from the kitchen. She set down the ladle so sharply that broth struck the stone. "You would have no creditors if people didn't work on both banks," she said.

The man turned the paper over. "I lend against things that can be returned."

"Then lend against hunger. It keeps returning."

He did not smile. Nor did he threaten her. He told Varn that the investors beyond the city would cease supplying iron, oil and rope unless someone gave them a recoverable claim. There were workers far from this river whose wages depended on the ferry debt being paid. Jake had no way to verify the investors or the workers, but the claim was not implausible. Each person at the landing could name a supply that had already failed to arrive.

The fourth result was called from the wall while the measuring chain hung from the punt's bow: Arm Chair GM had defeated Benedict Arnold by 1.42. A little crowd argued over the points. The creditor glanced toward them and then back to his clerk. A recorded margin could decide a competition without deciding what counted as a boat.

The clerk measured the painted brace. "Who owns this?"

"The singers paid for the arch," Mara answered.

"Who owns the seats?"

"The people who sat on them," said the singer, appearing behind Jake.

"Who owns the hull?"

No one answered. The lost ferry's ribs had belonged to the concession, but the punt was made of theater benches, market wood, donated rope and timber cut from an arch. The clerk could not put one claimant's name against all of them. He waited while the creditor considered the empty spaces.

Jake expected Varn to declare that the city owned the punt. Instead he said, "If you seize that boat, you must also claim the labor that made it. Show me where the charter gives you a person."

The creditor folded his measuring chain. "I can seek an injunction before dark," he said. "And I can make your guarantee due at dawn."

"Then do that," Varn answered. "But don't tell your clerk he inventoried a boat. He inventoried a quarrel."

It was the first time Jake had heard Varn refuse to reduce a situation to the figure he could charge. The refusal did not undo the tolls. The creditor wrote down every name he could obtain, including Varn's and Mara's. He asked Jake for his name as a witness. Jake gave it, though he knew that a record could as readily be used to threaten as to protect.

The daughter was hauling the punt back into shallow water. She had heard the exchange and offered no verdict. She asked the clerk to help keep the bow from striking the stone. He looked at the ink on his fingers before taking the rope, then did as she asked.

## V. The LLC and The Blew Waffle's — A Road of Shoulders

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; FICTIONAL EVENTS BELOW ARE NOT EVIDENCE OF CAUSATION.** **The LLC (home), 145.78** versus **The Blew Waffle's (away), 99.92**. Winner: **The LLC (slot 4)**. Margin **45.86**.

The first sack burst at the lower market turn.

A porter had tried to drag it by himself from the kitchen store to the landing. Its stitching caught on the broken masonry left from the amphitheater steps, and flour scattered white into the mud. The man stood above it in disbelief. No one had offered to pay for the passage of flour; everyone had offered to eat the bread.

Sena came out with a broom and a basin. She did not scrape the wet flour into the food bowl. Instead she separated what was dry enough to save and gave the rest to Mara for paste. Their economy had become so narrow that spoiled flour might still seal a plank.

Two children knelt to collect the dry grain. A man from the far bank objected that he had been waiting since morning for a sack already promised to him. Sena told him the flour was not a debt she owed a person whose name she did not know. He gave his name. She repeated it, then asked him to carry a basket rather than stand where he was and count another person's loss.

The punt could carry a few sacks or several passengers, but not both. The daughter had refused freight during the morning rush. Her refusal had been called selfish by men whose own arms were free. When the kitchen threatened to close, those men returned carrying long poles and cloth slings.

Mara drew a route in the mud: not a new bridge, not a heroic river crossing, but a way of moving the heaviest goods to a higher stone shelf where the punt could be loaded without sinking into the soft landing. It required four people on each pole. The upper way passed behind the broken amphitheater. Its steps were gone, and every load would have to be lifted across the gap.

Jake took a corner of the first sling. The weight settled into his shoulder and drove him sideways. They tried again, this time counting aloud. On the third step a carrier slipped; no one released the cloth, but all eight had to stop while the injured man tested his ankle. The sack did not fall. The work had taken longer than carrying it down the ordinary road would have done before the toll.

Near the wall a herald announced that The LLC had beaten The Blew Waffle's by a wide margin, 45.86. The result raised a cheer among several onlookers, then slipped beneath the sound of the carriers counting their steps. Jake wondered how many kinds of decisive victory a city could celebrate while its food moved one shoulder at a time. The matchup supplied no answer and caused none of the material trouble.

At the stone shelf the daughter inspected the sacks. One had been wetted along its seam. She told Sena it would have to remain behind; the boat was already taking water at the stern.

"That one feeds twelve," Sena said.

"Then you must decide whether twelve eat after I come back, or six ride without me coming back."

Sena stared at the seam. She was not grateful for the choice. She sent the wet sack to the kitchen to be baked at once and put two smaller baskets on the punt. The man from the far bank climbed aboard carrying one of them. His hands were raw where the pole had rubbed.

The carrier with the twisted ankle sat beneath the wall while the children brought him water. The porter whose sack had burst asked whether they would be paid for the lifting. Sena said she could pay with supper and no more. He swore, kicked at the mud, and came back the next time a sling was filled. His return did not make the arrangement fair.

Jake was standing with the empty sling when the creditor's clerk approached. He had been watching the entire line. "The route across the upper shelf may constitute a bypass," he said.

"A bypass of what?" Jake asked. "The missing steps?"

"Of the approved approach to the concession."

Mara rested her hands on the carrying pole. "Then improve your approach."

The clerk looked at the damaged steps and the river beyond. He did not produce a fee. He asked to borrow a pole for the evening, when he would help carry the last of the sacks. His master, watching from the road, said nothing.

The food moved. The law did not. Both facts could be true without one being a victory over the other.

## VI. Red Leopards and El Niño — The Hour No Boat Crossed

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; FICTIONAL EVENTS BELOW ARE NOT EVIDENCE OF CAUSATION.** **Red Leopards (home), 173.68** versus **El Niño (away), 126.20**. Winner: **Red Leopards (slot 2)**. Margin **47.48**.

By dusk the queue had grown beyond the wall.

On the near bank stood people who wanted to go home, people who wanted to sell, people who wanted to carry news, and people who had stayed because they could not bear to leave someone else waiting. Across the river a lantern burned in the window of the house where the saddler's sister lived. A second lantern showed farther along the bank, at a level Jake had not seen before. The water was climbing the stones again.

The daughter ran her hand along the new brace and pressed at its painted letters. She tested the oar in the socket, then examined the leather Jake had wrapped around its handle. Two of the stitches had worked loose.

"Give me the needle," she said.

There was no needle among the tools. Mara offered a thin nail. The daughter shook her head. She could have made it work, Jake thought, but she was tired of making things work when the difference between work and disaster was being charged entirely to her.

The creditor arrived with an order sealed in brown wax. He did not call it a seizure, and Jake could not read the entire instrument from where he stood. He did hear the terms read aloud: no more unrecorded passage, no movement of secured freight until ownership could be established, and any further loss to be borne by those operating the craft. The creditor offered to set aside the last condition if Varn signed another guarantee.

Varn looked at the daughter. "Could you make one more trip?"

She did not answer immediately. A boy on the far bank had begun waving a strip of white cloth. The ferryman recognized him and shouted that his grandmother was waiting at the near gate. The boy waved harder when he heard the voice.

"One more," the constable said.

The daughter held out the oar. Its end was splitting where Jake had cut too deep. She set it against the stone so that everyone could see the fracture. The ferryman asked for the tool and tested the edge with a thumbnail. He did not say it was safe.

A final herald came down from the competition wall: Red Leopards had defeated El Niño, 173.68 to 126.20, a margin of 47.48. It was a clear archival sporting verdict, but the El Niño name did not put a speaking storm in the marketplace, and no contest result made the river rise. The people in line scarcely turned toward the announcement. The only decision within hearing distance was being made by the woman holding the oar.

"No," she said.

The constable asked her to repeat it.

"No more crossings today."

The boy on the far bank was still waving. Her father looked out toward him. Sena stepped forward with a wrapped loaf for the grandmother, as if one final passenger and one final piece of bread might be enough to persuade the daughter.

"You think I don't see them?" the daughter asked. She turned the cracked handle so the light caught it. "I see every person who waits. I also see the water where you are not looking. I will not put them into a boat that cannot get them home."

The creditor said the craft was an essential service. He cited Varn's personal obligation and the loss already incurred by the abandoned ferry. The daughter asked which part of his order required him to row. He began to answer, then stopped. The constable's face tightened. No one had words broad enough to make a wet piece of wood sound safe.

She called to the far bank. The boy could not hear her over the river. She asked the singer to send the message, because the singer's voice had carried across the water before there had been any dispute about tolls. He climbed the broken wall and shouted that the boat would not come until morning. The boy lowered the cloth. Jake watched him remain for a long moment before the lantern at the house shifted toward him.

The daughter loosened the painter and ordered the punt dragged above the wet line. It took six people. Jake reached first, expecting to be useful, but she directed him to the stern where the keel might shear against the stone. The ferryman put a shoulder beside his. Mara carried the tools. The porter with the injured ankle found a rope and worked from where he sat.

The boat was hauled clear. Water spilled from the seams and ran back toward the river. The daughter placed the oar beside it, then stood on the damp stones with her hands open.

"Tomorrow," she said, "someone will have to answer three questions before I push off. Who decides the load? Who repairs what breaks? Who pays the people who take the risk?"

Varn did not promise to answer. The creditor asked for the questions in writing. She told him he had heard them. The singer repeated them from the wall, not as a refrain but so the people on the farther bank could know why the boat had stopped.

Jake had carried arguments to the gate for weeks. He could have offered another notice, another compromise, another brilliant distinction between public and private. All of those would have given the gathering a way to admire the form of the answer and postpone the work beneath it.

Instead he took the broken oar and placed it where the clerk could examine the split. Then he moved out of the daughter's way.

The crowd did not cheer. Several people were angry. One called her cruel. Another demanded to know whether his mother would now sleep on the wrong side of the river. The daughter did not make herself innocent by answering every accusation. She asked Sena for oil, helped light two lamps, and gave the ferryman the larger one to hang beside the grounded punt.

That evening the city had enough light to see the passage it lacked.

---

## After the Sixth Reckoning — A Shore With Its Own Terms

For an hour no boat crossed.

The river remained where it was, except for the slow, heavy movement of its current. People kept arriving at the wall and were told there would be no passage. Some cursed the ferryman's daughter. Others sat beside the grounded punt and inspected its seams as though the danger had only become visible once it stopped moving.

Sena served the last bread with the broth. She did not reserve a place for Varn or the creditor, though both men received a portion when their turn came. Mara carried the damaged arch timber back to the cartwright's shed to shape a new brace. The apprentice followed with the cracked oar. The singer climbed to the wall every quarter hour and called across the river that the ferry was held until safe terms could be agreed, not lost a second time.

Jake stood beside the keel until the last light withdrew from the wet stones. He had heard the sixth week's results, including the 132.20–130.02 victory of the proposed Benedict Kenobi slot-one banner over Eggs Benedict. The historical sports sequence now read four defeats followed by two wins, **2–4**. It was a small, genuine turn in one kind of record. No one could use it to infer his real-world motives, or to settle a city's invented debts.

He remembered how the daughter had rejected his beautifully planed oar in the morning. He had been irritated by how easily she found a flaw in a thing he had worked to make right. Now its cracked handle lay inside the shed, and she had risked the anger of both banks rather than trust another clever repair. She had not taught him a lesson for his benefit; she had stopped a boat for her own reasons and would bear the names the city gave that refusal.

When he asked what he could do, she said, "Come early. Bring wood that hasn't already been promised to someone else."

He said he would try. She corrected him: "Find out who needs it first."

They left the punt on the stones. On the far bank a lamp moved from house to house as the stranded people made places for one another to sleep. A boy's white cloth hung by a window, no longer a signal for rescue, only a marker that someone was there. Jake could see it until full dark. He could not know what would be decided in the morning, or whether Varn would hold his ground against the creditor. But he knew the crossing had ceased to be something the city could claim existed merely because it needed it.

The oar had to fit the hand that would row.

**Historical handoff.** The Week 6 winners, by archival slot, are 1 Benedict Kenobi, 5 Benedict Coomhersnatch, 10 Mixon it up for a week, 11 Arm Chair GM, 4 The LLC, and 2 Red Leopards. All six 2023 fixtures cover twelve distinct team slots. The proposed Jake/slot-1 reading stands at **2–4** after consecutive archived wins, including Week 6 **132.20–130.02**, margin **2.18**, subject to unverified ownership. The concession argument, suspended crossings, weather, strangers, food, labor, negotiations and narrative choices are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not ESPN history. No Week 7+ competitive result or future season knowledge enters this installment.

# 2023 — Week 7: The Names on Both Banks
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six source-joined competitions · NONCANON historical fiction*

**Provenance and limits.** The six Week 7 scores, home/away labels, slot identifiers, and winners derive solely from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a` (Flaim / ESPN 1417621 historical extract). The original ESPN provider SHA is unavailable; historical human-owner joins remain unverified. Jake's proposed archival slot-one association is **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**, and is not a basis for attributing other teams to historical people. Home/away competition fields do not identify physical venues. Only Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`) receives limited interior viewpoint; the other named residents make externally witnessed choices, never independently narrated internal thoughts. Four distinct 2023 Benedict banners must not be collapsed into one owner. Two Dreaded Snake is a historical banner with no later morphology inferred. El Niño is an archival name, **not a speaking or human-shaped being**. Every river, creditor, charter, map feature, crowd, injury, office, act of rescue, meal and dialogue in the scenes below is **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not an authenticated historical transaction. No Week 8+ results enter this story's characters' knowledge. This companion Chronicle does not turn each weekly encounter into a mandatory novel chapter. Previously drafted 2023 Prologue/Weeks 1–6, the separate *Fourth Address* causal reading, and protected 2026 hard-canon Prologue and Chapters I–III are not modified.

The white cloth was still in the window when the city woke.

Jake had watched it through the final hour of the night, first as a pale mark against glass and then as a square without color. From the near bank he could not tell whether the boy who had waved it yesterday had slept, whether anyone had brought him food, or whether his grandmother on this side of the river had stopped listening for a boat. The punt lay above the high-water mark. A clean line of wet stone showed where it had been dragged.

The ferryman's daughter was at the landing before sunrise, sitting beside the split oar. She had not changed her mind.

The constable appeared with a fresh permit, ink still dark on the paper. She told him he could hang it on the punt if he liked. He would have to carry the boat himself.

He asked how the people across the river would know whether they should wait.

"Ask them," she said.

It was a cruelly simple answer to a question the city had made impossible. The river was not a border whose sides had ceased to belong together. It was a piece of the world the city had depended upon while refusing to account for the people who moved over it. Jake had spent six weeks helping put the wrong kind of answer on the wrong kind of page. Today he could not make the crossing safer with a sentence. He could at least try to find out whom his sentences had omitted.

He left the landing while the bell for the seventh scoring week's verdicts was being rung in the market. The daughter called after him that her three questions still stood. He raised one hand to show he had heard. It would have been easier to promise that they would be answered by evening.

He did not.

## I. Benedict Kenobi and Benedict Arnold — The Wood Already Promised

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; THE FICTION BELOW DOES NOT CLAIM ACTUAL CAUSATION OR OWNER IDENTITY.** **Benedict Kenobi (home), 124.53** versus **Benedict Arnold (away), 102.04**. Winner: **Benedict Kenobi (slot 1)**. Margin **22.49**.

Jake had slept with the wrong question under his pillow.

He had written it after midnight on the back of the creditor's order: How much timber will make the punt safe? The daughter had said she would not launch it until someone answered who decided the load, who repaired what broke, and who paid the people taking the risk. Jake's question reduced all three to an object that could be purchased. At dawn, when he unfolded the paper, he saw that he had left no room for the woman who had refused to row.

He reached the cartwright's yard before the forge was lit. Mara was already there with the apprentice and a saw laid across two trestles. A straight length of seasoned ash rested beside the wall. Jake put his hand on it, relieved to find something that had not been salvaged from a ruin.

"That belongs to the undertaker," Mara said.

"I haven't asked to take it."

"Your hand has."

The carpenter from the far quarter arrived carrying a burial cloth. His sister's husband had died during the previous cold spell. They had waited for an ordinary coffin because the dead man had repaired half the roofs of the street and no one would agree to bury him in a box made of green wood. The long plank was the only straight timber the carpenter had found. The crossing needed one as well.

Jake began to calculate the thickness they might cut away without weakening the coffin. It was the old habit of assuming that two needs could always be made compatible if a clever man spoke long enough. The carpenter watched him examine the wood and said nothing until Jake took out his ruler.

"Have you come to measure the man or the boat?"

"The boat."

"Then measure the boat. Leave the dead his length."

Jake put the ruler away. At the notice wall, the week's first competitive result had already been nailed up: Benedict Kenobi 124.53, Benedict Arnold 102.04. The slot-one banner had won a third straight contest after four defeats, bringing that archived team to **3–4**. The people reading the scores congratulated one another with the peculiar joy of belonging to a victory they had not themselves achieved. Jake felt the pull of it. There had been mornings when a tally determined how he stood among his friends. This morning, the number could give him neither a plank nor the right to borrow one.

Mara made him lift the damaged oar with her. Its crack ran farther than it had appeared under the lantern. The apprentice pressed a thumbnail into the wood and a sliver came off without resistance. Mara said a new oar would need a broad grain and time to season. The cheap substitute would not last through the next rise.

"Then we wait," Jake said.

"Some people have waited since yesterday," the apprentice answered.

They went to the daughter with their empty hands. She was sitting on the keel of the grounded punt, sharpening an iron hook that held one of the ropes. Jake told her about the plank without calling the refusal an obstacle. She asked how much wood remained in the yard, and Mara listed it piece by piece. There was a narrow length taken from a collapsed market stall. There were three short pieces of pine. There was a pole whose center had softened in the rain.

"None of those," the daughter said. "Not for an oar."

A pair of officials were coming down the road behind them, carrying a bundle of permits for a boat that would not move. Jake wanted to turn and argue. The daughter caught his sleeve.

"Bring me the people who have to use this boat," she said. "Not another man who can issue permission."

She did not intend a crowd. She wanted four working carriers, someone who depended on the evening flour, one person who had been stranded on the other bank, and the man who kept charging for a crossing that did not exist. She would not meet them in the creditor's office. If the boat was to become safe, its terms had to be spoken where the danger could be touched.

Jake had come with a question about wood. He went away carrying a list of people whose names he did not yet know. Before leaving, he looked toward the unused plank in Mara's yard. The coffin would be finished. The boat would remain grounded. He had never before allowed two incompatible needs to remain true without trying to erase one of them.

## II. The KING of the Octagon Village and The LLC — The Account of Empty Places

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; THE FICTION BELOW DOES NOT CLAIM ACTUAL CAUSATION OR OWNER IDENTITY.** **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 164.66** versus **The LLC (away), 104.30**. Winner: **The KING of the Octagon Village (slot 3)**. Margin **60.36**.

There were more chairs in the counting room than people in the meeting.

The city's managers had brought the chairs from their separate offices because none of them was willing to sit beneath another department's seal. Jake arrived to find six ledgers on the table and a space where the ferry account should have been. Varn occupied the last chair, the one nearest the door. The creditor's clerk stood at his shoulder with the silence of a man whose employer had told him to listen for anything that could be turned into a debt.

The clerk of stores read the week's shortages. Flour was stranded on the far bank, lamp oil on the near. A physician had crossed with a patient before the boat was grounded and could not return to her other cases. Two children who usually carried water for the kitchens were missing from their assignments because they were staying with relatives across the river. Someone had counted them as absences from work. Someone else had counted them as empty mouths.

Sena came in carrying no document. Her apron smelled of yesterday's burnt barley. When the clerk began to subtract the missing children from the list of people needing food, she said, "They haven't stopped being hungry because they aren't here."

"It is not our jurisdiction to supply the opposite bank."

"Then whose bread is this?"

She set a loaf on the table. It had been baked with grain brought from beyond the city, milled by workers on the other side, carried to her oven by the same road that the concession had blocked. The clerk replied that origin did not determine jurisdiction. Sena broke the loaf and placed the two halves on opposite sides of the empty ferry account.

"Now there's your jurisdiction."

The debate ought to have ended there, Jake thought, and knew that it would not. The chair of public works wanted a new boat built under the existing lease. The treasurer wanted no expenditures until the lender released its claim. The constable wanted order at the landing. Each request protected something real. Together they produced a crossing that could not function and no one who would answer for it.

Outside a herald called the second result: The KING of the Octagon Village had defeated The LLC, 164.66 to 104.30. People cheered at a decisive margin while the officials disputed whether the cost of waiting should be charged to transport, health, or relief. Jake watched the creditor's clerk copy a phrase from the minutes. The clerk did not look interested in the winners. He was interested in which office had admitted responsibility.

Varn spoke only when the others began assigning the daughter's work to the account named "voluntary service."

"She has refused," he said.

"Then replace her," said public works.

"With whom?"

The official glanced toward the door. "A paid crew."

"Paid from which account?"

No one answered. Jake had heard a dozen versions of this exchange in the past month, always ending with someone else's labor disguised as the absence of a cost. He stood and read the daughter's three questions exactly as she had spoken them. He did not add his own name or suggest that he represented her.

The treasurer asked for the document to be submitted formally. Sena turned the second half of her loaf so its torn face pointed toward him.

"Can you hear it without a paper?"

The official wrote a note to himself. It was a beginning, though Jake resented needing to call it one.

They agreed, by a narrow and awkward show of hands, to publish a count of people delayed on both banks. No department would be allowed to subtract those beyond its reach from the total. The clerk of stores opposed the motion because he could not verify the names. Sena offered to send kitchen workers door to door. The constable promised two runners. The treasurer attached the cost to a temporary line that could be erased before winter. Varn voted for it, knowing that his creditors might treat the new ledger as proof the city had assumed the liability he carried.

The vote bought neither boat nor bread. It changed what the city would have to admit was missing.

When the officials returned to their offices, the creditor's clerk remained beside the table. He took out a second sheet and copied the names of those who had voted. Jake did not challenge him. Recording a decision was not yet extortion. But he watched the pen move and understood how easily a city could make itself visible to someone prepared to own its debts.

Sena wrapped the divided loaf in her apron. She would take it back to the kitchen. One half was already stale. She said there were people who would eat it all the same.

## III. Arm Chair GM and Two Dreaded Snake — The Message That Crossed

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; THE FICTION BELOW DOES NOT CLAIM ACTUAL CAUSATION OR OWNER IDENTITY.** **Arm Chair GM (home), 145.76** versus **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 103.74**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **42.02**.

The first message from the opposite bank was not a plea for rescue. It was a complaint about the signals.

At daybreak the boy with the white cloth had found an elderly woman who could see the city's lamps but no longer distinguish their colors. He raised white for an injured person, as the ferryman had instructed the week before. On the near shore a watchman took it for a request to cross immediately. He sounded the warning bell, and fourteen people hurried to the landing. The punt was still on its stones. No one came.

By midmorning the people across the water had begun showing every scrap of pale cloth they owned. From Jake's place at the tower stair, their windows looked full of small flags. The city had given them a language with only one word in it.

The singer came up the tower carrying a slate. He had once directed crowds in the amphitheater by holding painted boards above his head. The work had seemed theatrical then, an agreeable craft for people who wished to be seen. He had brought those boards now, stripped of their old heroic verses, and offered them to the watchmen.

"Red, white, blue," said the constable. "Three conditions."

"Do they know what the colors mean?" the singer asked.

"We'll teach them."

"How?"

The constable looked across the water. The gap seemed suddenly larger than it had when they were only trying to cross it.

A herald began calling the third competitive verdict from the market below: Arm Chair GM 145.76 over Two Dreaded Snake 103.74. There was nothing uncertain about the archive's winner or the difference between the scores. The singers in the tower could repeat the result with perfect fidelity. They could not yet explain the meaning of a white cloth to a woman who could not see color, or determine which of the far-bank families had never heard the signals agreed upon on the near bank.

Jake suggested a pattern of lamps rather than colors. The watchman brought out three lanterns and placed them at different heights. Someone on the opposite bank answered with two lights in a window. Were they replying, or was that simply how their household had always been lit? Jake felt the temptation to announce agreement. He had spent too long among official tallies, where a response could be counted and called consent.

The singer put the lanterns out.

"Send words," he said.

The punt could not cross. A courier could travel the long road through the hills, but the watchman estimated that the journey would take most of the day. The route had not been certified safe for someone carrying supplies; it was an old road, not a bridge conjured out of an argument. Sena asked whether the courier could carry letters instead of food. The constable objected that letters would not feed anyone. Sena replied that people who could describe their needs might prevent food being sent to the wrong house.

The boy's grandmother was on the near bank. She had waited since the previous evening with a blanket over her knees and a basket of apples gone soft. The messenger proposed taking one of the apples and her name to the boy as proof he had reached the right people. She chose the least bruised fruit, then changed her mind and selected the worst.

"He'll know I did that," she said. "He always says I keep the good ones."

Jake wrote the message while she spoke. He left the grandmother's spelling unchanged. He had made enough small mistakes by tidying things into forms that others could read but their owners could no longer recognize.

The singer found two young couriers willing to take the longer road together. The constable demanded their names, the time of departure and the route they intended to use. This was reasonable, Jake thought, until the clerk asked for an estimate of the value of each letter. The singer closed the ledger with the flat of his hand.

"No value," he said. "Only the names."

The couriers left before noon. From the tower Jake watched them diminish along the road until the roofs hid them. Across the river the white flags remained in the windows. He could not tell the households that a message was on its way. He could only resist treating their silence as an answer.

The grandmother took her blanket to the wall and sat where she might see the first sign of returning feet. No one promised her the couriers would arrive before dark.

## IV. Mixon it up for a week and El Niño — Bread by the Long Road

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; THE FICTION BELOW DOES NOT CLAIM ACTUAL CAUSATION OR OWNER IDENTITY.** **Mixon it up for a week (home), 153.30** versus **El Niño (away), 155.16**. Winner: **El Niño (slot 9)**. Margin **1.86**.

In the granary, the grain sounded like rain.

Sena plunged her hand into a bin and listened to the kernels sift between her fingers. The sound did not reassure her. Grain made noise whether it was enough or not. She was looking for dampness along the wall, for the flour dust that meant rodents had found a way in, and for the scattered black husks that told her the upper sacks had been stored too long.

The storekeeper had prepared a count of barrels. Sena asked to see the contents. He said his figures had been certified. She pushed a barrel toward him and waited while he broke the seal.

The oats inside were wet.

A careless roof patch had let water reach the loft. They could save some if the grain was spread in the sun. The sky had clouded, and the courtyard had already been promised to the market sellers displaced by the toll. The storekeeper offered to write off the loss. Sena asked him whether a number could be fed to a child.

Jake came to the granary to carry the names gathered after the counting-room vote. They were arranged by shore. The near-bank sheet had forty-seven names and six unnamed people whose neighbors knew their needs but not their surnames. The far-bank sheet was almost empty. He had drawn lines below the few entries to make room for the couriers' return. The blank lines looked more honest than invented numbers and more frightening.

A herald passing the open door announced the week's closest result: El Niño 155.16 over Mixon it up for a week 153.30. The margin was **1.86**. Jake thought of how little difference a tally needed to produce a winner, while a storehouse could lose a season's worth of labor by an error no official wanted to name. He did not treat the archival El Niño banner as a person who had sent the clouds, or the competition as a cause of the spoiled grain. The sky had its own weather.

The storekeeper proposed that they keep the damaged oats for animal feed. Sena said some could be dried, but only if they acted before the evening mist. Mara brought tarpaulins from the cartwright's yard. They laid them between the granary and the hall steps, where children usually practiced festival dances. One of the dancers objected. The winter procession had been rehearsing there for weeks.

"We can move," she said after seeing the oats. "But tell them why."

Jake wrote a notice. The child took the chalk from him and added a drawing of a small grain sack with water leaking from its side. Other children came to help, repeating the story more accurately than the storekeeper's accounting figures had done. They marked which tarpaulin held oats, which held barley, and which must not be touched. Work became legible without becoming painless.

By afternoon the clouds broke just long enough for pale light to lie on the cloth. The storekeeper brought two apprentices and did the carrying himself. Sena directed which grain to save. The officials would later have to decide whether the damaged sacks were part of the concession's losses. No decision of that kind could undo the hours the children had given to turning damp grain under a clouded sky.

Jake wanted to leave the blank far-bank ledger at the kitchen in case the couriers returned. Sena would not take it.

"Keep it where everyone can see what's missing," she said.

He went back to the wall. He pinned the names on two separate sheets, with room for the unnamed and the absent. The near-bank list drew arguments at once. A baker demanded that the hospital receive grain before the households. A dock laborer asked who had decided which strangers counted as citizens. A woman with a newborn said the list had spelled her family name wrong. Jake changed the spelling.

At dusk a bell rang once from the long road. It was not the ferry's bell, and no one could tell which traveler had rung it. The watchman asked Jake whether to announce the couriers' arrival. Jake looked at the empty far-bank sheet and refused to turn hope into news.

## V. Red Leopards and Eggs Benedict — What the Singer Would Not Sing

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; THE FICTION BELOW DOES NOT CLAIM ACTUAL CAUSATION OR OWNER IDENTITY.** **Red Leopards (home), 131.56** versus **Eggs Benedict (away), 102.56**. Winner: **Red Leopards (slot 2)**. Margin **29.00**.

The singer would not perform the verse that morning.

It had been commissioned for the road's opening, a triumphant song about citizens joined by one bold work. At the time the singer had believed the refrain. Now the road ended at a sealed toll gate and the ferry lay broken beyond it. Children were still taught the words in the market school, because the song had become easier to preserve than the thing it celebrated.

The council had asked him to sing it at an evening gathering. They wanted to demonstrate that the city remained united despite the crossing's failure. The invitation called the gathering a ceremony of confidence. The singer read it aloud to Mara and laughed once, without humor.

"They want a bridge made of sound."

A printed table of the day's competitions hung beside the invitation. The fifth verdict read Red Leopards 131.56, Eggs Benedict 102.56, a margin of **29.00**. The crowd knew the two archival banners by their separate names. Jake could not infer the human owner of Eggs Benedict from any other 2023 banner containing the same word, any more than the singer could identify a family by the song it happened to know.

The singer offered a different performance: he would recite the names of those delayed on the two banks, pausing after each for someone who knew their circumstances. The treasurer objected. It would be impossible to check every account, and some of the names were only descriptions. A man listed as "father of the twins" could be three different people. The singer agreed. That was precisely why the names needed to be spoken before an audience that could correct them.

The constable feared disorder. Jake thought the fear justified. The crowd had been patient too long for patience to be a natural resource. He suggested that the recital begin with a statement of what the ledger could not prove. The singer looked at him as though he had come offering a clever permission slip again.

"Will you say it?" he asked.

Jake said he would.

The amphitheater no longer had its lower steps. The workers had taken boards from them to repair the crossing. People sat on broken stones and walls. Others stood in the street, holding bowls of Sena's thin supper. The treasurer arrived with the city's seal. Varn stood behind him without his coat of office. He had promised to attend because the concession depended on public goodwill, but he could not make himself an uninjured man by appearing in the crowd.

Jake opened the gathering with the admission that no one possessed a complete list of the people separated by the river. He told them the names had been assembled from kitchens, watch posts and household reports, and that none of those records made the people on the far bank less real. The public statement was short. It did not solve the problem of proof. It made the problem audible.

The singer began.

At the third name a woman raised her hand and said her uncle had died before the ferry broke. The singer crossed it out. At the sixth a boy shouted that his mother had returned by the hill road. The mother, standing at the edge of the gathering, answered that she had returned alone and her younger son remained behind. The singer divided one line into two. By the twelfth the clerk of stores was taking corrections faster than he could write them. Jake brought him another sheet.

A man in the upper gallery began chanting the old road song to drown out the names. Others joined him. Jake felt a familiar anger rise at the cheapness of that comfort. He wanted to shout them down. The singer stopped and waited. When the chant ended, he said that he knew the words as well as anyone and had written some of them. He had not come to forbid a song. He had come to keep its promises from being mistaken for completed work.

He did not ask the crowd to approve him. He read the next name.

At the end of the recital, there were seven descriptions without verified names, three entries moved to the wrong bank, one person reported missing who had died months before, and an unfamiliar surname in a hand no one recognized. The list was less neat and more useful than when they began. Sena took a copy to the kitchen. The constable took a copy to the gate. The creditor's clerk asked for a copy too. The singer gave him one.

Jake expected him to be angry. He merely folded it with care.

The gathering dispersed without the official song. A girl stayed behind to collect the scraps of the printed program. She asked the singer if the old refrain would ever be sung again. He told her he did not know. Then he asked whether she would help him carry the correction sheets to the wall before the wind lifted them away.

## VI. Benedict Coomhersnatch and The Blew Waffle's — The Terms Beyond the Water

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; THE FICTION BELOW DOES NOT CLAIM ACTUAL CAUSATION OR OWNER IDENTITY.** **Benedict Coomhersnatch (home), 104.76** versus **The Blew Waffle's (away), 156.18**. Winner: **The Blew Waffle's (slot 7)**. Margin **51.42**.

The young clerk returned from his employer's house with a proposal that carried no seal.

It would provide timber and wages for a new crew. The city would assign the punt, the temporary road and all public improvements along the approach as security. In return the lender would suspend its demand for immediate repayment. The clerk presented the terms in the granary because the counting room had refused to keep him a chair and the ferrywoman had refused to meet there.

Jake read the proposal once. The wages were real. So was the security. The city would be paying the people who rowed with an obligation attached to the places they had already saved by unpaid work.

The daughter stood beside the opened grain barrels. Her father had come with her, wearing his bandaged arm inside his coat. Mara and Sena sat on sacks. Varn remained near the door. They had not convened a tribunal, and the clerk had no authority to bind his employer to anything beyond the text he carried.

The final competitive verdict of the seventh week came from the street: The Blew Waffle's 156.18 over Benedict Coomhersnatch 104.76, a margin of **51.42**. The shout echoed through the open granary shutters. Jake heard in it the pleasure of an answer that did not require a committee. The people around the sacks were considering obligations that could not be resolved by counting points.

The daughter asked the clerk who would decide the load.

"The appointed master of the vessel."

"Who appoints him?"

"The concession."

"Who repairs what breaks?"

"The appointed crew, at the expense of the service."

"Who pays them if the service stops?"

The clerk searched the page. The wage clause applied only to days on which passage was made.

"Then it's the same boat with better ink," she said.

Varn asked whether she could make a different proposal. She answered that she had already made one, at the landing, in front of everyone who would bear the consequence. He admitted he had heard her. That was not the same as being able to obtain the money. His concession had lenders, workers and customers beyond the people in this room. He would not pretend he could erase their claims by wanting a good outcome.

Mara set the new oar blank on the floor. It had been cut from the sound half of a shed beam with the owner's permission. It was shorter than the daughter wanted. She would need to test it before anyone depended on it. The apprentice had spent the afternoon shaping the handle without planing away the grip. The daughter turned it in her palm and set it down without praising him.

"We can make that safer," she said. "We cannot make a bad contract safe by making a better oar."

The clerk looked at the sheet of names on the wall. He had copied it at the amphitheater, not for the first time that day. He pointed to the blank spaces beneath the far-bank heading. "You can't draw terms for people whose names you haven't confirmed."

"No," Sena said. "But you can stop pretending they aren't there."

Jake proposed that a second set of terms be written, not to settle the loan, but to define the temporary work. The daughter would choose safe loads; Mara would sign for repairs she had inspected; two people from each bank would count waiting households; and any crew member could stop work on a cracked hull or dangerous current without forfeiting wages already earned. None of the conditions would be presented as an Atlas law or a real historical institution. These were citizens bargaining inside a story, without any certified jurisdiction beyond the page.

"Two from the far bank?" the constable asked. "How will they agree?"

"The couriers," said the singer from the doorway. "If they get there."

The clerk did not accept the proposed terms. He agreed only to take them to his employer and return with an answer. Varn refused to sign the lender's draft before hearing that answer. It was the first time Jake had seen him decline a document without trying to replace it with a speech. The creditor's clerk left with both papers, one folded inside the other.

The daughter carried the short oar outside. She tested it in an empty rowing motion. Its blade would not reach deeply enough to turn the punt in a high current. She told Mara where it would have to be lengthened. Neither of them pretended it was ready.

Late in the evening the two couriers appeared on the long road, bent beneath their bags. They had not come across the river. They had walked around its upper bend and returned with mud on their hems and a letter from the grandmother's family. The boy had found shelter. His grandmother's apple had arrived; he had sent back the seeds in a small paper twist because he wanted to plant them when he came home.

The letter contained another list, made by the households who could not reach the city's counting room. Some names were wrong; some belonged to families the near-bank officials had forgotten; one belonged to a man who insisted that he owed money and did not want relief counted as a gift. The people across the river had written their own conditions for any resumption of the ferry. They wanted notice before each crossing, a place to shelter those denied passage, and a representative who could refuse a load when the waiting crowd became too great.

The daughter read every line. Then she put the letter beside her own three questions. The two banks did not yet agree, and the creditor had not withdrawn his claim. But there were now people on both sides whose wishes could be compared instead of presumed.

Jake carried the second list to the wall. He pinned it beside the first without joining them. In the distance, the singers' stage remained dark. No lamp on the far bank could yet tell him whether the city would honor what had been written.

He did not write that the crossing had reopened.

He wrote the date of the letter and the names of the people who had signed it.

---

## After the Seventh Reckoning — The Unfinished Count

At the wall the two lists moved in the night wind. One had been written on the near bank by clerks, kitchen workers and people willing to correct the city's mistakes. The other had come from the households beyond the water. The second was less orderly. Its spelling shifted from line to line. Beside one name there was the drawing of a boat and a question mark. Beside another, an account of how much grain had been consumed during the stoppage. The records did not match.

Jake was glad they did not.

He had spent the earlier weeks thinking that the greatest service he could offer was to make every entry consistent. Consistency could prevent an injustice when two officials named the same obligation differently. It could also conceal an injustice when the official version of a need was simpler than the human need itself. The daughter had made that danger visible by grounding a boat. Sena had made it visible with a divided loaf. The boy beyond the river had made it visible with an apple's seeds.

The record on the sporting wall was easier. Six seventh-week contests had taken place. Their archival winners, by slot, were **1 Benedict Kenobi**, **3 The KING of the Octagon Village**, **11 Arm Chair GM**, **9 El Niño**, **2 Red Leopards**, and **7 The Blew Waffle's**. The proposed Jake/slot-one team had beaten Benedict Arnold **124.53–102.04**, moving from four defeats and two wins to **3–4** after seven scoring weeks. That result did not document a real person rowing, bargaining, feeding strangers, or receiving a letter. It belonged to the game whose banners were posted above the wall.

Below, the daughter had left the short oar leaning against the stone. It was not ready. The lender's proposal had not been accepted. The households on opposite shores had not chosen their representatives. The treasurer still guarded his erased line of spending. The roads and buildings in this account had not been placed on an approved Atlas.

In the morning the city would have to decide whether to pay people for the time a dangerous boat could not safely move. It would have to decide whether a promise made on one bank could be negotiated from the other. It would have to decide what sort of authority was created when people wrote down what they needed and refused to let someone else make their absence a reason to disregard them.

For now there were two sheets of paper and no boat between them.

Jake took a stone from the path and set it at the foot of the lists so the lower corner would stop lifting. He left the names visible, including those he could not verify. From the opposite bank came a single light. He could not know what it meant. He did not answer it with a signal of his own.

The story ends after Week 7, with no future results available to its characters.
