# 2023 — Week 8: The Bell Without a Tongue
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six archival contests · substantive NONCANON historical fiction*

**Source and temporal boundary.** All six Week 8 competition results below come from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`, labeled Flaim / ESPN 1417621. The original ESPN provider SHA is unavailable. Historical human-owner joins remain unverified: Jake's proposed connection to archive slot 1 is **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. Home/away competition fields do not identify physical venues. The four distinct 2023 Benedict banners represent separate archive slots, not four authenticated people or one shared owner. The archival name Two Dreaded Snake is not proof of later character morphology. El Niño is not a speaking or human-shaped being; its 2023 league banner must not be mistaken for a person or evidence of historical weather. **Only Jake** (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`) receives **limited interior viewpoint**. Every settlement, river, fever ward, lender, creditor, road, debate, public office, instrument, wage, dialogue, and personal decision below is **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not verified ESPN history. No Week 9+ result enters the characters' knowledge. This is a companion Chronicle, not an automatic chapter of the 2026 novel. The protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III, the separate 2023 *Fourth Address*, and 2023 Prologue/Weeks 1–7 are unchanged.

The bell was older than the tower that held it.

Nobody had been able to agree when it was brought there. The tower keeper believed it came from a ship; the mason who repaired its stairs said it had belonged to the first market; the singer claimed there was a verse about it in a book whose spine had been burned away. Only one thing was certain. The bell could no longer sound.

Its tongue had been removed during the third week of repairs to the public hall and carried to the ironworks as collateral for an unpaid order. The market clerk had recorded it as a movable asset, though the people who used to hear it at midday had never thought of a bell as a thing that could move.

Jake climbed the tower on the morning of the eighth reckoning because the two sheets of names still hung near the landing. Rain had softened their lower corners. A great deal had been said about the people who could not cross the river, but neither bank had yet found a way to speak as one. The ferryman's daughter had refused to launch the punt. The creditors had offered conditional wages while retaining rights over the road. A messenger had brought words from the opposite shore, and words had come back with him. Nothing had been signed.

From the top of the tower Jake could see the abandoned theater roof, the observatory chimney, the little square of canvas over the old waiting bench. He could not see the opposite bank's kitchens. The river curved behind a row of roofs before its other side came into view. He had lately discovered that every vantage point was a way to mistake a portion of the city for all of it.

The keeper showed him the empty bracket where the tongue had swung.

"They still ring the small verdict bell," he said. "People like to hear who has won."

"Could this one be made to work?"

"If you bring back what belongs inside it."

Jake imagined a bell whose sound might reach both banks. He imagined the people hearing it at the same hour, understanding that they were not forgotten. Then he looked at the worn bracket and asked how much the tower keeper had been paid to keep the stairs safe.

"Enough to go up them," the keeper said. "Not enough to make anyone else come down."

Jake descended before the first contest scores were posted. He had mistaken sound for a message. The question was not how far the bell could be heard, but who would be allowed to decide when it rang.

## I. Benedict Kenobi and El Niño — The Cold Room

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Benedict Kenobi (home), 142.10** versus **El Niño (away), 205.16**. Winner: **El Niño (slot 9)**. Margin **63.06**.

The observatory had become a fever ward without anybody voting to make it one.

Its lenses were still bolted to their mounts, and star charts hung above narrow cots. Sena had put the sick beneath them because the observatory had the only high windows that opened on opposite walls. Its director had objected twice. On the third evening he brought blankets and a kettle, then stopped arguing about the building's official purpose.

Jake came with a sack of charcoal from a merchant who insisted it was a loan. He carried it up the steps while a woman beside the door counted the people seeking shelter. Four were from the city quarter, two from the farms beyond the toll gate, and one had no name she could give them. She wrote a line for that last person too.

The stove had a cracked door. Its hinge was bent outward and the latch could not close. Jake recognized the shape of the break: the saddler's sister across the river had been waiting for hinges of her own. The memory embarrassed him. He had congratulated himself when those hinges were carried beneath an old woman's coat, as if a single clever passage could repair every oven divided from its owner.

The observatory director brought a strip of tin. It was too short. Sena asked for wire; the storekeeper brought three twisted pieces that snapped when stretched. Finally Jake took the thin iron hook from his own satchel, one he had been using to fasten the papers he carried between meetings, and bent it around the hinge pin. It held the door shut. It would not last a winter.

The first verdict of the week arrived by a boy who had run until he could hardly speak. Benedict Kenobi had fallen to El Niño, **142.10–205.16**, a margin of **63.06**. The proposed slot-one record was now **3–5**. The point total was immense; it established a competition result, not a storm, sickness, misfortune, or reason for the ward's need.

Jake felt the old instinct to look for what he could have done differently. On a contest wall, a defeat that large demanded a story of mistakes. Here, though, the room's deficiencies had not appeared that morning. People had become ill over many days; the stove had rusted over years. No verdict could explain the neglect.

A child on a cot asked whether the bell in the tower was ringing. Jake said the larger bell had no tongue. The child accepted this with the grave practicality of someone who was accustomed to tools breaking. Then she pointed to the silvered observatory lens behind him.

"That shows far away things, doesn't it?"

"So I've been told."

"Can it show my mother?"

Jake followed her finger. The lens was turned toward a patch of sky above the river. The girl was from beyond the bank. She had come with the last overland courier and had grown too tired to return. Jake could make a dozen comforting promises, each one requiring a road, a boat, a doctor, or a message that no longer belonged to him.

He said, "I don't know where she is."

She looked back at the lens. "Then why keep it pointed at nothing?"

The director heard her. Without ceremony he loosened a clamp, lowered the great tube, and aimed it toward the distant fields. It was not powerful enough to find one woman among the roofs. The girl looked anyway, one eye closed, her fingers on the cold brass.

Jake stepped back from the lens. He had wanted a bell to speak for everybody. The girl wanted to look for someone in particular. He could carry charcoal and mend a latch; he could not substitute either for her mother's presence.

At the doorway Sena handed him a paper bearing the names of the people who needed medicine, including the blank line for the one who could not answer. She would not call it a census.

"Take it to the people selling remedies," she said. "Don't take it to the people selling speeches."

## II. Eggs Benedict and Two Dreaded Snake — The Remedy with a Seal

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Eggs Benedict (home), 177.38** versus **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 114.98**. Winner: **Eggs Benedict (slot 12)**. Margin **62.40**.

There were remedies in the apothecary's cabinet, but the cabinet had acquired a second lock.

The first key belonged to the apothecary. The other belonged to a collector who arrived with the lender's seal and an inventory of goods pledged against a delivery. The collector had not stolen the medicine. He had done something harder to argue against: he had bought the right to prevent its removal until a dispute over payment was settled.

The apothecary placed Sena's list on the counter and held his hand flat upon it.

"These are people," he said.

"These are entries," the collector replied, pointing at his own inventory. "I am here for the entries."

Jake asked to read the delivery contract. The collector pushed it across the counter with a finger on the bottom margin. The terms were written plainly enough. The supplier would send more medicine only if the apothecary accepted the disputed road toll as part of future transport charges. The river concession had reached inside a cabinet in the city.

A washerwoman waiting near the door said she had three coins and would pay for one packet now. The collector told her the sale was suspended. She put her coins down anyway, spaced neatly in a row. The apothecary did not touch them.

Outside, another verdict was being shouted: Eggs Benedict **177.38** had defeated Two Dreaded Snake **114.98**, by **62.40**. Those were separate 2023 archive banners, with no verified owners behind the historical names. The scores had no authority over the lock, the contract, or the woman standing in the shop.

Jake looked for a clause permitting emergency distribution. He found one allowing goods to be released if the supplier's agent considered them at immediate risk of spoilage. It was not the sort of rule that would save anyone by itself. He thought of calling the fever ward unheated, but that would be a lie: the stove now worked poorly, and the medicine's risk was not the same as the patients' risk.

He put the contract down.

"Will you tell the supplier they may spoil?" he asked the collector.

The man said, "Not without inspecting them."

"Then inspect them."

The collector took down his key. He opened the cabinet and counted each bottle while the apothecary watched. The washerwoman moved beside the door. With the cabinet open, Jake could see several packets whose labels were curling from damp. The collector lifted one, rubbed a corner of its wrapper, and left a gray smear on his thumb. He wrote the item number on a slip of paper.

The apothecary said that the damaged packets could still be used after inspection. The collector refused to decide. He could authorize movement only to the supplier's separate examiner, who was three hours away by the long road. His inspection did not make a person free to take the goods.

Jake did not ask the washerwoman to seize them. He could imagine the story being told afterward, an easy story of bold people who had broken a lock and saved the city. He could also see the apothecary's hands, marked by years of work, and the merchant whose next delivery might never arrive.

The washerwoman gathered her coins. "I'll take my washing to the ward," she said. "They have blankets no one will touch."

She left without asking permission. The apothecary folded Sena's list and gave it back to Jake.

"Bring me the name of the examiner," he said. "A person can refuse. A title can pretend no one heard."

Jake went to find the examiner. Behind him the collector locked the cabinet twice. One of the keys turned reluctantly; for a moment metal ground against metal, and the collector had to pull it out and try again. The medicines stayed where they were. The people who needed them were still waiting.

## III. The KING of the Octagon Village and The Blew Waffle's — A Place at the Table

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 114.40** versus **The Blew Waffle's (away), 100.33**. Winner: **The KING of the Octagon Village (slot 3)**. Margin **14.07**.

A meeting of the two shores had been announced in the public hall. Only one shore had been told where the public hall stood.

The notice named no time for the distant households. It instructed them to send appointed representatives across a crossing whose boat lay dry above the stones. The councilors had set benches in two facing rows and left a gap between them wide enough for a procession. At the far end stood a table, already occupied by men with keys.

Jake found the singer in the doorway, measuring the empty benches with a folded length of rope. The singer had refused the old triumphal verse the previous week. Now he was preparing to teach people a song in which the last word of each line could be changed without breaking the rhythm.

"It's not for a performance," he said when Jake asked. "It's so they will notice when someone leaves a person out."

The market clerk came down the aisle with a stack of printed voting cards. Each bore a seal on its upper half and a space for a mark below. There were enough cards for the registered city households; the names from the opposite bank had been filed as petitions, not voters. The clerk placed the cards neatly beside the keys.

A messenger arrived with the second bank's letter. He had walked the long way around the upper bend, and one boot was tied closed with string. The letter asked that no agreement concerning work on the river be concluded until those who would supply the labor had approved its terms. The meeting chairman thanked him and moved the document into the petition pile.

Jake asked where the petition pile would go when the meeting started.

"Into the record."

"And the voting cards?"

"Into the tally."

The distinction was neat enough to stand on its own legs. It was also the entire deception.

The small competition bell rang somewhere beyond the hall. The KING of the Octagon Village had beaten The Blew Waffle's, **114.40–100.33**, margin **14.07**. The result was certified in its own contest. It did not confer any literal crown, jurisdiction, real-world venue, or right to conduct the hall's meeting.

Jake laid the far-bank letter on an empty card so the words covered the official seal. The clerk reached to lift it. Jake kept one finger on the corner, then released it. He did not own the paper, and he had no right to appoint himself defender of those who had written it.

"Who may speak?" he asked.

"Registered participants."

"Who may register?"

"Those present."

The messenger, still standing in the aisle, said he could walk back with the answer. He asked whether a reply written in the clerk's hand would count as the bank's consent if no one could cross to object.

The chairman did not answer him. Instead he asked the constable to clear the aisle.

The singer climbed onto a broken bench and began his new song. Its first line named all the people who would have to carry wood, risk their hands and feed those left behind. When he reached the final word, the people in the hall supplied different endings. Their voices collided. A few laughed; others corrected the names. No one could pretend they had sung the same thing.

Jake felt a sharp wish to turn the confusion into a victory. He had helped carry one of the letters. He knew exactly what statement would sound generous from the chairman's chair. But the messenger was right: a speech made in a room that some people could not enter was not their vote.

The meeting adjourned without a tally. The chairman called the delay regrettable. The clerk collected the unused cards. The singer stayed to mend his rope.

The empty benches remained on the far side of the aisle. The gap between them was no longer a passage for dignitaries. It was the distance the meeting had failed to cross.

## IV. The LLC and Benedict Coomhersnatch — The Stone Under the Door

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **The LLC (home), 172.68** versus **Benedict Coomhersnatch (away), 148.16**. Winner: **The LLC (slot 4)**. Margin **24.52**.

Varn brought a stone to the counting house. It was the size of a loaf and streaked with red clay.

He placed it on the treasurer's desk, directly over the place where a line of public spending had been erased. The stone had come from the old landing. Its surface was worn flat by feet. Varn said the lender's agent wanted to know whether the whole landing could be listed as recoverable property in a new arrangement.

"Recoverable from whom?" the treasurer asked.

"From the city."

"By the creditor?"

"By anyone the creditor assigns."

The treasurer sat very still. When he finally moved, he took the stone off the sheet so the paper underneath could breathe. The erased line remained visible when the light struck sideways. It was a line for wages, never paid and never properly canceled.

Jake had come to ask after an examiner for the apothecary's medicine. The counting room had become a place where everybody arrived with one reason and left carrying three. He stood by the window with the names of the sick under his arm and listened as Varn repeated the lender's latest offer: timber and labor now, the old public landing pledged as security later.

A woman from the workshops asked to see the boundaries of the pledge. Varn showed her a diagram. It had been drawn with a ruler and marked with no known bearings. The blank space behind the landing might include the amphitheater steps that had already been used to build the punt. Or it might include the path to the ward. The paper gave no dimensions.

"Then this is not a boundary," she said. "It's a permission to draw one afterward."

Varn did not deny it.

Outside, the fourth archived verdict was posted: The LLC defeated Benedict Coomhersnatch **172.68–148.16**, margin **24.52**. The LLC's 2023 competitive banner and score did not establish a creditor, business transaction, negotiation, or named human proprietor in this invented city.

The treasurer opened a chest and lifted out two old instruments: a weighing chain and a plumb bob. He offered them to the workshop woman, saying she could measure the landing before any collateral paper was signed. Varn objected that the lender would not wait for a complete survey.

The workshop woman asked Jake to hold the chain's end. They went outside together. At the landing she made him stretch it between the worn stones. From there the chain ran beside the temporary path and stopped before the water. The stones were irregular; the next length would have to begin from a new mark. By dusk they had measured less than half the ground that the proposed pledge seemed to include.

Jake's hands had been rubbed raw. The work was humiliatingly slow compared with the arguments he had been making all week. He could not turn it into a parable about honest measurement. The woman asked him to carry the chain back. He obeyed.

In the counting room the lender's man was waiting. He wanted the corrected diagram before nightfall.

"Then you may help carry the instruments tomorrow," the workshop woman told him.

He said his business was to approve the terms, not to measure stones. She folded the unfinished paper and placed it under the red stone on the desk. Varn watched her do it.

Jake had wanted the old erased wage line restored. It still had not been restored. But nobody signed away the landing that day. The lender's man left without the diagram. The workshops would lose a day's paid work to finish the survey, and the opposite bank had not even been asked what the shore meant to its people.

At the door Jake remembered Sena's instructions: names, not speeches. He asked the treasurer for the examiner's address. The man knew only the supplier's office and wrote it down with a blunt pencil. It was enough to start another walk. It was not enough to open the cabinet.

## V. Benedict Arnold and Mixon it up for a week — The Road That Did Not Agree

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Benedict Arnold (home), 119.66** versus **Mixon it up for a week (away), 177.36**. Winner: **Mixon it up for a week (slot 10)**. Margin **57.70**.

By afternoon the courier's boots had failed altogether.

He sat on the market steps pulling the string from one sole. On his return journey to the distant bank he intended to take the upper road again. It passed fields, a mill with no turning wheel, and a shallow ford that was too uncertain for carts. He had traveled it with the letter; none of the route had been entered on an approved map of this proposed Commonwealth.

Jake asked whether the courier would carry a second letter, this one to the apothecary supplier's examiner. The man examined the address and said the office lay on his way only if the short footpath above the mill was open. He had heard a landslip had blocked it, but nobody knew if the report was true.

The road's uncertainty seemed to amuse the young errand boys gathered nearby. One offered to run ahead and mark the safer places with white chalk. Another brought two pieces of bread. The courier accepted the bread but refused the chalk scheme.

"Rain will wash it off," he said. "Someone will come afterward and follow what is left."

A girl from the far bank, temporarily staying with relatives in the town, took a slate from her bundle. She drew the fork beside the mill and added two marks: one for what she had walked herself, one for what she had only been told. The boys asked her what the marks meant. She explained and made them repeat it.

Jake found himself wanting to keep her slate. It was a better map than the creditor's perfect diagram, because it admitted uncertainty. Then she slipped it back under her arm and looked at him without offering it.

A shout from the verdict wall interrupted them. Benedict Arnold had lost to Mixon it up for a week, **119.66–177.36**, margin **57.70**. The names were archive team banners. Nothing in the result verified a road closure, courier, village, map, injury, or settlement, and no history of later team characters could be read backward into them.

The girl asked Jake whether the letter needed an answer before morning. He said he did not know. The supplier would have to decide whether the damp packets could be released; the apothecary would have to decide whether they were safe to use. The people in the ward might not be able to wait for either.

She opened her slate once more. This time she drew the long road, not the shortcut. The courier leaned across her shoulder and corrected a bend. Where he could not remember the land, he left the chalk untouched.

The singer came from the hall with his rope and watched the two make their imperfect route. He asked why they had drawn it if it could not tell every traveler where to go.

"So they know which parts we don't know," the girl said.

The singer repeated the answer aloud as if testing its rhythm. He did not make a song of it.

The courier bound his boots with wire from the market forge. The far-bank girl handed him her slate, then took it back and copied the route onto a scrap of cloth with charcoal. She kept the slate because it was hers. He accepted the cloth because it could be folded in a pocket.

Jake gave him the examiner's letter, sealed but unmarked by any city office. The courier agreed to deliver it if the long road remained passable. He did not promise the short route would exist when he reached it.

When the messenger left, Jake followed him as far as the edge of the market. He saw the wire beneath the soles begin to bite into the leather. The man stopped and retied it once, with deliberate patience. Then he walked on.

Jake returned to the old bell tower. He could hear the small contest bell from there. The larger instrument above him remained silent. He was beginning to think that what made a message trustworthy was not how loudly it was delivered, but whether the receiver had a way to answer it.

## VI. Red Leopards and Arm Chair GM — The Work That Could Be Refused

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Red Leopards (home), 136.12** versus **Arm Chair GM (away), 184.80**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **48.68**.

At sunset the ferryman's daughter entered the public hall carrying the broken oar.

The councilors had been waiting for her to accept a place at the long table. A notice had been prepared naming her representative of the crossing laborers. It offered her authority to recommend safety rules, subject to the lender's existing rights. Beside the notice lay a fresh permit for the punt. The permit was unsigned.

She laid the oar across two chairs. The split had widened since the sixth week. When she pressed her fingers on either side, the wood opened like a mouth with nothing to say.

"I have come to return something," she told the chairman.

He pointed to the empty seat.

She did not take it.

Jake stood near the wall, where the names from both shores had been copied onto separate sheets. He had carried the second bank's letter into this room before. Its words were present; its authors still were not. He watched the daughter lift the permit and read the provisions one at a time. The chairman spoke often while she was silent. She waited for him to finish before asking the first question.

"Who decides when the hull is sound?"

"The appointed inspector."

"Who pays for the timber?"

"The provisional fund."

"Who can refuse to row?"

"The permit holder, in extraordinary circumstances."

She placed the paper on the table. The chairman called this a reasonable concession. He seemed to expect relief.

"Is an ordinary day with a broken boat an extraordinary circumstance?" she asked.

The chairman said no answer could cover every possible situation. The daughter picked up the oar and walked toward the doors. The councilors began to protest. She stopped long enough to set the wood against the wall, so they could see the whole split without her hands hiding it.

Beyond the hall, the last archived result of the eighth scoring week was being read. Red Leopards **136.12** had lost to Arm Chair GM **184.80**, a margin of **48.68**. The competition verdict belonged to the scoring ledger. It did not identify any speaker in the room as an actual owner of those historical banners, nor did it authorize or condemn a single fictional political act.

Jake went after the daughter. She stood on the hall steps looking toward the landing. There were lamps in the near windows and a few beyond the bend, but he could not determine whether any message was meant for them.

"I thought you were going to make them promise wages," he said.

"I asked who could refuse."

"That will take longer."

She picked a splinter from the broken oar. "How many nights will it take to repair a person they decide may not refuse?"

He wanted to answer with the three proposed work terms from the week before: authority over loads, responsibility for repairs, compensation for the hands at risk. Those terms had seemed complete when written. She had shown him the part that the paper concealed. A person needed the freedom to say no even after a permit was issued and a wage promised.

The constable brought the daughter a second document. The council had changed one line. It now said the operator could halt passage whenever the operator judged the craft unsafe, without loss of the day's base compensation. It did not say where that compensation would come from. The fund was still provisional, and the lender retained the proposed rights over the landing.

She read the line twice, then handed the document back.

"Keep that sentence," she said. "Bring money that is not a claim on the shore."

The constable left. She returned to the landing alone, carrying the oar as if it were a witness that no clever speech could cross-examine. Jake remained on the steps. He had not won the argument. More importantly, he could no longer mistake the revision for a resolution. It was one useful sentence resting above an empty purse.

The great bell overhead did not ring. The daughter would not take the boat out, and those across the river would remain across it tonight. The city would have to decide whether it valued their passage enough to pay for it without turning the shore itself into collateral.

Jake went inside and copied the revised sentence beside the names from the far bank. He left a wide space underneath for the answer that had not arrived.

## After the Eighth Reckoning — The Sound That Had to Be Earned

The tower keeper found Jake after midnight, sitting on the stairs below the silent bell. He had come to close the upper door against the wind.

"The ironworks may return the tongue," the keeper said. "Someone has offered to settle the old order."

"Who?"

"I wasn't told."

Jake stood and went to look at the empty bracket once more. The bell could be made to sound. He knew it would be an impressive thing, a noise large enough to persuade the markets and lanes that a decision had occurred. He also knew how easily a city could turn noise into a substitute for an answer.

He asked the keeper not to hang it yet.

"That's not your bell," the keeper said.

"You're right."

He went down the stairs and left the question with the person who kept the tower. Outside the observatory window was lit. Somewhere within, the child might still be using the lens, searching for a mother beyond its power to distinguish. The medicine was still locked pending an examiner's judgment. The map of the uncertain overland road had gone with the courier. The lender's survey was incomplete. The ferrywoman had gained a sentence worth keeping and nothing that would make the punt safe by morning.

On the wall, the week's six verdicts were exact. They did not change in the dark: **El Niño (slot 9)**, **Eggs Benedict (slot 12)**, **The KING of the Octagon Village (slot 3)**, **The LLC (slot 4)**, **Mixon it up for a week (slot 10)**, and **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)** had won their respective contests. Benedict Kenobi's eighth-week loss, **142.10–205.16**, left the proposed slot-one lane at **3–5**. The contests were real archival records; the tower and all its people were invented.

The keeper barred the tower door and went home. Jake returned to the public hall, where the names from both banks were laid out beneath two unlit lamps. The right-hand sheet held the signatures of people who had consented to speak for themselves. The left-hand sheet held the words of those who had asked not to be spoken for until they could answer.

He did not combine them.

Instead he set the revised right-to-refuse clause between the sheets and pinned the blank space below it open with the stone from the landing. No signature appeared there by morning. An unsigned place was not yet a defeat. It was proof that the city had not made consent disappear by writing it as though it already existed.

**Historical handoff and HOLD.** All six Week 8 fixtures cover twelve distinct archival slots. The proposed Jake/slot-one lane is **3–5**, with the verified Week 8 defeat against the El Niño banner; owner linkage remains user-attested rather than authenticated. The grounded punt remains grounded; the debt, route, medicine, wages, two-bank representation, safety, geography, and all resident decisions are **NONCANON / PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. No Week 9+ results are available to these characters. No independent Author Council, Atlas, Umpire or Founder approval, merge or publication is implied.

# 2023 — Week 9: The Ash Beneath the Road
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six archived competitions · substantial NONCANON historical fiction*

**Provenance and authority.** Every competition result below is drawn from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`, labeled Flaim / ESPN league 1417621. The raw upstream ESPN provider SHA is unavailable. Human-owner assignment to the 2023 archival slots is **not provider-authenticated**. The candidate Jake / archive slot 1 connection is **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. Home/away competition fields do not identify physical venues. Four distinct Benedict banners in the 2023 archive must not be collapsed into one owner or assigned modern identities. The El Niño banner does not imply a human, an observed historical storm, or a speaking character. The sole proposed principal interior viewpoint is **Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`)**, a fictional literary persona; all other people remain externally observed. Every ward, foundry, road, river, moneylender, craft, building, civil decision, event, dialogue, motive and weather condition in this installment is **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not fantasy-league history. No Week 10+ result enters the characters' knowledge. This companion Chronicle does not overwrite the approved 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III or promote itself to canon.

The heat failed on the night the warehouse keeper tried to make it a private matter.

It was the least impressive failure anyone in the city could remember. No tower fell. No ship was driven against a bank. At the observatory ward, where the windows had been opened for the sick and the stove had been coaxed into keeping its door shut, the fire simply went out. A cinder dropped through the grate and went black. Someone fed the iron box another sliver of wood, and a vein of red appeared under the ash, too weak to hold.

Sena told the people on the cots that the fire would be restored. She said it in the tone she used for things she could make happen with her own hands. Then she went into the courtyard, where Jake was waiting with the supplier's unanswered letter, and asked him how many coins he had.

He had five. Three were for the long-road courier, one was borrowed from the market kitchen, and the fifth had been hidden in a seam of his cloak since the autumn's first public contest. It was a pitiful sum beside the rival figures being carried daily to the contest wall. Yet Sena did not ask for a figure; she asked to see the actual coins.

She turned one over. Its face was worn smooth. "This buys kindling," she said. "Not another week of fuel."

He asked about the apothecary. She said the supplier's examiner had still not come. The damaged medicine remained behind two locks. The ferryman's daughter had not moved her grounded punt, because no safety test or wage fund had been agreed. A distant bank still had no seat in the council that wanted to decide the work of its inhabitants.

"I know," Jake said.

"Knowing is the part you do when you cannot leave the room," Sena answered. "Are you staying?"

He looked toward the hill. Lights trembled in the windows of the counting house, where people were considering a pledge of the landing stones to secure a shipment. He had spent days describing the absurdity of that pledge. The argument had not kept anyone warm.

Inside the ward a girl who had once asked to see her mother through the observatory lens was standing by the cold stove with both palms pressed together. She was not praying. She had been taught that a flame could sometimes be persuaded to rise if a person waited patiently enough.

Jake put the five coins back in Sena's hand, keeping none for the courier. Then he asked the girl whether she would show him the ash bucket. She took him to the back stair, and there he discovered that the keeper had been saving the unburned pieces in the ashes instead of throwing them away. It was not a miracle. It was enough to start a second fire.

The contest bells had not yet rung. By morning they would ring for six decisions, and the decisions would be final within their own narrow authority. The city had many instruments for declaring an end. It had almost none for keeping a beginning alive.

## I. The LLC and Two Dreaded Snake — A Measure Too Small to See

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **The LLC (home), 136.16** versus **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 135.78**. Winner: **The LLC (slot 4)**. Margin **0.38**.

The warehouse had enough coal to feed the observatory stove for three nights. The keeper called it an emergency reserve and would not say whose emergency it belonged to.

Jake came with Sena's bucket and the two young apprentices who had volunteered to carry sacks. The keeper led them down a stair so narrow that the apprentices had to turn sideways. At the bottom, black dust coated the stone like the residue of a vanished tide. A wooden tally hung from a hook. There were thirty-seven notches, one for each sealed sack, though Jake could see only thirty-five.

Two of the sacks were already open.

The keeper said he had opened them for inspection. One apprentice found a fresh spill beside the eastern wall, where coal dust had collected beneath a hinge. He brushed the dust aside and exposed the mark of a wheel. Someone had rolled a loaded barrow out of the room after the bags were counted.

"Not a theft," the keeper said quickly. "A transfer."

"To whom?" Jake asked.

The keeper took the tally off its hook. He was a small man with the flushed face of someone unused to cold air. He made a show of checking each notch before replying.

"The counting house. There is a lien against the river inventory, and a fire must be kept in the rooms where the books are stored. Paper is worth very little after it freezes and splits."

The apprentice nearest the stair gave a short, incredulous laugh. The keeper stiffened. Jake had expected an easy outrage, a sharp answer about children shivering while ledgers stayed warm. But there were people working late in the counting house, too. The public record of an unpaid wage could be destroyed as thoroughly as a sick child's bedding.

From the alley a herald called the first result. The LLC had prevailed over Two Dreaded Snake **136.16–135.78**, a difference of **0.38**. It was the smallest sort of competitive victory, an outcome decided by less than a single point. No one at the warehouse had earned coal, legal title, creditor authority, or public trust by association with either archive banner. The owner identities behind those 2023 names remained unknown.

The keeper seized on the sound of the herald as if it might settle the room's argument.

"An exact reckoning," he said. "The difference always appears when one counts properly."

Jake lifted a handful of coal dust from the floor. "Can you tell me whose fire this belongs to?"

The keeper looked at his palm without touching it. The weighing apparatus was upstairs, he said; the dust was not a whole unit; no record would accept it. He was right about the record. He was also refusing to see the reason the dust had fallen there.

One apprentice, Ledo, pointed out that the wheels had left the warehouse twice that morning. The other, a girl with soot on her collar, said a cart had gone toward the ward, but not from this alley. She might have been mistaken; the city had half a dozen carts and only two serviceable horses. Jake made them repeat what they knew separately. He would not join their uncertain observations into an accusation merely because it was easier to be angry at a keeper.

The keeper, however, had begun to understand that the missing sacks would be noticed. He offered to open a third sack for the ward if Jake would agree not to mention the wheel mark. The offer was made quietly. It would have cost the keeper very little.

Jake felt the lure of it: warm the ward tonight, let the small dishonesty go, live to argue about better rules tomorrow. He also knew that the keeper had offered someone else the same bargain before. The coal was not his to trade for silence.

He set down the bucket. "I will not accuse you of stealing what I cannot prove you stole," he said. "And I won't promise silence about what we found. If you send the ward one sack, put the transfer on that tally."

The keeper stood with the plank in his hands. At last he scored a new mark beside the thirty-seventh notch, wrote 'ward' beneath it, and sent Ledo upstairs to borrow the scale. It was a poor bargain for a man who wanted his accounts to appear immaculate. It was a small, usable beginning for everyone else.

They carried less fuel than they needed. At the ward the girl by the stove asked whether the coal was enough.

Jake watched Ledo shake a last black pebble from the sack. "For tonight," he said, and could hear that the answer was both true and insufficient.

## II. Arm Chair GM and Benedict Kenobi — The Room That Could Not Be Won

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Arm Chair GM (home), 144.12** versus **Benedict Kenobi (away), 137.40**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **6.72**.

The examiner arrived on foot, with one good boot, one boot repaired at the heel, and no medicine.

He had been delayed two days at a collapsed culvert above the upper road. The bridge had not fallen; a stone beneath the approach had shifted, leaving a gap wide enough to trap a wheel. He had helped the courier drag a cart around it and walked the rest of the way. When Jake found him at the apothecary's door, he was asleep sitting upright, with his satchel held between his knees.

The collector with the second key refused to wake him until the proper hour. The apothecary ignored the rule. He bent over, took the man's shoulders, and said, "There are people waiting."

The examiner opened his eyes and asked for water. He wanted to see every damaged packet, the original packing list, and the reason that a sealed inventory had been exposed to damp air. The collector objected to the word 'damaged' before anything had been examined. The washerwoman, who had left three coins on the counter the previous week, arrived with a basket of freshly boiled linen. She set it down without asking a question.

Jake had brought Sena's list of patients, but he did not hold it out. Names could give urgency; they could not establish whether a crumbling wrapper held a safe remedy. His own desire to make the examiner hurry frightened him. He knew nothing of the packets' preparation, and he had watched too many people call a difficult judgment cowardice because it prevented a satisfying ending.

The examiner unwrapped one packet, inspected the seal within it, and smelled the powder. Then he did the same with another. He asked the apothecary to bring a basin and warm water. Nothing happened for a moment. The apothecary had lent the basin to the ward.

The second result came by the open door: Arm Chair GM defeated Benedict Kenobi **144.12–137.40**, margin **6.72**. The candidate slot-one team, associated with Jake only through user attestation, had suffered another defeat and stood at **3–6** after nine scoring weeks. That was the complete competitive consequence established by this encounter. It did not prove that Jake misjudged medicine, chose wrong about a basin, or caused anyone to become ill.

He had once treated a loss as a demand to inspect himself for the precise flaw that produced it. The instinct had not left him. But the examiner was now making decisions whose consequences would be borne by people who were neither winners nor losers of the contest. Jake could not audit those decisions into innocence.

The examiner judged two packets safe for limited use after their inner seals held. A third packet had taken water through a seam; he condemned it. The collector protested that condemned goods would be charged against the apothecary's inventory. The examiner wrote his finding anyway.

"How long will the sound packets last?" Jake asked.

The apothecary named the people likely to need them first. He did not give Jake the answer in days. He asked the washerwoman to carry one packet to Sena, and the collector stepped into the door before she could leave.

"Who bears the charge?"

"I do," the apothecary said.

"You haven't the credit."

"Then write that I haven't it."

The collector looked from one face to another. There were seven people in the shop, and none had begun to shout. He was not made into a villain by the keys in his hand. He was a man who had chosen to turn responsibility into a question another office must answer.

Jake started to speak, then stopped. The washerwoman had already lifted the basket, folded the packet into the dry linen, and taken two steps toward the door. She looked at the collector. "Is it locked?" she asked.

He moved aside.

The packet reached the observatory before sunset. The examiner's finding did not unlock the rest of the cabinet, erase the supplier's toll claim, or end the ward's fever. Nor did Jake write a celebration about it. He sat with the apothecary while the man cleaned the table, and discovered that the worst noise in the room was the soft rasp of a knife scraping spoiled powder into a waste jar.

"What shall we do next?" Jake asked.

"Wash the knife," the apothecary said. "Then count what is left."

## III. Red Leopards and The Blew Waffle's — A Song with No Hero

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Red Leopards (home), 84.64** versus **The Blew Waffle's (away), 146.73**. Winner: **The Blew Waffle's (slot 7)**. Margin **62.09**.

By the third reckoning, the market kitchens were boiling water in cauldrons too large for their hearths. They had lent pans and ladles to the observatory and were trying to serve the workers on the upper road with what remained. A boy had been given the task of keeping two tall pots from tipping. He stood between them, one hand on each handle, and looked as though he had been sentenced to hold the world together.

The singer came to the square carrying the old rope from the council hall. He had stitched colored thread into it wherever a name had been omitted from the failed meeting. He wanted to arrange a public meal for people whose work had been demanded without compensation. The kitchen master agreed, provided no one called it charity. It was a meal for labor already done.

But there was little grain.

A merchant offered six sacks if the singer would perform a triumphal ballad for the lender's new river agreement. The merchant owned the sacks, not the agreement, and insisted that the bargain was simply commercial. A song would help the council accept a difficult compromise; the flour would help families accept a difficult week. He spoke as though each kindness canceled the cost of the other.

Jake was asked to witness the terms. He had refused the keeper's bargain for silence at the warehouse, but this was different: the singer could choose the words, and six sacks would feed the ward and the far-bank runners. Was it so wrong to lend a song for a night when the alternatives were empty bowls?

The singer listened, then asked who would be named in the verse. The merchant said the city, the lender, the builders, and the charter. Not the ferryman's daughter, whose labor had become the center of the dispute. Not the people who had refused to sign from the other bank. Not the woman who had surrendered her basin to the sick. Those names, the merchant said, would make the song political.

"The charter is political," the singer said.

"I am paying for a celebration."

"You are paying for a story in which there was no disagreement."

The third verdict passed through the crowd. The Blew Waffle's defeated Red Leopards **146.73–84.64**, margin **62.09**. The difference was decisive as a contest score; it did not say that either historical owner had fed anyone, sponsored a singer, been humiliated, or entered the market square. Jake heard the numbers while the kitchen boy struggled to keep his two pots upright.

One of the pots slipped.

The singer dropped the rope and caught its handle before the boiling water could spill across the boy's legs. Steam rose around them. The kitchen master shouted for everyone to move back, then discovered his own hands were shaking. Half a pot of water had escaped onto the stones. No one was injured, but the boy began to cry with a sudden and private grief, as if he had failed at something immense.

The singer took him behind the stall. When he returned, he told the merchant he would sing one verse for every sack, but he would choose the verses. The merchant would be free not to pay. The audience would be free to leave.

The merchant scowled and said no patron could guarantee payment for such a performance. He offered two sacks as an advance without demanding a particular song. Jake suspected he wanted the appearance of generosity after the near scalding; he did not know. The singer took the two sacks. The other four remained on the wagon.

The music began in an unexpected register, low enough that people had to approach to hear. It was not about the famous victory or the defeat that had just been posted. It named the things that had failed: the cart axle, the ward stove, the council chair, the far-bank message, the safe oar. Then it named the hands that kept working after each failure.

Jake listened from a place outside the circle. He could not tell whether the song made any difference to the city's decisions. He could see that the boy who had nearly been burned was eating bread in the kitchen master's lap, and that a few people who had thought themselves forgotten were singing the names beside their own.

When the performance ended, the merchant ordered his wagon away. The singer did not chase it. Jake helped carry the two sacks inside. He had no phrase to make their scarcity seem noble.

## IV. Benedict Arnold and The KING of the Octagon Village — Iron for a Different Use

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Benedict Arnold (home), 72.10** versus **The KING of the Octagon Village (away), 139.12**. Winner: **The KING of the Octagon Village (slot 3)**. Margin **67.02**.

The ironworks could make the great bell speak again. That was the answer the tower keeper had sent Jake, written in a hand that betrayed more hope than its author meant to admit.

A new tongue would cost iron, coal and an afternoon of the master smith's labor. Its metal could be borrowed from the broken wagon wheels accumulating beside the foundry, though the smith would need to know which wheels belonged to the city and which to people awaiting repairs. The tower's stairs were sound enough to carry a worker if the wind was mild.

Jake took the message to the foundry because the ward had asked for a new stove hinge. The broken hinge still held under the hook bent from his satchel; each time the stove was opened the hook grew thinner. A second hinge would keep the door shut. It would not make the fuel last longer.

At the foundry he found Mara, the cartwright, with her left sleeve rolled above the elbow and a welt running across her wrist. A cold rivet had slipped while she braced a wheel. She was irritated at the injury, but more irritated that everyone asked her to display it as proof the workshop was dangerous.

The smith said there was enough iron for one casting that day.

"Then the hinge," Jake answered.

"The bell would give warning," the tower keeper said.

"Warning of what?"

"Of the ward running cold. Of boats coming back. Of meetings."

"The ward needs to be warm before we warn it."

Mara took the written request and read both sides. The keeper had not argued that the bell mattered more than the sick. He had argued that a signal reaching two banks could call people to help when another fire failed. It was not a frivolous idea. The cost of the casting was the same iron that could keep one stove usable, and no one had agreed who should decide.

The fourth result came from the square: The KING of the Octagon Village had beaten Benedict Arnold **139.12–72.10**, margin **67.02**. It was the largest margin of the six archived Week 9 contests, but it created no literal ruler, judicial decree, inherited title, or evidence of a historical person. Here the only 'kingdom' Jake could see was the corner of the workshop belonging to the tools that still worked.

Mara asked the smith for the old wagon hub instead of a whole wheel. She turned it over in her hands. The hub had cracked along one rib and would never carry a heavy cart again, but its iron was good enough for the hinge. She proposed to cut two pieces from it and save the rest. Then she pointed at a pile of worn horseshoes.

"That metal is harder," she said. "It may make a tongue that sounds wrong."

"How wrong?" the keeper asked.

"We'd have to hear it."

The smith refused to run both castings in one heat. Too much work, too much uncertainty. Mara asked Jake to stay and work the hand bellows while she forged the hinge cold over a form. She could make it without a casting if the metal cooperated. The smith watched, ready to stop her if the work cracked.

Jake took the bellows. His arms ached long before the first piece bent. The hinge was ugly: a small black loop whose two ends did not quite meet. Mara drove a pin through it, tested the motion, and declared it fit for a stove door. The smith did not offer praise. He put the horseshoes aside for the tower keeper.

At the observatory the hinge held. Sena opened and closed the stove three times, then thanked Mara rather than Jake. The distinction was obvious; Jake was nevertheless surprised by the size of his relief.

The large bell remained mute. There might be a way to make it sound, but it had not yet been made. On the stairs the tower keeper tried to hum the note he remembered. He could not find it. He laughed, embarrassed, and said perhaps he had imagined how beautiful the bell had been.

Jake said that someone should tell him when Mara made the next attempt. Then he went to fetch more wood, because the hinge could only hold a fire that someone fed.

## V. Mixon it up for a week and Benedict Coomhersnatch — The Far Bank Answers

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Mixon it up for a week (home), 127.52** versus **Benedict Coomhersnatch (away), 145.22**. Winner: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (slot 5)**. Margin **17.70**.

The far-bank courier returned without the signatures that the city council had requested. He brought three things instead: a heel of dark bread wrapped in linen, a small wooden paddle marked with two cuts, and a piece of cloth from a shawl.

The bread had been sent by a household that had run out of flour but wanted the people in the ward to know they were eating. The paddle was a pattern for the cross-brace the ferrywoman had refused to trust; the two cuts marked places where the wood had split during the last attempt to test it. The cloth belonged to the girl's mother.

Jake saw the shawl before the courier explained. It was blue at the edge and gray toward the middle, with a line of small stitches where someone had repaired a tear. He could not know whether it belonged to the woman the child had asked about; the child had never described the shawl. He wanted desperately to make it hers.

The courier had traveled through the upper bend with two laborers and a cart of empty jars. They had been stopped at the toll station, though the goods were not intended for sale. The keeper of the station had let them pass when one laborer volunteered to repair a broken gate. The repair took the better part of a morning.

"Did they agree to the council's plan?" Jake asked.

"They agreed we should bring you these."

"Not the same thing."

"No." The courier's voice was hoarse. "Nor did they refuse to help."

The fifth verdict was posted as they stood beside the public waiting bench: Benedict Coomhersnatch defeated Mixon it up for a week **145.22–127.52**, a margin of **17.70**. Each name belonged to a separate archived slot. The result did not prove any human lineage, bargain, courier journey or vote. The matching of banners to fictional disputes would remain a literary interpretation, not an archival join.

Jake took the paddle to Mara at the foundry. She studied the two cuts, then made the courier explain how the far-bank workers had measured the flex. They had wedged one end beneath a stone, hung water pails from the other, and stopped when the grain began to open. The test was rough but it was more than a declaration of confidence.

"Can this make our punt safe?" Jake asked.

"Not alone," Mara said. "It tells us where one piece failed."

She asked the courier whether the people across the river would send someone to help test a replacement. The courier said they would, if no one attempted to treat their labor as evidence of consent to the debt. Mara nodded. The ferrywoman, who had entered behind Jake, said she would attend the test but not launch the boat. Everyone in the room understood that her refusal still mattered.

The cloth went to the ward, where Sena asked the girl to identify it herself. Jake kept silent while the child pressed the hem between her fingers.

"This is her stitch," she said, pointing to the uneven repair.

Sena did not ask Jake to certify what the child knew. She asked the courier whether he had seen the mother. He had. She was alive, working in a kitchen beyond the far fields, unable to make the long journey because someone had to tend an older relative. She had sent the cloth to prove she had not forgotten.

The girl held it against her cheek. She did not smile. She asked why her mother could send a piece of her shawl but not herself. Jake stood there while Sena answered that the journey was long and the people were not free to cross. It was a harder truth than his earlier offers of comfort and the first one the child could hold beside something she recognized.

Later the courier opened the bread. He broke it into four pieces and offered one to the ferrywoman. She shook her head and asked that it be taken to the kitchen workers. Jake thought she was refusing a gesture from the opposite bank, then noticed she had brought her own crust in a pocket. She was eating while she worked; she did not want the first new food to be given to a person who already had some.

He went with the courier to the kitchen. The far bank had not surrendered its voice. It had sent a damaged tool, a sign of life, and food it could barely spare. That seemed a more serious beginning than the council's demand for signatures.

## VI. El Niño and Eggs Benedict — The Weight of an Unlit Window

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **El Niño (home), 152.88** versus **Eggs Benedict (away), 150.34**. Winner: **El Niño (slot 9)**. Margin **2.54**.

That night the ward's windows were dark. The stove worked, the fire was fed, the medicines from the examined packets had been carried inside, and Sena had asked the workers to cover the glass with cloth to preserve warmth. Anyone looking from the far bank might have mistaken the absence of light for abandonment.

The courier said so. He had been asked to report whether the people at the ward were still there, and the windows were the only reliable marker from the upper road. Jake suggested that someone light a lamp in the tower. The tower keeper, tired of standing beside his silent bell, offered the lantern he used to check the stairs.

But the lantern could not be seen around the river bend from every approach. They would need another light farther upstream, and a person to tend it. A signal was not a structure of one brilliant object; it was a chain of people who could afford to stay awake.

The sixth and final verdict was shouted beneath the hill: El Niño defeated Eggs Benedict **152.88–150.34**, margin **2.54**. In this archive El Niño is a **competition banner only**, not a person, storm, prophetic force or observed cause of any fictional weather. The verdict settled a close contest. It did not tell anyone whether the ward's lanterns should be lit or whose households would remain visible after dark.

Jake took the tower keeper's lantern and walked with the courier along the upper road, stopping where the roofs concealed the ward. At the first rise they found a widow closing the shutters of her small house. She said she could keep a candle in her front window for an hour after sunset, if the oil was supplied. Beyond her home a cobbler offered his workshop lamp, provided no one demanded he leave it burning through the night. A boy said he could carry messages between the two, and his father called him back.

It was a practical exchange, not a council action. No votes, no seals. Each person named a limit to what they could do. Jake wrote those limits on the back of an old contest notice. When the boy's father saw him writing, he said, "Don't make that into a promise for all of us."

"I won't," Jake answered.

They tried the lights from the far curve. The widow's candle vanished behind the roof, the cobbler's lamp was visible for a moment, and the tower lantern glowed against the stone like a tiny red eye. The courier stood beside Jake and said the far-bank night runners would have to choose their own stations. Jake had wanted the city to provide a single answer that traveled everywhere. It was becoming clear that no answer traveled without somebody carrying it.

At the ward Sena came out with the washed basin and asked why he had returned so late. He told her about the lights. She listened, then asked whose oil would burn when the donated supply ran out. He showed her the limits written on the old paper. She did not applaud him. She pointed to the girl sitting at the doorway, wrapped in her mother's cloth.

"She can decide when our lantern is wanted," Sena said. "She is the one who watches for the road."

Jake went to the girl. He asked whether she would like a lamp at the window or a dark room to sleep in. She considered it solemnly. Then she pointed toward the courtyard, not the road.

"Could you light it where they are carrying water?" she asked.

He had been arranging a sign for distant observers. She wanted the people nearby to see their footing. He moved the lamp.

When he came back, the courier was helping Mara and the ferrywoman carry the empty medicine jars to the kitchen. No one had repaired the punt. No one had signed the concession. The far-bank representatives had not approved a new council. The apothecary still owed for medicine that had already been used, the coal reserve remained disputed, and the city's labor accounts had no secure fund.

Yet a hinge held, two packets had been opened under examination, a child had heard news from her mother, and workers from opposite shores had agreed to test a plank before trusting a river. None was a solution. Each would make the next refusal or promise more difficult to ignore.

### After the Ninth Reckoning

In the morning the market clerk arrived with a proposal that every light be entered in the municipal inventory. He had drawn columns for duration, owner, maintenance charge and range of visibility. He said the city should be able to defend its signal system against claims by the lender.

Jake read the columns carefully. For once, he did not want to tear up a form. The widow needed oil; the cobbler needed permission to sleep; a tower keeper could not climb unsafe stairs forever. What offended him was the clerk's blank line for "purpose." The word had been left empty because the clerk expected one purpose to be declared on behalf of everyone.

Jake showed him the paper with the neighbors' limits. The clerk frowned at the boy's father's refusal and the girl's request for a lamp in the yard.

"These are incompatible," he said.

"Yes."

"We shall have to settle them."

"Some of them."

The clerk asked what to put in the purpose column. Jake could have said safety, representation, commerce, the public good. Each would have been defensible and each too large. He went inside the ward and found the girl feeding a last sliver of wood into the stove. Sena stopped her before she burned her fingers, and she stepped back without resentment.

Jake returned to the clerk.

"Write what each light is for," he said. "And where it fails."

The clerk made room for a second column. He worked quietly for a while. Jake watched, then left him to it. There were things to do in the city that did not become more important merely because he was watching them.

By the river the grounded punt lay above the stones. Mara and the ferrywoman were examining the far bank's marked paddle, testing its failure with a bucket of water rather than a person's weight. The courier had gone to find a carpenter. No passage was authorized. The debt remained. On the far bank, beyond the bend where the city could not see, someone might be preparing an answer of their own.

The ninth reckoning had ended. Work had not.

---

The proposed Jake/slot 1 link remains user-attested, not provider-authenticated. No private real-world owner motives or 2026 character morphologies are projected backward. All future events, including Week 10 results, are outside the characters' knowledge. Independent Authors Council, Atlas, Umpire and Founder canon/publication approvals have not been obtained.
