# 2023 — Week 10: The Timber That Did Not Float
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six archived competitions · substantial NONCANON historical fiction*

**Provenance and authority.** The six competitive records in this installment reproduce Week 10 of `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`, labeled Flaim / ESPN league 1417621. The original upstream provider SHA is unavailable. Historical team/owner joins are **UNVERIFIED**, except that the proposed Jake / slot-one literary connection is **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. Four distinct Benedict banners are not a single verified human lineage. The El Niño banner is an archived competitor label, never a speaking storm or a human figure. Home/away competition fields do not identify physical venues. The **sole proposed principal interior viewpoint is Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`)**; the other historical opponents are names on public competitive records, not presumed identities or private motives. Every road, river, person, ward, foundry, contract, snowless frost, letter, illness, tool, speech, and political decision portrayed below is **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. It is not evidence of a historical league event. The story knows no Week 11+ outcome, does not change the approved 2026 Prologue or Chapters I–III, and remains **NONCANON / EDITORIAL HOLD**.

The first plank to break did so without anybody standing on it.

Mara had set a newly shaved length of timber across two blocks behind the foundry, not over water but over a shallow trough that still held the rain of last week. A stone weight hung from the center by a leather strap. The plank bent, held, bent again. The carpenter who had come from the far bank made a mark at the point where the grain opened. When Mara added another small stone, the timber gave a dry report, like a door slammed in a far room.

Nobody had been injured. Jake had expected to feel relief. Instead he found himself staring at the pale split, trying to determine what the sound would have meant with a person above the river.

The ferryman's daughter examined the two halves. She did not thank the carpenter for proving her right. She asked which of the pieces had been chosen because it was cheap. The carpenter pointed to both.

Beyond the testing yard the punt remained grounded, drawn up on the landing stones, and the proper ferry was still lost. The river had not made any promise to wait for a better machine. Across it were households whose stores had thinned and workers who had refused to exchange their consent for a debt they had not incurred. On this bank stood the ward, its stove finally working behind Mara's ugly hinge, with enough coal for a few nights. There was medicine in examined packets, a girl holding a piece of her mother's shawl, and a bell without a tongue.

Mara lifted the broken pieces out of the trough.

"We need one that lasts," Jake said.

"We need to learn how one fails," she answered. "Then we can ask what lasts."

There had been nine weeks of contests. Each verdict had been made in a language of exact figures, and an opponent could lose by less than the weight of a pin. Jake could make those distinctions readily enough. He had not learned how to measure the part of a plank that would never bear a human load. There was a kind of arithmetic in refusal, too, and it did not become less exact because a council disliked hearing it.

That morning the creditor's steward summoned the city to the hill. The public notice offered a winter settlement: fuel released to the ward, maintenance of the upper road, and payment for tests of the grounded boat, all in exchange for a concession over the landing. The name of the claimant was written large enough to read across the square. The sum to be paid by the people whose feet would use the landing was written in much smaller letters.

Jake folded a copy and placed it beside the two pieces of timber.

The tenth reckoning began with one question he had no authority to settle: which promise could be tested before somebody was asked to trust it?

## I. Mixon it up for a week and Arm Chair GM — The Gate at the Upper Bend

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Mixon it up for a week (home), 129.93** versus **Arm Chair GM (away), 131.58**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **1.65**.

The first wagon never reached the hill. Its left wheel stopped at the upper-bend gate, where a collector had drawn a chain through a post and made a neat chalk mark across the road. The wagon carried sackcloth, dried roots, and a narrow wooden chest belonging to a cooper. None of it would keep a furnace hot, but the cloth could cover the ward's worst window. Sena had sent Jake to meet the driver because she knew he could read a collector's hand.

The collector showed him a schedule of charges. The wagon had crossed into the city's repair district, he said, and must pay a road assessment. The driver pointed to a ragged patch of gravel where the axle had nearly broken. The collector replied that repair was expensive precisely because the road had grown so bad.

"You cannot charge them for the improvement until you make it," Jake said.

"I am not charging them for an improvement. I am charging them for the district."

The collector's answer was more difficult to argue with because he had stopped pretending to give one. Jake stepped aside to let two farm carts turn. The road was too narrow for them to pass the halted wagon. A boy in the second cart began tapping his heels against its side in a rhythm Jake recognized from the public contest bells. The boy stopped when his father told him to.

The cooper opened his chest. Inside were replacement iron bands for barrels. He showed Jake a cracked band that had come off a barrel at the ward. "See that?" he asked. "One finger's width. Leave it another day and the whole thing goes. A barrel does not care what a council calls the crack."

The day's first public result reached the gate by a runner: Arm Chair GM had defeated Mixon it up for a week **131.58–129.93**, a margin of **1.65**. Jake heard the digits spoken twice. The runner did not ask for a story about their significance. He only wanted someone to mark the result on his slate before he continued toward the market.

The collector seized upon the crowd's attention. The steward's notice, he declared, would settle the question of road maintenance at the hill. Anyone who paid at the gate today could claim credit against the new assessment. He had no receipt book for this promise, only a charcoal pencil and the back of a former toll docket.

Jake caught himself reaching to write down the promise. He had spent so many days following paper to its source that writing had begun to feel like resistance. Instead he asked the driver how much longer the wagon could wait. The man said the roots would survive, but the cooper's bands were needed before the ward's water barrels split.

"Then take the bands on foot," Jake said.

The collector protested that dividing the cargo would divide the charge. Jake showed him the posted schedule: carts, wagons, and commercial loads. It said nothing about two people carrying the iron they required to mend a water vessel. The collector read the line slowly. He could deny the distinction, but not without admitting that the notice lacked it.

The cooper and Jake carried the chest through the gate. The driver remained with the wagon. The cloth was still trapped. Jake did not invent a legal victory out of a box of barrel bands. The collector could amend the notice by nightfall, and the wagon's delay was real. But the cooper made it to the ward in time to replace a splitting hoop, and two apprentices learned to hold the wet staves together while he worked.

In the afternoon, Jake returned to the gate. The boy was asleep in the cart. The collector had brought a proper receipt book, and the driver had paid part of the assessment rather than spend another day waiting. Jake helped unload the cloth and brought it to Sena without calling this a success.

She hung one piece over the window that leaked the coldest air. The other she set aside for a patient who could not bear the draft. When Jake told her the margin of the first reckoning, she asked whether it was close enough to change the driver's bill.

"No," he said.

"Then remember which difference belongs on which account."

## II. El Niño and Two Dreaded Snake — A Bell on Trial

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **El Niño (home), 132.12** versus **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 126.06**. Winner: **El Niño (slot 9)**. Margin **6.06**.

The tower keeper had proposed a tongue for the bell made from the horseshoe iron Mara set aside. He wanted to hear the old note again. Mara said the iron might strike hard enough to crack the bell's lip. A softer wooden striker would tell them whether the tower could carry sound without spending the last piece of metal worth forging.

Jake went up to the tower because the keeper had asked him to bear witness. He found three people at the base of the stairs: the keeper with his improvised striker, an old woman who remembered the tower before the landing was leased, and a mason examining mortar that had loosened along a seam. Nobody wanted to be the first to call the tower unsafe. The keeper could not bear the idea that the bell might be silent by choice rather than defect.

The steps climbed in short flights. One of them moved beneath Jake's boot. He told the keeper; the keeper said he had been avoiding it for years. At the top the air was colder than the stone. Through a slit in the wall Jake could see the ward roof and the narrow trail where people carried water at night. The river itself appeared only in pieces, between buildings and the bare dark crowns of trees. A signal from here could reach the people in the square. It would not round every bend.

The keeper drew the wooden striker back and tapped the bell.

The first sound was not a note so much as the beginning of one. The lip shuddered; the wood bounced away. He tried a different position. The second attempt gave a low, rough tone that Jake felt in the hand he had set against the masonry. A pigeon burst from the roof, flapped once around the open arch, and settled again on a lower ledge.

For a moment the keeper smiled. Then the mason ordered him to stop.

A fine white line had appeared in the mortar beside the bell's mounting pin. It might have been there before. The mason knelt and ran a thumb over the dust. Jake waited for him to announce a certainty. He said only that the mounting needed to be inspected from below and that another strike could widen whatever had moved.

At the tower door they met a messenger bearing the second verdict. **El Niño, an archived competition banner and not a person or an observed storm, prevailed over Two Dreaded Snake 132.12–126.06, by 6.06.** Neither the victor's name nor that margin explained the tower's weather, the mason's finding, or the safety of the mounting. The messenger had no opinion on the bell, but he listened when the keeper told him what they had tried.

The keeper insisted that a tower without a voice was a dead thing. The old woman corrected him.

"It has a stair," she said. "A ledge. A roof where pigeons can be dry. A good view of the road if you don't climb too high. Those things existed before the bell."

It was not the consolation the keeper had asked for. Jake heard the cruelty in it before he understood the gift. The tower had not failed to become itself simply because one useful thing had ceased.

They carried the striker down. At the ward, Sena listened to their report and refused the keeper's offer to strike the bell in an emergency. The risk was not hers to impose on a mason or a passerby. Instead she asked the old woman which houses could hear ordinary voices from the upper road. She knew them by their chimneys and the times their occupants came out to fetch water.

Jake began to draw a chain of possible callers. Halfway through the diagram he put down the charcoal. He had made another system with people reduced to points.

"Would you go and ask them?" Sena said.

He did. One householder said yes only until the children were asleep. Another would watch at dawn but not after dark. A third refused because her brother had fallen from a city stair while doing unpaid repair work. The answer became a patchwork rather than a proclamation, and it contained a refusal that Jake wrote in the same sized letters as the offers.

The tower stayed mute. By evening, with no glorious sound to announce its usefulness, the ward knew which voice might answer when the road was too dark to see.

## III. The KING of the Octagon Village and Red Leopards — The Feast of the Uninvited

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 113.22** versus **Red Leopards (away), 152.92**. Winner: **Red Leopards (slot 2)**. Margin **39.70**.

The creditor's steward served hot food at the hall where the winter settlement was to be explained. People stood in line beneath banners that had been borrowed from a celebration and hung with their colors turned inward. The roasted roots had a sharp smell, more vinegar than spice; the bread was pale and fresh. Jake had eaten nothing since dawn. He joined the line before he learned that the second queue, farther from the doors, was for those who had come merely to listen.

A man with a ledger asked his name.

"Jake."

"Will you consent to the presentation of the settlement?"

"I've come to hear it."

The man placed his finger on the line. Those two answers, he said, belonged in the same column. Consent to a presentation was not consent to its terms. Jake knew that. He also knew how a clerk could later forget the distinction when the page was read aloud. He walked out of the line. The smell of the food followed him across the room.

The far-bank courier stood by a window, holding his injured wrist against his body. He had brought a carpenter to the test yard and returned to discover that the steward had listed him as a representative of the distant households. A person near the dais was explaining that the courier's presence proved the settlement had been fairly offered to both banks.

"Did they choose you?" Jake asked.

"They chose me to bring a failed plank."

"Will you speak?"

"Of the plank."

The steward opened with the condition that made his offer appear generous. In exchange for the city's acceptance of the landing concession, fuel would be released immediately, the repaired road would be maintained, and workers would receive a stipend for tests performed under the steward's inspector. He described the arrangement as a single rope: release one strand and the others fell away.

The third result was carried into the hall between the steward's opening and his reading of terms. Red Leopards had defeated The KING of the Octagon Village **152.92–113.22**, a margin of **39.70**. The square outside answered with cheering, but Jake saw two men in the hall continue dividing bread without so much as looking at the messenger. A verdict might command the public wall and leave a hungry person concerned only with the next meal.

The steward turned toward the courier and said the distant bank had already provided its witness.

The courier lifted the carpenter's broken plank. "I witnessed this," he said. "That is all."

Someone laughed; someone else shouted that the far bank ought to accept the generosity it was being offered. The steward asked whether the courier was authorized to reject the proposal. He said no. The steward spread his hands, as though an inability to reject meant a willingness to agree.

Jake had prepared a speech about the injustice of a council without two banks. He could feel it in his pocket, folded against the notice. There was an old satisfaction in exposing an error cleanly before a crowd. But the courier's wrist was swelling. The man needed to sit; he had carried timber and names a long distance for people who would have preferred a signature.

Jake asked the steward whether a witness could be permitted to leave without creating an inference in the minutes. The steward objected that the question was irrelevant. Jake raised it again, less elegantly, and made the clerk repeat the words "not authorized to accept or reject." There was no applause for the correction. The courier left before anyone could trap him in another question.

Outside, a kitchen girl had brought a tray of the same food to the doorway and was offering portions to those who had not joined either queue. Jake asked whether she had permission. She said it was cooling. His first instinct was to tell her the whole event had been arranged to turn food into assent. She handed him a piece of bread before he could say it.

He ate. The meal was real; so was the attempted bargain. He had no right to make hungry people renounce the first because the second was false.

He carried two portions to the courier, who had sat on a low step with his arm in his lap. The courier accepted one and asked Jake to take the other to the carpenter. Then he said that his people would require a true account of the meeting, including the fact that he had not represented them.

Jake promised to send it. This time he did not promise it would change the steward's mind.

When they returned to the yard, Mara was measuring a different piece of timber. A meal had been served; a country had not consented.

## IV. Benedict Arnold and The Blew Waffle's — What the Test Was For

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Benedict Arnold (home), 170.33** versus **The Blew Waffle's (away), 130.12**. Winner: **Benedict Arnold (slot 8)**. Margin **40.21**.

The foundry's next experiment was not a plank laid across two stones. Mara had constructed a cradle from sawhorses and lengths of rope, with the split timber hanging beneath it as a warning. The far-bank carpenter had made a new brace from his own stock. The ferrywoman would inspect the grain, the weight and the knots; she would not place the brace in the grounded punt, and nobody would be carried over water. Jake repeated those conditions in his own words until she told him he had heard her.

He had. What troubled him was how eagerly he had heard what he wanted: a test could be made, therefore a crossing must be nearer. He looked at the machine with its ropes slack and saw only distance between one kind of proof and another.

Mara suspended sacks of wet sand from the cradle. The first hung without incident. With the second, the new brace made a small complaint as the fibers settled. The carpenter touched the timber, stopped, and measured the line along its grain. He replaced one sack with two smaller loads, believing the weight might be distributed more fairly. Jake helped him tie them in place.

A foundry apprentice wanted to stand on the brace to prove his own confidence. The ferrywoman ordered him back. He protested that the sacks did not move as people moved. She agreed with him and told him that was precisely why no person would be the next weight.

The fourth competitive verdict arrived on a scrap held by the apprentice's brother: Benedict Arnold defeated The Blew Waffle's **170.33–130.12**, margin **40.21**. Jake noted the name and the distance between the figures. He did not confuse the victory with engineering evidence; four Benedict banners existed in the archival season as distinct slots, and none of their names granted a family claim to this bridge, this river, or the workers standing beside it.

The third suspended load caused the cradle to twist. Mara saw the rope bite into the edge of a support and called for everyone to stand clear. The carpenter grabbed the far knot to ease it; the knot held fast, drawing the strap against his wrist. The ferrywoman cut the rope with a short knife. The wet sand hit the trough, sent water over their boots, and pitched one sawhorse sideways. The brace struck the ground with an ugly crack.

No one fell into water. The carpenter's wrist was scraped but could still move. The apprentice went white and sat down among the scraps. Jake found himself shaking harder than the apprentice. The certainty he had carried into the yard was not courage; it was an ignorance that someone else had nearly paid to correct.

The ferrywoman knelt beside the broken brace and showed Mara the compressed fibers at its end. They had learned where the load went when the cradle shifted. They had not learned how a punt would behave in current, with people frightened, with wet ropes, or with a landing that could not be trusted.

The carpenter asked for another piece of wood. Mara refused until the labor already spent was counted and a safe place to work had been made. The steward's inspector, who had watched from beyond the yard wall, reminded them that payment was contingent on acceptance of the settlement.

"Then you have purchased no test," Mara said.

The inspector said nothing. Jake could imagine a report describing her as uncooperative. It would be accurate in the narrowest way and entirely false in the way that mattered.

They carried the damaged brace to a dry bench and laid it beside the first. The apprentice went to the kitchen and came back with a basin for the carpenter's wrist. He did not ask again to stand on the timber.

By dusk the ferrywoman had placed four marks on the trial rig: where the rope had slipped, where the wood had split, where the support had moved, and where no person had been allowed to stand. Mara told Jake not to turn the last mark into a triumph. It was the unspent life that made another test possible.

For the tenth day in succession the grounded punt remained exactly where it should be: out of the river. Jake understood now that this was an active decision rather than a failure to act.

## V. The LLC and Benedict Kenobi — A Defeat with an Address

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **The LLC (home), 176.96** versus **Benedict Kenobi (away), 143.09**. Winner: **The LLC (slot 4)**. Margin **33.87**.

When Jake returned to the hall, the day's fifth reckoning had already been posted. The LLC defeated Benedict Kenobi **176.96–143.09**, a margin of **33.87**. Within the proposed user-attested slot-one literary connection, that gave Jake's competitive banner a record of **3–7** after ten weeks. It did not authenticate a historical human-owner join. Nor did the loss excuse any fiction about what a real owner felt. Here, in this invented city, Jake felt the figures in the same place as the ache in his shoulder from carrying iron and timber.

He had three arguments in his pocket. One was the speech he had prepared for the steward. One was a claim that the hall had misrecorded the courier as the far bank's representative. The third was a prior estimate, signed in his own hand, saying the improvised punt could be repaired with a small quantity of timber and put back into service before the next market day.

It was the third that frightened him.

He had written it before he saw the first brace split. He had not been careless about numbers: he had counted the loose planks, the length of the river edge, the work that seemed possible. But he had not asked the ferrywoman what made a test safe, and he had written the time as though certainty could be borrowed from a calendar.

The steward had attached the estimate to his proposed settlement. The city could meet its delivery obligations, the argument ran, because its own witness had certified a near-term crossing. Jake saw his words copied in a fine hand beneath the heading "Available Transport." They had moved into a place where the doubt he had failed to write could no longer follow.

Varn, the clerk who had argued against the earlier concession, found him studying the copy. "You can dispute their use of it," he said.

"They've quoted me correctly."

"Then dispute what you meant."

"I meant it."

Varn said nothing for a time. He looked older than Jake remembered, though the days since the last hearing could be counted without difficulty. The ward had used examined medicine; the foundry had used time and wood; the hall had used claims. Each resource was beginning to run out at a different rate.

The steward called for objections. Jake rose. He could feel the hostility before anyone spoke. His name had become attached to every failure of the crossing and to a great many promises he had not made. If he stood to challenge the settlement, the crowd would hear another speech by a man whose contest record had worsened and whose river had not reopened.

He did not give the speech.

"I wrote that estimate," he said, indicating the page. "I was not qualified to say the boat would bear a load. I have seen a brace fail on land under conditions gentler than the river. Withdraw my estimate from your evidence of available transport."

The steward asked whether he was recanting the need for a crossing.

"No."

"Then do you propose that sick people freeze while engineers debate a piece of wood?"

Sena was in the room. Jake did not look to her for rescue. He had come to correct an error, not to make her endorse the correction.

"No," he said. "I propose we stop presenting an untested crossing as payment for a debt."

Someone near the door shouted that a man who lost his own contests had no standing to decide another person's road. A second voice asked who would pay for the wood already wasted. Jake could answer the first accusation by saying contests were separate from navigation, but he could not answer the second without naming the people who had cut timber and hauled sand without secure wages.

"Place the failed tests in the cost," he said. "With the names of the people who performed them. And place the river's closure in the plan as a present fact."

The steward said that such a correction would jeopardize the creditor's offer.

For a moment Jake wanted to withdraw it. He pictured the ward stove without coal. The thought was not abstract: he had fed it with the wood from a bucket, watched a hinge fail to align, and seen the girl sit close enough to the heat to risk her hands. He could give the hall a reassuring lie and perhaps release three nights of fuel. Whether those nights would be purchased with someone's drowning was a calculation no one had put on the page.

"I cannot let you use that estimate," he said.

The steward ordered the correction entered and adjourned the question of the landing. The creditor's agent left without confirming the fuel release.

Outside, Varn accused Jake of making an expensive truth at a cheap moment. He did not say it cruelly. Jake could not tell whether the accusation was fair. The city had not acquired a winter supply by hearing him speak. The river was no closer to being crossed. The apprentice from the foundry stood under the eaves with a bandage in his hands, looking for the carpenter who had scraped his wrist.

Jake gave him directions. Then he went to the ward, where Sena counted the remaining coal with the thoroughness of someone unwilling to make another person's speech into heat.

## VI. Eggs Benedict and Benedict Coomhersnatch — The Price Paid Before Consent

**VERIFIED ARCHIVAL RECORD; NO CLAIM OF FICTIONAL CAUSATION.** **Eggs Benedict (home), 170.80** versus **Benedict Coomhersnatch (away), 186.16**. Winner: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (slot 5)**. Margin **15.36**.

The money came in coins, not as a rescue.

The kitchen workers had taken the unsold bread from the hall's feast and sold it by portions at the market, giving away what was too stale to sell. The cobbler had placed a small jar by his door. The cooper charged half his usual price for the replacement barrel bands and put the remainder into the jar. One widow brought a coil of lamp wick and demanded that it be counted as material, not charity. A carpenter offered no money at all; he offered to teach two apprentices how to distinguish a split along the grain from a harmless surface mark.

Sena laid these contributions on a long table in the ward. There was not enough to pay the creditor or reopen the landing. There was enough to buy fuel for a limited span and to compensate the workers for a new, bounded test, if no one mistook that test for permission to launch a boat.

Jake had spent the day trying to keep a false promise off the record. The new arrangement unsettled him in another way: it had no central benefactor. Nobody had won it. People were choosing to pay for work that might conclude with another refusal.

The ferrywoman came through the door carrying the broken brace. Mara followed with the carpenter and the apprentice, who held his bandaged hand close to his coat. The far-bank courier stood last, uncertain whether the room meant to make him a delegate again. Sena asked the courier to sit and eat before anything was said about signatures.

They set conditions on the table alongside the coins. The carpenter would demonstrate a new brace on dry ground, under a controlled load, without anyone standing on it. The ferrywoman could stop the trial for any reason related to safety. The workers would receive their compensation whether the piece passed or failed. A further crossing would require a separate decision by the people responsible for it, and no clause in this payment would consent to the creditor's toll.

Varn arrived with a clerk who carried a blank form headed "Undertaking." He read the conditions and asked who had authorized them. Sena replied that each contributor could withdraw before the money was spent and that the people doing the work had agreed to the risk as described. She did not claim the power to speak for the city.

"That is not a government," Varn said.

"Then don't call it one."

Jake almost laughed. The answer was direct without being a victory. Varn pulled a chair closer and began making separate columns for money, materials, work, and permission. Jake remembered the market clerk's inventory of neighborhood lamps, and how blank spaces could turn a good effort into a command. He asked Varn to include a column for what a contribution did not purchase.

"An awkward column," Varn said.

"A necessary one."

The clerk wrote it. Beside the carpenter's time, he entered **no passage authorized**. Beside the widow's wick, **not a public lighting levy**. Beside the kitchen workers' coins, **not acceptance of landing concession**. Beside the far-bank courier's presence, after the courier requested it twice, **no authority to consent on behalf of households across river**.

The sixth verdict was brought in just as the last entry dried: Benedict Coomhersnatch had defeated Eggs Benedict **186.16–170.80**, margin **15.36**. The two Benedict banners represented separate archival slots, whatever resemblance the names suggested. Jake wrote the figures on a scrap away from the undertaking. He would not place a competition outcome among the consent terms simply because both belonged to the same day.

The ferrywoman took the new conditions to the testing yard. She returned after dark without having tested anything. The support blocks, she reported, were too wet and the rope still carried a weak splice. Testing would begin when the ground was sound enough to trust. The kitchen workers did not demand their money back. The apprentice, who had learned what a cut rope could prevent, asked whether he could replace the splice under Mara's eye.

Varn was not satisfied. The arrangement bought only time, he said, and time acquired without ownership could be taken away by any later crisis. Jake knew enough of the city's failures to fear that he might be right. But he had also seen what ownership had done when the lender tried to turn the river into collateral: it had promised a crossing that did not exist.

He asked the courier whether anyone on the far bank could receive a copy of the undertaking by the upper road. The courier said he could carry it, but he would not return with an answer until those who lived there had chosen what to say. He refused a travel fee that would have made him appear to act under the council's employ. Sena offered him bread and a clean bandage instead. He accepted both.

The girl from the ward was waiting beside the table with her mother's piece of shawl wrapped around her wrist. She asked Jake whether the new paper would let her mother come home.

He looked at the form. It named fuel, tests, workers, and things not purchased. It did not provide a safe passage. He wanted to promise that one would follow. The girl knew the difference between a piece of cloth and the person who had worn it. She would recognize the same evasion in his words.

"Not yet," he said. "It may help us stop doing the wrong thing while we find the right way."

She studied him for a moment. Then she asked whether the paper would at least keep the stove burning for another night.

"That part," he said, "we can count."

### After the Tenth Reckoning

In the morning the river ran under a sky the color of dull iron. No ice bridged it; no bridge had been built; the grounded punt had not moved. The proper ferry was still lost, and the landing remained under a disputed concession. The lender's steward had neither withdrawn the winter offer nor guaranteed the ward its fuel. The far-bank households still lacked recognized representation, and nobody had made the dangerous journey seem safe by writing about it.

At the testing yard Mara set fresh blocks on dry gravel and rejected one after finding that it rocked when she pressed it. The carpenter showed an apprentice the difference between a knot that held a load and one that merely looked tidy. The ferrywoman inspected the ropes herself. She neither thanked Jake for the undertaking nor promised a launch. Her right to stop the work was no longer being treated as an inconvenience to be erased by an inspector's signature.

Jake watched them for a while, then went down to the market. A mother was teaching her son to mend a strap. A porter argued with a seller over an unpaid cart. The steward's proclamation was still fastened to the hill wall, untouched by the new table at the ward. Most of the city did not know the conditions Sena had helped write, and some who knew them did not approve. Jake did not try to persuade the street that the tenth week had ended well.

He took the road to the upper-bend gate. The collector's amended schedule now charged hand-carried iron as well as carts; the change had come exactly as Jake feared. Yet the cooper's two bands were already fitted to the ward barrel. No new order could undo the water that barrel would hold. It was a modest and inadequate kind of resistance, all the more irritating for being real.

A runner from the ward caught him before he reached the gate. Sena had sent a request for another length of cloth, and the messenger wanted a definite answer. Jake had no money left to buy it. He told the runner where the cooper had found a remnant and how to ask, without turning a favor into a municipal obligation.

On his way back he passed the tower. The keeper had covered the loose stair with a board marked UNSAFE. The bell remained silent above him. Below, the ward's little lamps burned where people carried water, not where a distant public audience might admire them.

He had lost his reckoning by **33.87** and stood, within this proposed fictional slot-one frame, at **3–7**. He could not turn either figure into a reason to command other people. He could only decide whether he would continue to be corrected by what they knew.

Behind the foundry the first broken plank lay beside the second. Both were dry now. Their cracks were plainly visible. Neither could carry a human being across the river. Jake thought of how long he had regarded that as a useless fact.

The next test had not begun. That was not the same as the work having stopped.

No Week 11+ competitive outcome is known in this narrative. Independent Authors Council, Atlas, Umpire and Founder canon/publication approval remain outstanding.

# 2023 — Week 11: The Orchard That Refused a Name
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six archived competitions · substantial NONCANON historical fiction*

**Provenance and authority.** Six competitive results below come from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`, labeled Flaim / ESPN league 1417621. Raw upstream ESPN SHA and historical human-owner joins are UNVERIFIED. The proposed Jake / archive slot-one connection is **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. The four distinct Benedict banners are different archived teams, not one verified family or human owner. The El Niño banner is an archival opponent label, not a speaking person or a claim about real weather. Home/away competition fields do not identify physical venues. The **sole proposed principal interior viewpoint is Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`)**. All inhabitants, orchard, foundry, ward, lanterns, legal arrangements, dialogue, journeys, feelings, finances, weather, buildings, and consequences are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. No Week 12+ result is known in the diegetic present. This does not revise the protected 2026 Prologue or Chapters I–III. **NONCANON / INDEPENDENT EDITORIAL HOLD.**

The orchard had been planted in the wrong place, if one believed the engineers. It lay on a rise behind the old performance court, where stone dust from the quarry traveled whenever the wind took the western road. There was not enough water, the engineers said; no direct commerce; no grant of useful land. The trees had nevertheless reached an age when two could stand beside each other without touching and still give shade to anyone willing to sit between them.

In the last light of the previous evening, Mara had taken a short length of broken test timber to the orchard. She set it beneath one of the trees and wrote FAILURE across its flat end in charcoal. By morning someone had laid a child's woolen mitten upon it. Nobody would say whose mitten it was. Jake had watched three different residents turn aside rather than lift it.

He knew why they feared naming things. The creditor's collector had begun calling the upper road a public asset, then an assessable route, and then, in an order delivered after the tenth reckoning, a secured approach to the contested crossing. Each phrase had acquired a different fee. The river had not moved. The punt remained grounded. The proper ferry was lost. Yet ownership continued to travel inland faster than any boat could have carried it.

When he came from the ward, the first thing Jake heard was glass striking glass. A family of itinerant lantern makers had arrived with a handcart, a sheet of cracked panes, and a folded stall roof. They had not been invited by the council. One of the lantern makers stood before the broken plank, reading the charcoal word aloud as though it were a declaration of war.

"It failed," the woman said.

"It saved somebody by failing here," Mara answered. "That part goes on the other side."

A courier waited beneath the next tree, his boots split at the toes. He had returned by the upper road from the far bank. He carried no sealed official petition. He carried three small wooden counters, one blackened at the edge, and a message which he refused to repeat until the people to whom it belonged were present.

"At least tell me whether the households received the terms," Jake asked.

"They did," said the courier.

"And?"

"And they asked whether you had read them as a demand for your permission."

Jake felt the question strike harder than any refusal. The conditions agreed beside the ward stove had seemed to him careful and humane. They had not been an offer for him to grant. The people across the river had spoken as people whose lives were already under way, and whose answer would not wait to become legitimate until a council recognized it.

Sena emerged from the ward with a wooden bowl wrapped in cloth. She was tired enough to carry it with both arms although it contained almost nothing. Beside her walked the girl who wore her absent mother's shawl around her wrist. The girl looked toward the lantern makers and then toward the river that could not be seen from this hill.

"Are they here to make a signal?" she asked.

"Perhaps," Jake said.

"Then somebody ought to ask who is supposed to see it."

That was the first question of the eleventh week, though Jake would not understand its cost until night.

## I. Benedict Arnold and Two Dreaded Snake — A Light That Could Be Declined

The lantern maker's name was Ila. She would lend the city six lamps, she said, provided that no collector could convert the loan into a municipal purchase. She had encountered that trick in other places. A road tax paid by a tradeswoman became proof that she owned a stall; a market license became proof that she owed rent on an entire square. The family had kept moving because their wares were portable and the meanings assigned to them were not.

Mara brought the two halves of the first failed plank from under the tree. Ila studied the grain, then asked whether the proposed bridge was to be made of lamps.

"No," Mara said.

"Then I cannot improve it. But I can keep you from hiding a crack at night."

Together they fixed glass on a frame built for a street procession, not a crossing. The panes were of unequal thickness and showed the faces behind them in wavering sections. Jake found himself adjusting a crooked panel until Ila put her hand over his.

"It isn't yours to correct," she said. "That bend means the pane was saved. The straight ones broke."

It was the kind of remark he might once have copied into a notebook and admired for its elegance. The lanterns would not remain in a notebook. They would have to be carried by people, and people could be compelled to carry things that they had first carried willingly. The steward had already proposed requisitioning the ward's supply runners under the phrase temporary common service.

Ila took the lantern away from Jake and held it up before the waiting crowd. "Who will use this?" she asked.

Several hands rose. The collector's apprentice raised his hand as well, though he had arrived with a slate bearing the steward's seal. Ila gave him the heaviest lamp. He nearly dropped it.

"I meant to register the devices," the apprentice said.

"You are welcome to register their weight," Ila told him, "after you have held one long enough to know it."

A boy from the orchard offered to hold the lamp while the apprentice wrote. The apprentice looked at the slate, his ink, and the strained muscles of the child. Then he set the slate aside and accepted the boy's assistance as assistance, not a duty.

Jake wanted to call the gesture a victory. But nobody had overturned an ordinance. A young man had merely noticed the work his order concealed. He might forget the discovery when he went back to his superior. The boy would remember if the heavy lamp fell.

At dusk, six people carried the borrowed lights uphill and set them in a line facing the upper road. The far bank was out of sight. No one claimed the lamps could communicate across the river. Instead the lanterns illuminated the dangerous first turn for anyone arriving by land. A widow who had refused to enter the old toll house walked past the line with her grandson. She did not thank the city; she thanked Ila, who had paid for the oil.

"Does she owe you for that?" Jake asked quietly.

"Nothing tonight," said Ila. "Tomorrow she can decide whether I have earned anything."

The day's competition ledger arrived wrapped against dust. The record was exact where the village was not: **Benedict Arnold (home), 131.49**; **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 109.06**. Winner: **Benedict Arnold (slot 8)**. Margin **22.43**.

Jake wrote the figures in the competition register and did not put them on the lamps. The archived banners did not own the widow's walk; their result could tell no one who had consented to carry a light. When the widow passed the sixth lantern, Ila extinguished it at her request so the small boy beside her could see the stars.

The line of people did not dissolve. Five lights remained. For once an absence had been chosen, not imposed.

## II. Eggs Benedict and Mixon it up for a week — The Work of Keeping Seeds

Sena took Jake to a narrow enclosure behind the orchard, where a dozen clay jars stood upon a shelf beneath the eaves. The jars contained seed, not food. Some had been gathered before the loan on the crossing; others had come from the far-bank households during years when passage had been ordinary and nobody needed a legal doctrine to exchange a handful of grain.

Now the ward stove had enough fuel only because workers had volunteered coal against the lender's conditions. The ward's kitchen was short of vegetables, and the orchard keeper wanted the dry shelves dismantled for kindling. He had not asked Sena, whose medicines shared the same roof. He had not asked the families who had left seed in his care.

"We can preserve the labels," Jake said, seeing the jars and imagining an orderly list.

"Labels will not germinate," Sena answered.

A quarrel sounded at the entrance. The orchard keeper had brought two men with pry bars, neither a council officer. He was not cruel. His youngest child had slept beside a cold hearth the night before, and there was no reasonable argument that a shelf was more important than that child. He said he would return the jars, but he could not keep both the shelving and the roof against winter.

Sena opened a jar and tipped three seeds into her palm. They were so small that one rolled into the crease beside her thumb. She asked the keeper to name the family who had given them. He could not. Jake could not either. The refusal of memory did not make the gift municipal property.

For the first time since the road levy began, the argument had no obvious creditor. Everyone in the enclosure needed something honest. Heat was honest. A future harvest was honest. The weariness of the men waiting with pry bars was honest. Jake was tired of hearings where both sides could be represented by a single officious sentence. Here both sides could reach the same shelf.

Mara came to inspect the timber. She rejected the planks for crossing work because their narrow grain and hidden wormholes would fail under a changing load. For kindling they were excellent. When she said so, the keeper laughed without humor. "Every time I bring you wood," he said, "you tell me what it cannot do."

"Then listen when I tell you what it can," Mara replied.

She pointed to a stack of fractured test boards behind the foundry. Some had already been marked against future use. The boards would burn; they could be carried without threatening anyone over water. But the stack belonged to the workers who had supplied and tested the timber under the voluntary arrangement. Mara would not give it away on behalf of people absent from the orchard.

Jake offered to fetch them. The keeper refused to wait for another committee. Sena told him she would remain with the jars until the issue was settled, even if she missed her own evening meal. Jake went to the foundry on foot and returned with three apprentices and a carpenter. They were angry to have been called away from their work. They listened. One apprentice gave his share of broken board on condition that the first load warm the keeper's house, not the ward. Another declined. The carpenter permitted the sawdust but not the larger pieces. The division took less than an hour and left everyone slightly dissatisfied.

When the first bundle went to the keeper's house, he laid down the pry bar. Sena moved the jars to a safer shelf inside the ward, not as confiscated stores but as deposits that could be requested back. Jake carried the last jar himself. He wanted to ask whether the arrangement had saved the seed. Sena said they would learn when planting season came, if there was a planting season in which the owners could return.

The competition record for this encounter read: **Eggs Benedict (home), 175.06**; **Mixon it up for a week (away), 153.90**. Winner: **Eggs Benedict (slot 12)**. Margin **21.16**.

The figures were unusually strong in both columns. Jake wondered whether that was why the day's reader had spoken them with theatrical force, as though a larger number guaranteed a more important life. He had seen a child sleep in a warm room because someone allowed a board to burn, and a seed remain in its jar because someone refused to confuse custody with ownership. Neither decision would appear beside the scores. Both would have to survive the night.

## III. Benedict Coomhersnatch and Arm Chair GM — The Play With No King

The travelers had brought not only lanterns but a collapsible stage. They erected it in the old performance court, where the dismantled steps still exposed the rough earth beneath. The leader of the troupe was an aging man with a damaged voice. He introduced himself as Tavel, then said names were dangerous at courts and invited the audience to call him whatever suited the part he was playing.

His play required a king, a sailor, a prisoner, a witness, and a child. He had four actors and a paper crown.

"The child is the audience," he explained.

"Then why does the child never speak?" asked the girl with her mother's shawl.

Tavel regarded her carefully. He crossed out a passage. He was practiced in receiving a clever criticism and turning it into proof of his own generosity. Jake saw the habit because it resembled one of his own.

During rehearsal, an actor playing the sailor repeatedly announced that the river had been made safe by decree. The line received laughter the first time. On the third repetition, the ferryman's daughter stepped forward and asked the actor to demonstrate the kind of decree that kept a current from taking a life. The actor, who had never touched the river, faltered. Tavel offered to cut the line. She told him not to cut it.

"Make the sailor live with it," she said.

Tavel changed the scene. The sailor signed a paper and sent his brother out in a boat. The brother did not return. No one fell into a theatrical river; there was no painted water. The actor simply carried an empty pair of shoes to the front of the stage and set them at the king's feet. The silence was longer than the laughter had been.

Jake could not decide whether the new version told the truth. No brother had gone out under the particular order Tavel had invented. The grounded punt had remained empty because the workers held their refusal. But the absence of a dead brother was owed to their refusal, not to the wisdom of the council. The play contained a false event in order to reveal a true danger. That was a more difficult proposition than any charter opinion he had been asked to sign.

In the middle of rehearsal, the creditor's steward arrived. He was irritated by the crowd and offended by the shoes. He ordered Tavel to identify the lord accused in the piece. Tavel bowed and said his actors worked without lords. The steward asked whether the king was the council. Tavel replied that the king was the king when the actor put on the crown and the actor when he took it off.

The steward could not tax ambiguity, but he could stop a performance. He threatened the stall permission for the travelers. The lantern makers, who had come only to see the play, stood up. Ila removed the largest lamp from its hook and announced that its loan was revoked. The courtyard darkened along one side. Tavel continued under the remaining light, but he skipped his speech of praise for the city.

Jake found himself hoping the steward would leave. Instead the man sat in the front row. He took off his gloves and watched the sailor fail, and watched the empty shoes appear. He did not change his mind about the toll. At the end he told Tavel that he would be watching the troupe's next performance as well.

The ledger carried its own unequivocal judgment: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (home), 149.20**; **Arm Chair GM (away), 96.94**. Winner: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (slot 5)**. Margin **52.26**.

The discrepancy was the broadest of the six records that week. Jake heard a spectator call it a humiliation. He refused to repeat the word for the strangers behind the banners. He had no knowledge of their private reasons, and a competitive score was not a license to invent them. On the stage, humiliation had belonged neither to a winner nor a loser, but to the moment the paper king looked down at the empty shoes and discovered that no speech could put feet back inside them.

After the crowd dispersed, Tavel offered Jake the discarded script. Jake asked him to give it to the girl. She took it, folded it into four uneven pieces, and said she wanted to write the next ending herself.

## IV. The KING of the Octagon Village and Benedict Kenobi — The Price of Having a Room

The steward's offer came the following morning, although Jake did not know if it had been prompted by the play. A clerk brought a key and a folded letter to the little room Jake occupied above the market. The letter called him an independent recorder, praised his attention to the public interest, and invited him to oversee the creditor's new survey of the upper road. It promised uninterrupted access to his room, fuel for his fire, and permission to consult the creditor's records.

The work would be simple, the clerk said. Jake need only certify that the road was fit for its intended commercial use. He was free to note irregularities. He was not free to issue findings that impaired the lender's current security.

"Then you are asking me to finish the survey before it begins," Jake said.

The clerk looked relieved. "You understand the condition."

He had understood worse conditions too late. A transport estimate he had once offered without enough evidence had been used to justify an unsafe promise. His public retraction still stood on the hill wall, though somebody had smeared the lower half with mud. The new offer was dressed as a remedy for that embarrassment: take a better role, take better rooms, and become the sort of expert whose errors could be managed privately.

He looked around the chamber. Almost nothing in it had value: a coat repaired at the shoulder, a cup chipped along the rim, the ledger in which he had recorded the contest results, a window that leaked onto his pillow when rain came from the west. Yet he had worked there in a privacy he had never questioned. Each night he could close the door and listen to his own thoughts without needing permission from a person who wanted those thoughts certified.

When he declined the offer, the clerk put the key in his pocket. "The room stands on an assessable property," he said. "Occupation without a recognized commercial purpose will be reconsidered."

The threat was not an eviction order. It was worse in its way, because Jake could not answer it at a single meeting. He could spend each day waiting for the reconsideration. He could become the steward's surveyor to stop waiting. He could hide his records in the walls and pretend the choice had not been made.

He carried the ledger downstairs instead. At the market a potter offered him a place to leave it behind a counter, but Jake refused to place the contents of his memory on a stranger's rent account. The potter said a shelf could be offered without becoming collateral. Jake had no reason to doubt him, yet he wanted to keep the book in his hands.

On the day's competition sheet his own proposed historical slot bore a result he had not anticipated after the recent defeats. **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 119.98**; **Benedict Kenobi (away), 160.79**. Winner: **Benedict Kenobi (slot 1)**. Margin **40.81**.

In the provisional literary association of Jake with slot one, it was his fourth win against seven losses: **4–7**. He looked at the sheet longer than he intended. Victory had a brief, bodily power. He wanted to bring it to Sena, to Mara, even to the clerk who had offered the key. He wanted someone to understand that he was not merely the man who recanted, who could find only reasons a bridge must not be used.

But the score proved only what the archived competition recorded. It could not make his room secure; it could not turn an unsafe estimate into a sound one. He took his ledger to the orchard and began teaching two apprentices how he had checked each public figure. They copied the method rather than his conclusions. One challenged a margin he had calculated too quickly. Jake counted again and let the apprentice correct him in front of the others.

By sundown five people knew where the original records could be checked. He had not made the ledger public property, and he had not stopped the steward from taking the room. He had made the room less necessary to the survival of the facts it contained.

That evening he slept in his bed with the door unlatched, not because he had ceased to value privacy but because the clerk's key would no longer decide whether anyone else could ask him a question.

## V. El Niño and The Blew Waffle's — The Song That Was Not a Signal

The mother of the girl with the shawl was well enough to speak, but not well enough to walk to the orchard. Sena refused to let the young patient be carried for the performance. The fever ward had become a place where desperate visitors interpreted any permission as a promise of recovery. Sena would not add a theatrical procession to that burden.

So Tavel brought the performance to the ward's outer courtyard. He dispensed with the king and the shoes and sang the part of the sailor alone. The people inside could hear him when the wind fell, and could not hear him when it rose. The uncertainty altered the song. Each pause grew longer until a listener might have mistaken the silences for part of the music.

Jake carried no lantern. The girl had insisted on that. She did not want the lights placed where they would seem to announce that her mother was coming home. Instead she stood by the threshold and held the folded script Tavel had given her.

"Tell them the child has a line," she said.

Tavel asked what the line was. She told him it was not written yet. He waited. From inside came the small sounds of someone being helped with a cup. Mara, who was repairing the courtyard bench, stopped striking her mallet. Jake could hear the uneven breathing of the people sitting nearest the wall.

At last the girl spoke. "The boat did not come," she said, "but neither did anyone send me away."

Tavel repeated her words, softly, without the melody. Sena came to the threshold and asked him not to repeat them again. Her concern was not censorship. The girl had spoken once for herself. It was already becoming too easy for a talented stranger to make the sentence the property of his performance.

Afterward Jake helped fold the canopy. From the ward came a request for water and an argument over the clean cup. Ordinary need returned with a force he found almost reassuring. The audience had been moved. The ward still had to be staffed, the stove fed, the medicine counted, the girl allowed to be angry without becoming a symbol that improved anybody else's speech.

The fifth archived contest stood beside that day's events in a separate register: **El Niño (home), 175.50**; **The Blew Waffle's (away), 154.76**. Winner: **El Niño (slot 9)**. Margin **20.74**.

El Niño was the name of an archived competing banner. Jake did not encounter a walking storm; no speaking weather addressed him. Nor did the scored result establish that the fictional wind at the ward had any relationship to an actual season's climate. The record was precise on the competition and silent about the humans to whom its banners might once have belonged.

Near nightfall Ila offered to hang a lamp outside the patient's window. Sena considered it and then asked the woman to take the lamp to the lower path instead. The window did not need to attract observers. People fetching water in darkness did need to see the uneven step.

Jake watched Ila carry the lamp away from the ward, and understood that a sign could be a kindness precisely because it announced nothing. The mother slept. Her daughter sat by the threshold and wrote three new lines on the reverse of the discarded script. None had the word king in it.

## VI. Red Leopards and The LLC — The Walk That Stopped at the Bank

The courier returned with the last wooden counter after the sixth public reckoning had been announced. He had been keeping it in the lining of his coat. The first two counters had meant households willing to send food by the upper road and workers willing to inspect a passage when an actual safe design existed. The third was different. It bore a hole pierced through the center.

"What does that mean?" Jake asked.

"It means they refuse to make your list," said the courier. "Not all of them. Enough that the count you gave the council cannot speak for the far bank."

Jake felt the familiar impulse to correct the count. He could place the uncertainty in a margin and tell the council how many names were missing. He could not make missing people into consenting people. The courier explained that the counter was not an instruction to begin a new registration. The households had sent it to warn that anyone claiming universal authority on their behalf would be speaking beyond the message.

The creditor's steward had called a meeting at the river landing. He intended to exhibit the trial plank, the repaired lanterns, and the draft funding conditions as evidence that transport would soon resume. There was to be a formal declaration of progress. The ferrywoman had not accepted a launch, and Mara's tests had not proven a human crossing safe. Nevertheless, the word progress would be stamped and distributed before nightfall.

The ferryman's daughter came up from the landing carrying a rope end, wet and newly frayed. She had cut it from the grounded punt where it had begun to chafe against a stone. "Show them this," she told Jake.

He said it might be mistaken for an accusation of sabotage.

"Then let them ask who had to cut it," she replied.

At the meeting the steward spoke at length about common confidence. He pointed to the orchard lamps, the new stove hinge, the restored supply line by the upper road, the offer of further timber. Jake listened and recognized the shape of an old fraud: a handful of real improvements assembled into a conclusion none of them could support. The workers had made the ward warmer; they had not cured the crossing. The lantern makers had lit a path; they had not authorized a toll. The far bank had sent conditions and refusals; it had not consented to a road owner.

When the steward invited the ferryman's daughter to stand beside the broken plank, she refused. She went instead to the edge of the landing and laid the frayed rope where the crowd could see it. She did not give a speech. Mara took out a piece of charcoal and marked the point where the rope had worn through. Sena asked that the ward's name be removed from the announcement unless the workers who had saved it were permitted to correct the statement.

The steward tried to continue over their objections. The crowd began to move. For an instant Jake feared a rush toward the water. But the people went to the upper road, not the punt. They walked in a long, uneven line to the end of the safe path, the one Ila had lit. Some carried food for the far-bank courier. Others came only to witness the distance that remained. Children were kept behind the rough stones marking unstable ground.

Jake walked with them, carrying his ledger and the drilled wooden counter. No one had appointed him leader. Twice he fell behind to help an older porter keep his cart on the road. The river appeared between the bare trees, wide and ordinary. The line stopped before the landing. Nobody crossed. Nobody could use their presence as proof that they had asked to be carried.

The last of the week's competitive announcements came after the walk: **Red Leopards (home), 117.50**; **The LLC (away), 113.06**. Winner: **Red Leopards (slot 2)**. Margin **4.44**.

It was a close contest, and Jake could feel how readily a reader of the record might build a grand theory of boldness from four points and a fraction. But the six archived matches had told him nothing about the creditor, the ferryman, the orchard, or the people on the road. Those were proposed stories in a place whose Atlas had not been approved. He would not award the crowd's action to any real competitor or transfer the fictive lender's motives to any historical owner.

At the stopping place the courier showed the drilled counter to the people nearest him. He did not pass it to a spokesman. A woman asked that it be placed on the ground so all could see it without anyone appearing to hold authority for its makers. The courier complied. On the far bank there was no answering light. Jake waited anyway, then stopped waiting when the ferrywoman said the ground was becoming unsafe in the dark.

They walked back by the lamps. At the orchard the child with the shawl asked why they had gone all that way if nobody had arrived.

"To know where the road ends," Jake said.

She considered that and shook her head. "We knew that already."

He had no clever answer. She went to Sena, took the empty water bowl, and carried it into the ward. The walk had changed something, but he did not yet know what. He was beginning to suspect that what mattered had happened when everyone turned around.

### After the Eleventh Reckoning

The steward's declaration was never delivered in the form he had planned. He issued a shorter notice about continued assessment of the upper road. It claimed no consent from the far bank. It also withdrew none of the debt. His authority remained dangerous precisely because he had lost a public sentence, not the machinery behind it.

The proper ferry was still lost. The punt remained grounded. No passenger-load test had been completed; no passage authorized. Mara's next dry-ground brace trial had not begun. The orchard keeper had heat for a few nights and the seed jars had been moved out of reach of his pry bar. Their owners still had to be found. The ward had its safe hinge, insufficient medicine, and a mother whose recovery could not be promised. The toll claim, the workers' right to stop unsafe work, and the far bank's right to answer for itself remained unresolved.

Tavel left the court at dawn. Before he departed he returned to the ward for the girl's ending. She gave him a page she had written herself, but not the folded script he had offered. "You can use these lines," she said, "if you say I wrote them." He agreed. Jake was not present for the exchange. He learned of it when the girl showed him the script later and made him promise not to correct the spelling.

He had no longer been able to think of victories as an escape from the city. His fourth competitive win, recorded as **4–7** in this proposed slot-one frame, was genuine within the archive and did not warm a room or rebuild a ferry. The threats to his lodging remained. But the apprentice who had challenged his calculations came to the market unbidden with two new copies of the scoring method, each corrected by someone Jake did not know. The knowledge had begun to leave his custody without being lost.

At the orchard Ila's family packed its lamps. She would not leave them indefinitely on public loan. The widow and her grandson helped carry them to the handcart. For the first time, the path went dark because someone was taking back what belonged to her, and no one tried to call it theft.

Mara moved the broken test timber from beneath the tree and set it near the foundry. The child's mitten remained where it had fallen. Jake picked it up, intending to ask whose it was, then put it on a low branch where its owner could see it. He did not write a notice.

A fruit tree bore no label. From the road, in its season, anyone might find shade beneath it. The orchard had been planted in the wrong place, the engineers said. On the morning after the eleventh reckoning, Jake could see exactly where it stood. He could not say who would be entitled to name it.

---

No weekly sporting outcome is asserted to have caused any fictional civic act. No historical owner identities or actual physical venues are certified. The four distinct Benedict banners remain distinct archival competitors; the El Niño banner is not a person or meteorological evidence. All political, medical, interpersonal, industrial, geographical, financial and legal details are **NONCANON PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. Only Jake is a proposed principal interior POV, and his historical identity link is user-attested rather than provider-authenticated. No Week 12+ knowledge enters the tale. Independent Authors Council, Atlas, Umpire and Founder canon/publication approvals are all outstanding.
