# 2023 — Week 12: The House That Could Not Be Locked
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six archived contests · substantive NONCANON historical fiction*

**Evidence and boundary.** The six source-exact Week 12 competitive results are drawn from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`, labeled Flaim / ESPN 1417621. Raw ESPN provenance SHA, historical human-owner assignments and actual geographic venues remain **UNVERIFIED**; archive home/away flags are not physical locations. Jake's proposed association with slot-one Benedict Kenobi is **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. The four archived Benedict banners do not establish any shared human lineage. The El Niño banner is a competitor label, not a speaking person or evidence of the fictional climate. Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`) is the **sole proposed principal interior POV**. All other characters are externally observed fictional civilians. The room seizure, orchard, kitchens, roads, quarry, ward, fires, legal claims, economics, weather and relationships in this chapter are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. No Week 13+ competitive outcome enters this story. The protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III are untouched. **NONCANON / AUTHOR COUNCIL, ATLAS AND UMPIRE HOLD.**

When Jake returned to his room above the market, he found a strip of white paper pasted across the latch.

It was too narrow to be a proper notice and too carefully placed to be an accident. The paper joined the door to its frame, carrying a smear of the creditor's purple wax. Somebody had written *Occupation under review* in a hand that did not tremble. It was the sort of handwriting that made uncertainty appear official.

The room was still his, the landlord told him. No seizure had occurred. The mark simply warned that the property might soon be entered and inventoried. A reasonable man, the landlord said, would leave the strip unbroken until the steward could confirm that everything remained in good order.

"A reasonable man who sleeps outdoors," Jake said.

The landlord did not smile. He had a family below, a shop filled with goods purchased on credit, and a ceiling that stained when Jake's window leaked. It would not help him to be courageous on another tenant's behalf. He suggested that Jake take the back stairs, where a key might be found, and come in through the kitchen until the question was settled.

Jake stared at the strip of paper. Breaking it would be called interference. Leaving it intact would make the steward the master of every hour he spent in his own bed. He had declined the new survey commission in the eleventh week because it required him to certify the commercial value of the road before it had been safely surveyed. Now the fee for that refusal had arrived in a different form: not a fine, not an argument, but a door he had been told he could not open.

At the far end of the market Mara was walking beside a cart full of cracked tiles. She saw him at the stair and did not ask whether the notice was lawful. She asked whether he was hungry. The answer he gave was longer than it needed to be, and she waited until he had finished explaining the entire controversy.

"You can tell me later what the paper says," she said. "I asked whether you had eaten."

He had not. She took one of the broken tiles from the cart and put it in his hands. A painted blue bird crossed its surface, the beak broken off where the clay had split. "We are making a hearth that doesn't pretend to be a bridge," she said. "You can help carry it."

They walked toward the abandoned performance court. The people who had gathered there after the eleventh reckoning had gone home or returned to the ward. Ila's borrowed lanterns were gone; she had reclaimed them as their owner, not as the creditor's supplier. The orchard lay quiet above the court, its seed jars in temporary custody with Sena. The river was still impassable: the proper ferry lost, the punt grounded, the timber rig unapproved for passengers. But an older woman had begun collecting tiles to line a common warming room for those kept from their households by the closed crossing.

The woman had not asked the city to build her a house. She had asked for hands.

## I. Mixon it up for a week and The LLC — An Empty Threshold

The warming room began as a ruin. Its roof had belonged to a storage shed behind the performance court, where costumes had once been kept in boxes and musicians had waited out rain. The shed had a smoke-blackened wall and a floor that tilted toward the orchard. Before the concession dispute, nobody had claimed it because nobody wanted to repair it. The moment volunteers brought tiles and a repaired door, the collector appeared with an assessment slate.

"You cannot tax a wall for remaining upright," Mara said.

"Not the wall," the collector answered. "The improvement."

The old woman who had gathered the tiles called herself Nessa. She had crossed the river as a child on the lost ferry and remembered how a passenger could lean against its side when the wind came off the hills. She now lived in an upper-road room rented by the week. She had no title to the ruined shed, but she knew which stones beneath its threshold became slick when wet.

She stood before the collector, hands on her hips, and asked which improvement his slate intended to recognize. The collector named the door, the tiles, the patched roof and the fuel piled outside.

"The door was broken," Nessa said. "The tiles were broken. The roof leaks. The fuel has been borrowed from people who said what it may warm. If we have improved it enough for your levy, we have improved it enough to make the levy unnecessary."

The collector explained that the law did not work that way. Nessa asked whether he had come to help or to stand in a doorway. He answered that he was performing his duty. She placed a tile in his hands. He looked at it with the same baffled reluctance Jake had felt when Mara gave him the blue bird.

Jake began to defend the collector. He knew the boy, who had worked the gate since summer and had learned to sound older than he was. A legal order did not cease to exist because its bearer was embarrassed. But Nessa was not asking Jake to win an argument. She was asking the collector to hold something heavy while someone else worked.

They divided the tasks without obtaining a municipal charter. Mara inspected the wall and forbade a fire until a competent stove worker could test the draft. Nessa and the widow from the lantern path carried water for cleaning. Jake and the collector moved tiles out of the rain. The child with her mother's shawl sorted the fragments by color, making a river of blue birds across the floor. The pieces were not yet fitted; no promise of a safe completed hearth was made.

The collector's superior arrived before dusk and ordered him back to the gate. He departed with the assessment slate under his arm. He had not recorded an improvement or canceled a charge. Nessa waved to him as if he were simply another worker leaving early.

The day's source-exact competition record was read at the market's separate scoring board: **Mixon it up for a week (home), 167.76**; **The LLC (away), 127.84**. Winner: **Mixon it up for a week (slot 10)**. Margin **39.92**.

Jake wrote those figures only in the historical contest register. The archive result did not license any claim that the real-life people behind those banners had assessed, built or opposed a shelter. As Nessa laid down the last bucket, he felt the first stir of a thought he disliked: perhaps the room whose door he feared losing was less secure than a ruined shed no one had yet been able to claim.

## II. El Niño and Benedict Arnold — The Chimney of Salt and Clay

The stove worker refused to approve the warming room.

His name was Bren. He arrived at sunrise with a soot-dark bag and listened to Mara describe the wall. He examined the smoke stains, peered into the upper opening, and told them that the flue was blocked. If they lit a fire inside, he said, the room might fill with smoke before anyone noticed the change. There were easier ways to make a person cold than by killing him with a heater that could not breathe.

Nessa was angry. She had spent the night persuading three households to donate old cooking bricks. One elderly porter had already carried his blanket into the court, expecting to sleep under a repaired roof. Jake felt his own fingers stiffening in the morning air and almost asked Bren to permit a small trial fire while the door stood open.

Bren turned his face toward him. "Open doors do not make a blocked flue sound," he said.

"You've not heard me ask," Jake replied.

"I've heard people ask for the same thing since I was a boy. They call it a small fire because they want a small answer."

The refusal disappointed Nessa enough to bring tears. She wiped them away before the workers could see. She accused Bren of enjoying the power to say no. He said he would return with the proper inspection tools after the midday round. She said the people in the court had no midday round; they had only the day and the need not to sleep exposed in it.

Jake was tempted to present Bren's caution as a moral triumph. He had learned, expensively, that a declaration of safety could become an instrument of harm. But Bren had not come merely to deliver a warning. He stayed after the other tradesmen left and examined the masonry from outside. He found that a section of the old opening had been sealed with hardened clay, likely to prevent birds from nesting during years when no stove burned there. He could not restore safe draft before night without materials they did not possess. What he could do was help them move the intended warming area beneath the roofed arcade along the courtyard's eastern side, where no fire would be lit.

Mara objected to calling it a warming room. A roof without heat was shelter, she said, and a shelter must not be sold to frightened people as anything else. Nessa took the words harshly until she realized Mara was offering to help build that smaller, honest thing. They laid the tiles as a dry walking surface, not a hearth. They hung cloth to break the wind where the arcade was open, leaving the path clear for the elderly porter to walk out.

The old man tested the new floor with his stick. He told them that he would rather sit beneath a roof than stand in the frost, even if it did not warm his hands. Nessa gave him the blue bird tile to keep by his chair, and he placed it carefully where his stick would not strike it.

The competitive ledger for the second recorded encounter read: **El Niño (home), 169.20**; **Benedict Arnold (away), 107.90**. Winner: **El Niño (slot 9)**. Margin **61.30**.

It was the largest margin of that week's six fixtures, but it proved nothing about any actual storm. El Niño was an archived competitor banner; the cold, smoke, broken flue and shelter in Jake's proposed Commonwealth were inventions. He looked from the exact figures to the old porter testing the tile. Sometimes a very large difference could be measured without telling anyone whether the smaller number was enough.

Before he left, Bren promised to inspect the old flue again when he had safe access and supplies. Nessa did not demand a deadline he could not guarantee. She asked him what she could prepare without endangering anyone. He gave her a list of observations to make, not a recipe for making the hearth safe, and told her to keep the room unlit until inspected.

## III. Red Leopards and Two Dreaded Snake — The Far-Bank Voice

The courier came by the upper road carrying a cloth bag full of stones. He was not sent by the far-bank council, for no such council had been verified to speak on behalf of all the households. He said he had met six people at three doorways, heard a disagreement at a mill, and come away with fewer answers than when he set out.

Some wanted the lender's proposed road opened immediately, whatever the risk of the crossing. Others objected to the toll because it presumed ownership of a route they had used before any debt was registered. A mother told him that arguments over representation mattered less than whether she could reach her sister before the winter store ran low. A carpenter said the new tests should be witnessed by those who would travel, not only by those who would profit from the fare.

The stones were not votes. He had brought them because one household had used them to mark the place where a child should wait for a safe escort along the upper path. The place had changed twice as the path deteriorated. He feared that a paper map, once copied and sold, would fix the old position as though the danger remained stationary.

Jake showed him the broken test timber. The word FAILURE had survived on its end, faint in the grain. The courier asked whether the mark would be copied with the drawings. Mara said it would, because the break mattered as much as the shape of the original board. Then he asked Jake something worse: why did every city account call the far bank a destination, never a place from which someone might depart?

Jake began to answer that he had kept the headings of the older ledger. He stopped. It was the true reason, and its truth did not make it adequate. A destination was somewhere he expected to arrive. A place of departure belonged to people who might choose a journey without him. The city had recorded only its own reach.

The ferryman's daughter joined them on the court wall. The courier asked her whether a future boat, if safely made, would carry people both ways without an official direction assigned to them. She said a boat could travel in either direction. That did not guarantee that the employer paying for it would allow the crew to choose. She had refused the unsafe crossing in the sixth week and was still being called an obstacle by people who had never stood with her at the landing.

The courier took one stone from the bag and put it down beside the broken plank. It was a plain river stone, not a message of agreement. He said it could mark a question rather than an answer: whose risk was being priced? Mara picked it up and felt its weight. She said nothing. Jake wanted a phrase worthy of the moment and found none.

At midday the independent competition record named **Red Leopards (home), 164.18**; **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 126.63**. Winner: **Red Leopards (slot 2)**. Margin **37.55**.

No character in the orchard claimed to be either archived competitor; the real historical owner join remained unknown. The three-headed identity established in much later visual canon was not projected backward as a newly invented 2023 speaker. The numbers entered the scoring book, and Jake returned to the courier's question.

He took a clean sheet and wrote two headings: *Arriving* and *Leaving*. It was a small correction, inadequate to house a family or settle a toll. Underneath he left both columns empty. He would not fill them with names that other people had withheld.

The courier looked at the sheet, folded it without signing, and asked for bread. Jake went with him to Sena, who knew what the ward could spare and what it could not. This time he did not presume to promise either.

## IV. Benedict Coomhersnatch and Benedict Kenobi — The Room Above the Market

The landlord broke the wax strip himself.

He did it in the middle of the afternoon while Jake was carrying water to the arcade. The owner of the building came into the court with a length of the purple paper clinging to his coat, so agitated that he had forgotten to remove it. He had been summoned by the creditor, he said. He was ordered to surrender Jake's room for an inventory before the next public market. He could refuse, but only at the risk of having the debt on his own shop reviewed. He would not ruin his children for a tenant.

Jake did not blame him. That was the most difficult part. The man had given him a room when the city was ordinary, taken modest rent, tolerated the leaking window, and never complained about the late light. None of those past kindnesses had equipped him to defy a lender now. Jake had imagined the landlord as a character in his argument about independence. The man before him was frightened and embarrassed and wanted to get home.

"I can take my things tonight," Jake said.

The landlord shook his head. The inventory was supposed to remain intact. Jake could remove his personal papers but not the furniture. The broken cup, the mended coat, and the blanket belonged to Jake. The bed and the lamp had come with the room. The creditor had named them as fixtures. He asked Jake not to make a scene at the market.

Jake had spent years learning to make orderly distinctions. A fixture from a possession; a score from a judgment; evidence from desire; permission from consent. Here a distinction stripped him of the lamp by which he had read through the darkness. He wanted to protest that no honest inventory could count a bed without counting the sleeping man. But the landlord would be the one made to pay for his cleverness.

Mara found a handcart and came to help him. They climbed the stairs together. She did not make a speech about the law. She wrapped his cup in cloth, carried his books in two loads, and noticed that his coat had been mended at the wrong angle. She had noticed that months earlier, she said, but had not thought herself entitled to say so. Jake told her he had kept the repair because his mother had made it. He had not told anyone that before.

In the room he gathered the ledger last. The old pages had become heavier as the scores and weeks accumulated. For a moment he considered leaving it beneath the floorboards, forcing the steward to live with an archive that he could not identify. Then he remembered the apprentices who had copied the checking method. The book was not the only place the record lived, and an act of hiding would only give the lender another claim of secrecy.

He carried it down the stair in plain view. The landlord looked at the open cover and said he hoped the dispute would end soon. Jake almost answered that it had only begun. Instead he thanked the man for the years of shelter. The landlord accepted the thanks with a face that would not quite lift.

That night brought the fourth competition entry: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (home), 152.72**; **Benedict Kenobi (away), 123.16**. Winner: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (slot 5)**. Margin **29.56**.

In the strictly proposed literary link between Jake and archive slot one, the defeat left him at **4–8** through twelve weeks. He did not attempt to make his eviction a metaphor for the result. There had been other losses at the scoring board that year, and they had not taken his bed. The creditor had acted for reasons inside a fictional economic order, not because of a real league matchup.

Mara brought him to the foundry annex, where a dry storage room could be cleared for two nights if the workers agreed. They did agree, but only for two nights; after that the room would be needed for tools. Jake slept on folded canvas and could smell iron filings whenever someone opened the outer door. He was grateful, and he was ashamed of how quickly gratitude could begin to resemble dependence.

At midnight the foundry apprentice who had corrected his margin knocked quietly and asked how to enter the next result in the public copy. Jake rose from the canvas to show him. He did not need a lamp of his own. The apprentice had brought one.

## V. Arm Chair GM and The KING of the Octagon Village — The Child Who Declined an Ending

The little girl from the ward had finished her play.

She called it *The Man Who Counted Doors*. The title troubled Jake because he recognized himself before she said so. Her hero was a man who kept the keys to every empty room in a village and believed that if he counted enough doors, nobody would be cold. Whenever someone came to ask for shelter, he explained which door was lawful, which belonged to whom, which had a broken latch, and which must remain closed until repairs were authorized. The people slept outdoors while he improved his list.

Jake read the first two pages without smiling. He was not angry at the child. He was startled to discover that she had been watching him much more carefully than he had watched her. He had considered her grief the defining fact of her life. She had also been observing the speech of adults who claimed to know what should happen next.

"Do you give him a good ending?" he asked.

"No," she said. "He would think I agreed with him."

Sena was seated at the ward threshold, sorting medicine packets under a borrowed lamp. She glanced at the pages and told the child that some of the words were misspelled. The child said Jake had promised not to correct them. Sena smiled without approving the spelling. She asked whether the play might have a part for the woman who held the keys.

"There is no woman with keys," the girl answered.

"Then you have another problem," said Sena.

The girl went to the small table and began rewriting. Jake watched her scratch out a scene. She was no longer the girl waiting for her mother to cross a dangerous river. She was an author deciding that the world on her page had not yet earned its ending.

Outside, the old porter was testing the tile walkway in the arcade. He had brought his own stool and set it in the best shelter from the wind. Nessa had promised no one that the new shelter was warm. It was dry, and the porter was learning the route along it in the dusk. A young laborer stopped to help and received a sharp rebuke: the old man wished to know whether he could make the journey unaided. The laborer stood beside him without taking his arm until asked.

Jake took the next public scoring sheet from the messenger and read it aloud where the ward could hear. The record gave **Arm Chair GM (home), 155.06**; **The KING of the Octagon Village (away), 142.72**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **12.34**.

He kept the competition separate from the child's play. The labels were archive banners whose historical owners were not authenticated; none certified a king, general manager, or person in the fictional court. The girl asked whether twelve points meant the loser had almost won. Jake said only that the margin was smaller than some others and larger than some, which told her nothing about the people. She nodded and went back to her page.

At dusk she returned with a new scene. The man who counted doors was no longer the only person on the stage. A woman had come into the village carrying neither a key nor a list. She opened the door that had no lock and waited while the people decided whether to enter. The girl would not tell Jake what happened after that. She had left the last sheet blank.

He wanted her to finish it. He was tired of stories that could not promise rescue. But to insist on an ending would repeat the very habit she was mocking. He handed her the pages and asked whether she needed more paper. She said no. She needed someone to play the woman.

## VI. Eggs Benedict and The Blew Waffle's — A Place to Stand

The creditor's men arrived at the ruined shed on a clear morning. They had seen the new arcade floor and the borrowed cloth, and they wanted to know who was responsible for the structure. Nessa said she was responsible for collecting the tiles. Mara said she had checked which stones were too loose for feet. Bren said he had refused the unsafe fire. The old porter said he had tested the path with his stick. None claimed to own the house.

The steward appeared carrying the same folded survey agreement he had offered Jake. He announced that the city's failure to certify the upper route made the lender's position more precarious and the public debt more urgent. He proposed that the warming space be registered under the creditor's management until the disputed crossing could be restored. In exchange he would supply fuel and allow the people to remain.

Jake felt a sudden stab of desire for the offer. He was sleeping on folded canvas in a room that had to be cleared after two nights. The lender could make the arcade warm. The children might play beneath its roof. People who had volunteered broken wood could stop begging for scraps. A properly heated room seemed an indecent thing to refuse when so many people were cold.

Bren stepped forward and asked which inspected stove the creditor intended to fuel. The steward said the old flue could be repaired. Bren repeated that it had not been approved. Mara asked whose worker would have authority to stop the operation if the repair proved unsafe. The steward said a contractor would be retained. Sena asked whether use of the shelter would require people to acknowledge a debt incurred on the river. He did not answer.

The old porter rose from his stool. He walked slowly over the blue tiles, his stick ticking at each seam. Then he turned and walked back. The visitors waited for him, annoyed. When he reached the place where the roof began, he told them that the room had already given him something real. It had not given him heat. It had given him a way to stand outside the rain without owing an explanation for his poverty.

"Why do you need my name," he asked the steward, "to let me sit where I can breathe?"

The steward answered that every improvement required responsibility. Mara agreed. She pointed to the inspected, unlit hearth, the posted warning and the repairs still needed. Responsibility was not the same as ownership, she said. The workers had undertaken to keep the place safe for its present limited use. Their undertaking did not authorize a lender to add a fare to a river nobody had crossed.

There was no grand uprising. Most of the crowd remained silent. A few said the offered fuel should be accepted, whatever conditions came with it. Others feared the shelter would become another toll station. Jake could not decide for them. He remembered the drilled counter from the far bank, which had come expressly to prevent a city from counting absent people into a convenient majority.

He offered to read the steward's terms aloud and record the objections without pretending that a gathering at the court could speak for all the households. The porter waved him away. "Not another reading," he said. "Sit down and listen to the ones who need it."

Jake sat on the cold tile. Nessa spoke of the woman who had given her the first blanket. A man whose brother worked across the river described the cost of taking the upper road to fetch food. The ferryman's daughter said the punt would remain grounded while it was unsafe, even if the new shelter made the passage seem nearer. Bren described only what he knew about the flue, refusing the steward's invitation to promise a future fire.

The week's final competition notice arrived at the court in the hands of a messenger who had been sheltering from the wind. It read: **Eggs Benedict (home), 146.24**; **The Blew Waffle's (away), 162.98**. Winner: **The Blew Waffle's (slot 7)**. Margin **16.74**.

No competitive winner was made owner of this fictional house, and the archive's distinct Benedict banner did not become an invented family member. Jake entered the result separately. The steward, impatient, folded his unsigned proposal and announced he would return with a revised order. He left without supplying fuel. A refusal of bad terms had not solved the cold.

When the crowd dispersed, Nessa asked Jake where he would sleep. He told her of the foundry annex and the two nights he had been offered. She said she knew a household with a dry loft above the upper market. Its owners might want someone to help carry water in exchange for shelter, but she would ask before promising. Jake thanked her. It was the first time he had accepted such an offer without translating it immediately into a debt.

At the end of the day the old porter remained seated under the roof. He had brought a small wooden flute and was playing a tune that Jake did not recognize. The melody was imperfect. It ended twice before the man was satisfied. Nobody demanded that it become an anthem for the city.

### After the Twelfth Reckoning

By the close of the twelfth week, the lender still claimed the upper road and contested river approach. Jake's old room was subject to an inventory and he no longer slept there. The proper ferry remained lost; the punt stayed grounded; no passenger crossing had been authorized. There was no safe operating hearth in the ruined shed. The arcade offered a dry walking surface and wind shelter under provisional maintenance by the people who had worked on it, not a newly established public institution.

Across the river, the households remained free to reject the city's language about them. The courier had returned with his stones, and nobody had made the absence of their signatures into a political victory. The ward still lacked certainty about fuel, medicine and passage. Sena continued her work. The patient's mother was recovering unevenly, not according to the requirements of the play her daughter had written.

Jake's personal loss was smaller than the grief of the families kept apart by the river. It was nonetheless a loss. His bed stood in a room another person now claimed to inventory, and he did not know where he would sleep after the foundry reclaimed its tool space. A place to work and a place to live had once seemed to him consequences of maintaining a good record. The record survived his tenancy. The tenancy did not survive the steward's displeasure.

At the court, the blue bird lay among the tiled floor's other fragments. The child was rehearsing her play without a finished ending. She had persuaded the widow from the upper road to take the part of the woman without keys. No audience had yet been invited. Jake had not been asked to appear.

The six competition results were all fixed in the historical record. In the proposed slot-one reading, Jake now stood **4–8**. Nothing in that tally demonstrated the justice of the lender's actions or the virtue of the people resisting him. The scoreboard was the archive; the fictional world was the argument, and its people had begun making decisions Jake could neither predict nor possess.

Mara came to the foundry annex at night with a small stitched cloth parcel. It contained his mother's damaged coat, newly turned at the shoulder so the seam no longer pulled. He began to thank her for fixing it. She stopped him.

"I did not fix it," she said. "I made it fit the body you have now."

He tried it on. The repair was not invisible. A row of pale stitches ran along the old line, and his mother's darker thread showed through in places. He had feared the correction would erase her hand. Instead the old work had become more visible because someone else had learned how to carry it forward.

He slept beneath the coat on the folded canvas. In the morning he would help clear the tool room. He did not know where the work would take him afterward. Beyond the walls the river continued its indifferent course, and somewhere across it people were making choices without waiting for him to write them down.

---

**Fiction/source separation:** Week 12 competitive home/away names, scores, winners and margins are archival facts only. The creditor's inspection, threatened room inventory, borrowed tiles, unapproved flue, courier accounts, dramatic writing, decisions, and all speech and psychology are **NONCANON PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not claims about ESPN participants or actual 2023 life. Only Jake has proposed principal interior POV and his association with 2023 slot one remains user-attested, not provider-authenticated. Distinct Benedict archive banners are not human-owner lineage; El Niño never speaks or represents real meteorology. No Week 13+ knowledge appears within the fictional present. Independent editorial, Atlas, Umpire and Founder approval remain outstanding.

# 2023 — Week 13: The Room Beyond the Map
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six archived encounters · NONCANON historical-fiction draft*

**Evidence and creative boundary.** The six Week 13 score-and-banner records below reproduce the archive `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json` (Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`, labeled Flaim ESPN league 1417621). The raw ESPN SHA, historical owner assignments, real venues, transactions and private motives are **UNKNOWN**; home/away is archival ordering, not geographic proof. Jake/slot-one Benedict Kenobi is a **user-attested, not provider-authenticated** literary association. The four similarly named Benedict banners establish no shared human identity or lineage. El Niño is a competitor banner, **not a speaking person or a weather report**. Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`) remains the **sole proposed principal interior POV**; all other figures are fictional civilians seen externally. No Week 14 or postseason result is known inside the story. All rooms, people, court practices, labor, medicine, routes, economic arrangements and weather are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**. The protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III on main are not revised. **NONCANON — no independent Author Council, Atlas, Umpire or Founder approval.**

The girl had written a doorway into her play, but there was no wall around it.

Jake found the drawing on a scrap of butcher's paper beneath the roofed arcade, beside the old porter's flute. A single blue rectangle stood in the middle of the sheet. Beyond it she had drawn a road made of stitches, each black mark too far from its neighbors for anyone to cross without putting a foot into blank space. There were no actors in the picture.

He had come to tell Nessa that the foundry needed its tool room back. She had promised to ask about the dry loft above the upper market, but promises made on behalf of other households were precisely the sort of mercy the past months had taught him to fear. He would not arrive at the loft and announce that a stranger had given it to him. He had bundled the damaged coat his mother once wore into a satchel and prepared to leave the annex by evening.

The girl was sitting at the end of the arcade with her mother. She had been ill in the ward. Now she looked more exhausted by the effort of climbing three steps than by any fever visible upon her face. Her daughter had propped a board across her knees for a desk.

"Who is supposed to stand in the doorway?" Jake asked.

"Nobody, yet," the girl said. "The audience has to see it before anyone can own it."

The mother looked up. "She used to draw the people first."

"People can walk off the paper," the child said. "A door has to stay where you put it."

Jake almost replied that a door was only a hinge and an agreement about who could pass. Then he remembered the strip of white paper on his own lodging, and kept quiet.

The older porter played a brief tune, stopping whenever a note did not please him. In the court below, the unheated shelter stood open to the wind. The ruined shed's flue remained unapproved. The river still divided households: the proper ferry lost, the punt grounded, the creditor's claim over the upper approach disputed. None of those facts had changed because the girl had given absence a shape.

At the market, six contests would be reckoned before the week ended. Jake had a duty to record them exactly, and the separate contest register would do that. But the need that occupied his morning was more humiliating than any public score. He had to ask another household for a place in which to sleep.

## I. Benedict Coomhersnatch and Red Leopards — The Price of a Roof

The owner of the loft was a woman called Adra, who made nets for the orchard keepers and refused to let anybody describe the arrangement as charity. Her workroom occupied the lower floor of a narrow house off the upper market. Twine hung across the rafters in loops so intricate that Jake, who could reproduce the divisions of a debt to the smallest coin, could not tell where one net began.

Nessa had brought him there without first agreeing terms. That was one reason he trusted her.

Adra set a cup of thin soup before him, watched him hesitate, and lifted it away again. "If you want to discuss a room, you may. If you want to turn the soup into the first installment of a debt, drink water instead."

He accepted the soup. She did not look triumphant.

The loft had space for one sleeper provided he did not disturb a length of wet flax stretched near the window. Rain had penetrated the roof during a previous season. Adra had patched it herself, and the repair held except when the wind drove moisture under the western eave. She could promise shelter from ordinary weather but not dryness in every storm. The rent she wanted was two mornings each week carrying buckets from the public pump and an evening's help sorting knots by touch; if he found paid work, she preferred cash. She did not ask what he had once earned.

Jake wanted to refuse the second obligation. It sounded too much like the unpaid labor the creditor had tried to extract from the ferrymen. He began explaining how uncertain valuations could become forms of coercion. Adra listened until he reached the danger of implied entitlement, then laid the damaged net between them.

"Look at the rent you have been offered," she said. "Do you know how to mend this?"

"No."

"Then you cannot promise to finish it. You can learn a knot. You can fetch water. If you become unable to do either, you tell me before you invent a substitute."

She put a cord into his hand. The first knot came apart, and the second bound the wrong strands. He felt the old shame of labor performed badly before someone who had made it look effortless. Adra did not rescue him. She demonstrated once, then left him alone with the mistake. By the time he tied a serviceable third knot, his soup had cooled.

The lender's collector passed outside, asking after repaired buildings on the street. Adra stepped to the door and answered that she had repaired the western eave months ago. No assessment of the roof would make her swear that it never leaked. When the collector asked whether she had taken a new tenant, she said she had taken a man who was learning to carry water. She had not yet decided what he was worth.

Jake was grateful for the defiance and furious at himself for needing it. After the collector left, he asked whether Adra feared a levy on the room. She said she feared wet flax more. A creditor could put a number on her roof; he could not, by that act, make a dishonest knot hold.

The market's first independent competition entry reached him on a folded sheet: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (home), 172.00**; **Red Leopards (away), 179.68**. Winner: **Red Leopards (slot 2)**. Margin **7.68**. Jake copied the names, scores, winner and margin into the separate archival register. Neither banner represented Adra, the collector, or any owner of the proposed loft.

Nessa watched him fold the page. "Did you win your room?" she asked.

"No. I may be allowed to work for it."

"That is not the same thing."

"Not yet."

He spent the afternoon hauling two buckets and learning six more knots. On the stairs, Adra stopped him from carrying a third. "I offered two," she said. "Do not make me the keeper of your appetite to prove yourself useful."

For the first time in weeks, he considered that a boundary could be a gift when the person setting it was willing to obey it as well.

## II. The KING of the Octagon Village and El Niño — The Medicine Cart

Sena came to the market not seeking a judgment but seeking wheels.

The ward's old handcart had shed a pin on the upper road. The crates it carried were empty medicine vessels meant to be cleaned and returned to their owner. A second delivery would not be entrusted to a cart that could not turn safely on the steep lane. Sena had found a carpenter willing to inspect it; she lacked anyone who would carry the empty vessels to his shed without claiming an interest in whatever might eventually come back.

Jake volunteered. Sena gave him a list of six crates, three of which were too heavy to carry alone. She did not praise him. She directed him to the people who had already offered to help.

At the shed they found an apprentice sitting on a broken axle, his wrist bound in a filthy cloth. His master, a small man with tired eyes, had gone to purchase iron. The apprentice had attempted to straighten the pin with a stone and struck his hand. He was embarrassed by the injury, and more embarrassed by the possibility that a delay would cost the ward its next shipment.

Sena unwrapped the cloth, examined the hand briefly and told him it required proper care. She would not diagnose him in front of the others or promise his return to work. She asked someone to accompany him to the ward and told Jake to keep the crates outside until the shed was safe to enter.

"We could move the cart ourselves," Jake said.

"We could also make a second patient," she answered.

He hated how familiar the answer sounded. In the tenth week he had withdrawn an unsafe transport guarantee after the timber rig failed on dry ground. He had been right to do so; he had also begun to fear that every refusal made him smaller. Sena seemed untroubled by the suspicion that caution might resemble cowardice. She was occupied with the apprentice's hand and the day's actual injuries.

When the carpenter returned, he asked who had loosened the axle. Jake said nobody present could answer with certainty. The carpenter looked at the empty crates and counted them in silence. He offered to build a brace, but not to certify the cart for loaded use without seeing the wheel turn under its intended weight. There would be a delay. Sena accepted it, then surprised Jake by asking what work could be done before the test.

They washed and sorted the vessels in an open yard. The apprentices who were fit to work helped without moving the cart. An elderly woman from the ward showed them how to dry the narrow-necked bottles so that a little water did not remain hidden at the bottom. Jake spent an hour holding each one toward the light. The work had no heroic shape. It mattered anyway.

The competition messenger arrived as they finished: **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 145.10**; **El Niño (away), 151.12**. Winner: **El Niño (slot 9)**. Margin **6.02**. El Niño was the recorded competitor banner, not a climate event; the cart, injury, medicine vessels and cold yard were invented, unrelated to the real participants.

Sena read the figures without comment. Then she handed Jake a vessel in which a pale fragment had settled. He had missed it. "One more," she said.

He washed the vessel again. On the far side of the yard the carpenter put his new brace beside the broken pin, visibly separating what he had made from what had failed. Jake remembered the mother at the arcade, who had climbed three steps and needed a place to sit. He wanted the cart to be ready for her sake. Wanting it would not strengthen an axle.

When Sena finally released him, he asked whether the ward would receive what it needed.

"Some of it," she said. "And then there will be another morning."

## III. Mixon it up for a week and Two Dreaded Snake — The Theater of Unpaid Voices

The girl's play had acquired an audience before it had acquired an ending.

Ila, whose lanterns had once illuminated the performance court, had offered to hang two lamps beneath the arcade. She made the offer on the condition that anyone who borrowed them would return the oil. The girl objected. She wanted the stage to be dark when the woman without keys reached the door. Ila told her that darkness meant nothing if the audience could not see a hand reaching for the latch.

They argued in front of Jake without acknowledging him. It pleased him at first. The girl had once used him as a figure in her play, and he had expected, foolishly, that her work would remain a disguised conversation with himself. Now Ila and the child were quarreling about the audience's eyes.

The mother's role was to stand outside the drawn doorway and speak a single line: "Whose room is this when nobody is inside?" She did not want the line. It made the woman appear helpless, she said, as though she had never built a room or opened a door of her own. The girl replied that the line had sounded better when she wrote it.

"It sounded better to whom?" her mother asked.

The girl crumpled the page. For an instant Jake thought she would abandon the whole production. Instead she sat on the floor, smoothed the paper on her knee, and wrote a different line. She held it up for her mother to read. The woman smiled, then shook her head. They began again.

At the back of the court stood a warehouse porter who had lost two days of work when the upper road was closed. He asked who would pay for the performance. Ila explained that there would be no admission fee. He asked whether the lamps would be paid for. She answered that the oil would be returned. He asked whether the mother would be paid for repeating the story of a household that had suffered while the actors argued.

Nobody answered. The girl looked at her mother, suddenly uncertain of a permission she had taken for granted.

The mother set down the script. "Ask me," she said.

"Will you do it?"

"That is not what you need to ask."

The girl was silent. The porter moved to leave, but the woman asked him to remain. She told her daughter that she did not mind acting if she had a say in the words, the hour and whether a scene about sickness was performed at all. She would not be used as evidence that the ward had been saved simply because she could stand upright for ten minutes.

Jake knew then why the drawn doorway had troubled him. A door stood still. A person could withdraw consent.

The porter made his own offer. He would move the benches on the afternoon of the performance, provided the workers were fed and the audience did not block the arcade used by people seeking shelter. Ila promised two lamps, no more. The child wrote these terms on the back of the damaged page, leaving space for her mother to change them.

The third contest entry, by contrast, had nothing provisional about it: **Mixon it up for a week (home), 187.98**; **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 128.32**. Winner: **Mixon it up for a week (slot 10)**. Margin **59.66**. The unusually wide numerical result belonged to the archived contest only. It conferred no right to speak for the fictional mother, actress, porter, child or creditor.

Jake made his entry and turned back. The girl had put a stool at the threshold of the painted door. In rehearsal the mother sat upon it rather than passing through. Nobody had agreed what happened next. That, for the first time, was part of the design.

## IV. The LLC and Arm Chair GM — What the Mill Would Not Grind

A sack of grain arrived at the orchard with a hole cut neatly beneath its cord.

The cut was small enough that the sack could be carried without spilling much, but a hand placed beneath it came away dusted white. Sena thought mice had worried the woven fibers. Mara said no mouse she had known made a straight seam. The miller denied tampering with any grain consigned to the upper market. The creditor's steward denied that a claim on the road had anything to do with the damaged sack.

Jake was asked to inspect it because he kept records. He said the bag could be described and weighed; its cause could not be certified from the mark alone. He felt the impatient silence that followed. Everybody knew the difference between evidence and suspicion. They had come because they wanted him to say what the evidence permitted them to believe.

The orchard keeper opened the sack in front of three witnesses. Most of its grain was dry, but a seam of damp material lay against one side. The keeper refused to mix it with the sound stores until it could be inspected. A woman who had traveled all morning for flour demanded to know whether they intended to let children go hungry while they examined a thread.

"No," the keeper said. "We intend to keep this from making them sick."

The woman struck the table with her palm. The blow made a small cloud of flour rise. She was right about the hunger. The keeper was right about the risk. Jake could not distribute the truth into portions that would nourish either of them.

A younger mill hand had been watching from the doorway. He said the bag had been repacked when the storehouse door jammed, and that he had used a knife to open the old cord. He had not noticed the wet grain. His employer demanded why he had not spoken sooner. The boy answered that a worker who admitted one mistake was usually charged with all the others.

Mara asked him to show how the bag had been lifted. He demonstrated, and the seam gaped where the weight had pulled it. That did not establish when moisture entered. But it was enough to move the argument away from invisible enemies and toward an actual handling failure. The miller agreed to examine the rest of the consignment, with the keeper present, before grinding any part of it.

The hungry woman watched the decision with a face that made Jake ashamed of the comfort of being correct. He offered to walk to another storehouse and ask what could be released that day. She declined his company but accepted directions. There were other people whose grain might be needed; none had promised it. She knew better than to treat his description of a road as its delivery.

The contest board recorded the fourth pairing: **The LLC (home), 167.38**; **Arm Chair GM (away), 126.54**. Winner: **The LLC (slot 4)**. Margin **40.84**. Those figures were not testimony concerning real grain, culpability or a mill owner's decisions. The mill, handling error, hunger, and storehouse dispute were strictly proposed Commonwealth fiction.

As evening fell, the younger hand brought Jake the knife. He said his employer wanted it sealed as evidence. Jake refused possession and asked instead whether the hand wanted to surrender it. The answer was no: it was the only good knife he owned.

"Then keep it," Jake said. "We can record its shape without taking away your work."

The boy left before he could be thanked. The miller had not yet finished sorting the grain. The woman had not returned with flour. Jake knew his refusal had not filled either absence. It had prevented a third one.

## V. Benedict Kenobi and The Blew Waffle's — A Victory Too Narrow to Carry

The loft leaked on Jake's first night.

A thin bead of water appeared beneath the repaired western eave and traced a line down the plaster. He found Adra awake below, sorting nets by the last light of a shielded lamp. She heard him on the stairs and asked whether the roof had failed.

"Not the whole roof," he said. "Only the place you warned me about."

She brought up a bowl and showed him where to place it without touching the wet flax. It sounded simple. He wanted to offer an elaborate patch, perhaps a survey of the eave and a list of materials. Adra said she would inspect the repair when the weather allowed safe access. There was nothing useful to do on the roof in darkness.

They sat together while the drops fell. Below, the sound of a cart moved through the sleeping market. Jake expected Adra to ask about the river. Everybody asked about the river. Instead she asked why he carried a coat folded more carefully than his own clothes.

He told her it had belonged to his mother and that Mara had changed the shoulder seam. He showed the older stitches beneath the new ones. Adra took the fabric only after he offered it. She examined the line and said the person who made the correction had known when to stop pulling.

Jake wondered whether his mother would have approved the repair, but he had no authority to answer for her. He had kept the coat so long that its deterioration had come to seem like fidelity. Mara had made it wearable by refusing to pretend the old thread was indestructible.

Adra said she could show him how to keep the seam clean. She did not offer to take the coat away and improve it further. He thanked her for the limit.

At dawn he crossed the market to the contest board. One result had been pinned crookedly beneath the others, and two people were arguing over its small difference. The archive said: **Benedict Kenobi (home), 144.76**; **The Blew Waffle's (away), 143.72**. Winner: **Benedict Kenobi (slot 1)**. Margin **1.04**. For the proposed user-attested Jake/slot-one lineage, that made a fifth win against eight losses after thirteen weeks. The score was an exact archived competitive fact; the identity link was still unverified, and no real-life intent or character action followed from the win.

He checked the subtraction twice. A little more than one point. For an hour he allowed himself to enjoy it.

At the shelter he found the girl rehearsing with her mother. She had crossed out the line about ownership. The new line said, "I will open the door when you tell me who is on the other side." Her mother delivered it without raising her voice, and the girl nodded. The scene had become less impressive and more difficult to evade.

A courier from the far bank stood at the edge of the gathering, mud on his boots and a sealed bag under his arm. He had come to retrieve a list of medicines still needed across the river; he had no mandate to sign a toll agreement. He was not a delegate for every household. He said the proposed volunteer light stations on the upper road had been helpful to some travelers, but that other people feared the stations would become posts for collection.

Jake started to explain that the lights had not been authorized as toll structures. The courier lifted a hand. "I know what you said they were. I am telling you how they were seen."

They went to the end of the arcade where a map of the proposed upper road hung beside the unlit hearth warning. Jake marked the location of each light according to the reports he possessed, leaving gaps where he had none. The courier drew a circle around one lamp and said that it stood at a place people did not wish to pass at night. The safest route might be longer, and Jake's neat line had made it seem foolish.

"Then the line is wrong," Jake said.

"Perhaps it was right for the person who walked it. Not for us."

Jake took down the map. The courier did not instruct him to burn it. He asked only that it not be copied as a universal route. For a moment Jake considered his narrow victory, the scoreboard with a single point separating a winner from a loser, and the larger distances he had spent the season pretending were equally simple to measure.

He did not surrender his pleasure in the result. He did surrender the assumption that pleasure entitled him to be believed.

## VI. Benedict Arnold and Eggs Benedict — The Night of the Empty Stage

The public performance was scheduled for dusk, and by midday the benches had still not arrived.

The porter had gone to move them, only to find his employer needed him at the grain store. He had not broken a promise. He had told the child that he could help if he was available, and the child had written his name into her plans as though availability were a kind of possession. She was furious. Ila pointed out that anger would not carry benches.

Jake offered to fetch them himself. The mother stopped him. She said the porter had offered help as a person, not as a supply of muscle that might be replaced without the courtesy of asking. She sent the girl to learn whether he could spare an hour. They waited.

The delay thinned the audience. The first cold gust came through the unheated arcade as the lamps were lit. Nessa worried about the elderly people who had gathered on the edge of the court; the porter had been the one who knew how to make a safe passage among the seats. Ila moved the lamps higher to keep the walking path clear. Bren inspected the temporary fixtures for obvious hazards, but he would not certify the ruined shed's blocked flue, which remained unlit.

Eventually the girl returned. The porter had said no. He would not leave the grain store that afternoon, and he did not want the performance canceled on his account. He had suggested using the low stone ledge instead of benches.

She stood in the court holding the message as though it were a defeat. Her mother asked whether she still wanted to perform.

"I don't know if it will look right."

"Then we will know what it looks like when we do it."

The play began with a painted door and a woman standing outside it. There were no magnificent costumes. A boy carrying a sack pretended to be a road. An older woman placed a tile on the ground and declared that it was a place to rest. The girl spoke the part of a clerk who could measure the road but could not tell who was missing from it. Jake recognized words he had said, rearranged until they no longer belonged to him. He did not object.

At the new line the mother stood still. "I will open the door when you tell me who is on the other side."

No one answered. The girl had removed the answer from the script. A child in the audience called out that he was on the other side. Then the old porter called out his name. Others spoke, not as a council declaring themselves unanimous, but as people testing whether the silence had been left for them. Some did not speak. One woman rose and walked away.

The actor playing the road dropped his sack. Dust rose in the lamplight, and the girl laughed in surprise. Her mother stayed outside the painted door. She did not open it. The play ended there.

The final competition entry had meanwhile been delivered to the scorekeeper: **Benedict Arnold (home), 89.18**; **Eggs Benedict (away), 103.30**. Winner: **Eggs Benedict (slot 12)**. Margin **14.12**. Jake filed the exact numbers in the historical register. Those archived competitor banners neither spoke during the play nor identified any actor, porter or household in the fictional court. The distinct Benedict names did not license a family history.

The audience broke up slowly. A few people said the ending had disappointed them. Others asked whether there would be another performance. The girl answered that she had not decided. That response pleased her mother more than any applause.

Adra found Jake as he was putting away the record sheet. She carried a small loop of twine and showed him the knot he had tied that morning. It had held, she said, though one strand was still too loose. Tomorrow she would show him where to pull.

"How was the play?" she asked.

"Nobody opened the door."

"Then why are you smiling?"

"Because it stayed theirs to open."

He walked back through the market with his coat across his arm. A result of 144.76 to 143.72 was still his evening's small triumph in the proposed slot-one story. But the court had given him a different measure of success: a mother could change the words, a porter could refuse the work, a child could end the scene before the audience received the answer it wanted. The room beyond the map was occupied by people who had not asked him to draw them.

### After the Thirteenth Reckoning

By the end of the thirteenth week, the archived regular-season slate had six additional exact results, bringing the draft Chronicle to **78 of 84** documented regular-season pairings. The proposed Jake/slot-one record stood **5–8**, pending provider authentication of that owner association. The competition had not granted Jake any fictional property, legal authority, victory in the debt dispute, or knowledge of Week 14.

Adra had conditionally taken him into the loft above her net shop, with work and rent described plainly rather than disguised as patronage. Its roof could still leak. The foundry annex was reclaimed for tools. The old room remained under the creditor's threatened inventory. Jake's mother's coat had been repaired, not restored to an imagined untouched condition.

The proper ferry remained lost; the punt grounded; no passenger crossing had reopened. The contested concession, far-bank consent, workers' compensation and medicine-supply problem were unresolved. The ruined shed still had **no safe operating hearth**; only the sheltered arcade remained in limited use. The grain inspection did not become proof of deliberate sabotage. The ward cart had not been approved for loaded use. The upper-road light stations could be helpful to some and coercive in appearance to others. No fictional official was permitted to mistake those different experiences for a single consensus.

The girl's play ended with its door closed, by the choice of its actors. She had not solved the Commonwealth's law. She had learned something more important for the next telling: people whose stories she borrowed were entitled to change or withhold them.

Jake laid the contest register on Adra's table, where the bowls had been cleared. He did not spread the route map beside it. That map would have to be drawn again with the courier's objections preserved rather than erased. He took up the damp net and tried the knot she had shown him. The cord slipped once, then caught.

In the upper room the water had stopped dripping into the bowl. He could not tell whether the repair had held or the wind had changed. He left the bowl beneath the stain.
