# 2023 — Week 14: The Letters That Would Not Cross
*Commonwealth Historical Chronicle · six source-scored encounters · substantial NONCANON fictional continuation*

**Competitive evidence and story permissions.** Only the six archived Week 14 pairings, home/away labels, scores and winners are factual historical competition records, drawn from `living-novel/production/full-rewrite-2026/evidence/G01_2023_FULL_WEEKLY_MATCHUPS.json`, pinned Git blob `ded119cde5c660f87f197f269b8d6ddd2a11316a`, tagged Flaim ESPN league 1417621. The **raw ESPN SHA is unavailable**; actual historical owner identities, league transactions, physical host locations and private motives are **UNKNOWN**. The suggested Jake/Benedict Kenobi slot-one identity remains **user-attested, not provider-authenticated**. The four Benedict banners are separate archival team labels, not one verified family or person; El Niño is **a team banner, not a human speaker or real weather**. Jake (`CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER`) is the **only proposed principal interior POV**; everyone else is an externally observed fictional Commonwealth inhabitant. The invented city, river, law, material culture, weather, travel, economics and dialogue are **PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not statements about real owners. The story knows results through Week 14 only, **not any postseason match, champion or subsequent year**. The protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III are untouched. **NONCANON / Author Council, Atlas, independent Umpire and Founder HOLD.**

The first letter had no name on it.

It had traveled through the upper market in a sack marked *Unclaimed*, alongside a torn net, a wax tablet and a child's shoe. The messenger left the sack beneath Adra's workbench because the official sorting office had stopped accepting parcels for the abandoned approach to the river. No rule required Adra to look inside. She opened it because she recognized the torn net as one she had made, a pattern that could be returned to its owner if the owner still wanted it.

Jake was fetching water when she called him over. On the letter, someone had written: *For the person who carries the road away in ink.* There was no seal. No address. Nothing that established whether it belonged to him.

"Could be you," Adra said.

"Could be anyone who writes."

"Then it can belong to anyone who decides to open it."

He reached for the paper and stopped. He had spent much of the season insisting that a person's absence was not consent. He knew how to say it at meetings, how to defend it before angry lenders and impatient builders. A nameless letter made the proposition small enough to hold between two fingers. That made it harder.

Adra put it on the table without opening it. She asked him to finish the water before deciding what to do.

Across the quarter, the child's play had left a painted door leaning against the performance court wall. The mother who had stood before it in the thirteenth week had gone back to the ward to collect a fresh bandage for a neighbor. The elderly porter had taken his flute home; the blue bird tile remained by his chair. Under the roofed arcade the stove was still unlit, its blocked flue unapproved. Beyond the market the disputed road led toward a crossing no passenger had been authorized to use. The proper ferry was lost; the small punt remained grounded.

It was the last regular-season reckoning in the archive Jake would record that year. That was a fact about the schedule, not a prophecy about the future. He had six exact competitions to enter. He did not know what their totals would be, and he did not know who had written the letter.

## I. Benedict Kenobi and El Niño — A Name That Cannot Receive Mail

The creditor's collector came for the inventory of Jake's former room before the sun had cleared the highest roof.

He did not arrive with soldiers. Two assistants carried a measuring rod, a slate and enough string to bind a bundle of bedding. The landlord followed them up the stairs, looking not at Jake but at the strip of paper that still divided door from frame. Jake had come because the landlord asked whether he wished to recover any property left behind.

The only thing he had left was a broken cup, a stack of papers from an early road survey, and a hand-sized wooden box whose lid had warped in damp weather. He had forgotten the box. The landlord thought the steward might call it part of the room.

Jake found that he could not speak when the collector lifted it. The box had held nothing valuable for years. It had once been a place to put small, embarrassing proofs of affection: a button from his mother's coat, a folded paper given by a friend, a nail that someone had declared lucky. Most of those things were gone. The box had remained because he had never learned to throw it away.

The collector asked whether the object could be entered as a fixture.

"It has never been fixed to anything," Jake said.

"Then identify its owner."

Jake opened his mouth to say his own name. The collector was waiting with the slate poised; all he needed was a declaration, something certain enough to make the object administratively simple. Jake's hesitation enraged him. He was the owner of the box. He had every right to claim it. Why did a clear truth now feel like an invitation to surrender the rest of his history?

He answered. The collector wrote the name and handed him the box. It took less than a minute. The room did not become his again.

The landlord stood in the hall afterward and asked why Jake had not contested the inventory more forcefully. Jake said a box was not a tenancy. The landlord replied that he knew the difference, which was why he had asked Jake to come before everything was sold or stored.

Jake understood the rebuke. He had made the man into a frightened tool of the lender because the narrative had room for such a figure. The landlord was frightened, and he had made his compromises; he was also trying, clumsily and too late, to preserve one small belonging. The two facts did not cancel each other.

The collector's assistants carried out the bedding. Jake watched the room empty, noticing that the old stain beneath the window remained. He had thought a record could save a place by naming its violations. Now he saw the stain he had described in six careful complaints, unchanged after the person who filed them had moved away.

A bell rang at the market when the first contest was posted. The exact archive read: **Benedict Kenobi (home), 135.12**; **El Niño (away), 161.98**. Winner: **El Niño (slot 9)**. Margin **26.86**. The losing slot-one banner had a plausible user-attested Jake association, still not confirmed by the provider. The El Niño banner was not a storm blowing through the invented city, nor a person who had seized his room. Jake made the competitive entry without treating the result as an explanation for the inventory.

He returned to Adra's shop carrying the wooden box. She offered a shelf for it, then asked if he wanted the shelf. He said yes. There was no letter for the person named on the slate, he thought. At least not one he could recognize.

## II. Two Dreaded Snake and Eggs Benedict — The Rope Between Hands

Mara had brought the ferryman's daughter a coil of new rope. It lay on the ground beside the grounded punt, untouched.

The daughter had asked for rope to replace a frayed line that held the empty craft against the landing. She had not asked for help launching it. Mara had brought the length specified, then waited while the daughter examined the twist and judged where the fibers had been stored. The rope was serviceable. The price was not.

The maker wanted payment before it was cut; the ferryman's family had no unrestricted money left after the losses of the earlier weeks. A creditor's agent stood nearby offering to advance the cost. He required a signature acknowledging that the improved landing formed part of the concession. The daughter declined.

It was an argument everyone knew by now. The new fact was that the old line had begun to wear against a stone. It could not remain forever because the dispute had not ended. A broken mooring might send the empty punt away, and then even a future legal settlement would have no craft to test.

Jake had come with Adra to collect a length of discarded twine suitable for net repairs. He offered to copy the rope specifications. The daughter said she did not need another copy. She needed the line secured, the maker paid, and an agreement that neither action would be construed as consent to an unsafe voyage or to the lender's claim.

The agent suggested that she was concealing a profitable service from the public. She replied that an empty boat tied to a bank earned nothing. The agent said the city must not be kept hostage to one family's stubbornness. She asked which of his employers would place their own children aboard an untested punt.

He did not answer. Nor did she ask him a second time.

Mara proposed a smaller arrangement: the maker could supply only enough rope to secure the idle hull; the rest would remain on his shelf until a separate purchase was possible. Adra offered to buy the unused end for her nets if it suited her work, without paying the concession fee or pretending her purchase settled a river claim. The daughter agreed to test the mooring under supervision from the bank. The maker refused to release any length without partial payment. He was not a villain for wanting to eat.

They walked to his shop and laid the terms before him without promising a future contract. He inspected Adra's intended use and said the rope was too stiff for a fine net. She did not purchase it merely to rescue their plan. Instead the maker found an offcut of a different grade and named a price for that alone. Mara contributed part of the cost, on the express understanding that it was a personal purchase, not a tax or a permanent duty. The daughter accepted the offcut for a temporary safety tie after inspecting it. The longer, better rope remained unsold.

No one celebrated. What they had acquired would keep an idle craft from drifting, not make it seaworthy. The agent left with his papers. He was still free to argue about concession rights another day.

The second contest entry was delivered to the bank in a dry pouch: **Two Dreaded Snake (home), 148.22**; **Eggs Benedict (away), 152.90**. Winner: **Eggs Benedict (slot 12)**. Margin **4.68**. The archived figures identified neither the ferryman's daughter nor any real owner of a rope. They created no obligation to pay the fictional maker and no proof of a legal victory.

The daughter attached the temporary line and stood watching the punt tug against it. When Jake offered to stay until night, she said she would arrange her own watch. He felt himself being dismissed and understood, a moment later, that she was asking him to trust her work. He left while the boat still held.

## III. The KING of the Octagon Village and The Blew Waffle's — The Flour That Came Late

The mill's inspected flour arrived in the upper market after the people who had waited for it had gone.

There were three sacks, not the twelve that had been expected. The younger mill hand led the cart with his uninjured arm, keeping his bandaged companion away from the traces. The carpenter had repaired the cart's axle for a trial run with a modest load; no one presented the test as approval for the heavier medicine deliveries. The miller had refused to grind the damp portion of the earlier consignment. A little good flour was better than a false abundance, he said, though the hungry woman who had struck the table during the thirteenth week might not have thanked him for the phrase.

Sena stood beside Jake when the sacks were opened. The grain smelled clean to him, but he did not claim to know how to judge flour for safety. A baker who had volunteered the inspection sifted a small sample and said she would use it, with ordinary precautions, for a limited baking. The remainder would be stored dry until the kitchens could account for it.

A child from the lower market asked why the bread was not ready. Her father, a man with cracked hands, explained that grain could not become bread just because it had been needed for two days. The child said she was tired of explanations. He put a hand on her shoulder and waited without answering. Jake admired the restraint and knew it came from having nothing adequate to say.

The mother from the play appeared, carrying a basket of cloth; the playwright walked beside her, holding a folded script. The woman had come to help distribute bread vouchers to households that could not wait at the ovens. She refused to let the clerk set her daughter at the desk merely because she could read and write. The girl was an actor, she said, not an unpaid office.

The clerk flushed. He apologized to the child and asked whether she wanted to help for an hour. She said no. Then she reconsidered and offered to carry the blank slips to the kitchens, where she could see how many people were waiting. Her mother agreed only after hearing the route and the hour of return.

By afternoon an argument had broken out over the order in which the vouchers would be honored. One kitchen wanted to serve elderly people first, another those who had traveled from distant roads. The old porter asked whether being old meant he could push past a woman who had walked all morning with her child. She answered that it depended whether he could still stand. He told her he could, though not pleasantly. They made room for both. It was not a principle that would survive every shortage.

Jake volunteered to copy the list of households that had actually collected bread, but only as a record of what happened that afternoon. He refused to call it a map of everyone in need. The mother approved the distinction and asked him to help carry a tray. His hands were empty, and he did.

The posted competitive record remained wholly separate: **The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 128.04**; **The Blew Waffle's (away), 168.44**. Winner: **The Blew Waffle's (slot 7)**. Margin **40.40**. The wide result was not caused by the flour delay and proved nothing about real food distribution or the real league members behind the team labels.

At dusk the hungry woman from the previous week returned. A loaf had been set aside for her household under the kitchen's temporary rules. She accepted it without thanking anyone for making her wait. Jake thought that fair. Her child broke the warm crust before they reached the street.

The girl who had written the play watched them go. "We should not put that in the second act," she said.

"Why not?" her mother asked.

"Because it belongs to them."

Her mother nodded. Jake, who had spent so long wanting a complete account, felt a strange relief at the thought of a story deliberately left untold.

## IV. Arm Chair GM and Red Leopards — The Ledger of Unpaid Hours

The ferryman's daughter brought her workers' hour sheets to the court because the creditor's steward had announced another meeting there.

For thirteen weeks the argument had circled around who might authorize a river crossing. The new demand was cruder: the men and women who had inspected the landing, carried timber, patched road stones and watched the grounded punt wanted their work accounted for without accepting the lender's disputed concession. Several had done the labor voluntarily; others had been promised compensation by people who did not actually control a fund. The sheets did not agree about the difference.

Jake was asked to reconcile them. He declined to pronounce a final sum without hearing the people involved. The workers were tired of being heard. One woman said she had appeared at every meeting since the ferry was lost, and each time another clerk had invented a question whose answer was already written somewhere.

She placed her hands on the table. The nails had split from carrying stone. She did not ask whether her suffering looked convincing. She asked whether the hours marked on the sheet were hers.

Jake read them back. She corrected two entries. The steward disputed the rate; no one disputed that she had been there. The dispute was still enough to keep the money unpaid.

The creditor's agent offered to settle a portion of the claim immediately in exchange for a general receipt acknowledging the improved landing as concession property. The woman asked whether the receipt would also bind her sister across the river, who had worked on the far bank. The agent said the document governed only signatories. Mara observed that a form could govern nonsignatories indirectly if their labor was included in the value being conveyed. The room became very quiet.

The ferryman's daughter took back the sheets. She had come hoping for payment; she would not trade a disputed right of passage for a partial wage. She told the workers they could choose individually whether to accept the agent's terms. She would not certify that the choice was free of pressure when the ward needed medicine and their families needed flour.

One of the younger workers signed. He said his father required medicine, and he could not afford the dignity of an unpaid principle. Nobody called him a traitor. The agent handed him the stated amount. The woman with split nails did not sign. Others asked for time to read the document elsewhere.

Jake knew the receipt might be used in a later argument to suggest collective consent. He wrote a separate note naming exactly who had signed and what questions had been left unanswered, then offered each party a chance to inspect the wording. The younger worker demanded that his reason for signing not appear. Jake crossed out the explanation. It was not evidence of a contract term; it belonged to the man who had spoken it.

At the contest board, the fourth exact pair had been fixed: **Arm Chair GM (home), 165.36**; **Red Leopards (away), 136.44**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **28.92**. No team banner was the fictional agent, woman, worker or lender. The result did not authorize the use of a real person's private financial motives as story material.

The meeting ended without a collective agreement. One worker had money; others remained unpaid. The concession was still disputed. The daughter's refusal had not saved everyone from needing a lesser bargain. Jake could not decide whether he admired her more for holding a boundary or for admitting aloud that another person might not be able to hold it.

As they carried the sheets away, the woman with split nails handed the younger worker a small parcel of bread. He tried to refuse it. She told him to eat before he began explaining himself.

## V. Benedict Coomhersnatch and The LLC — The Net With Two Ends

Adra caught Jake trying to redraw the upper-road map beneath her window.

He had spread thin paper across the table, weighted it with the warped wooden box from his former room, and begun marking the light stations from the thirteenth week. The courier's objections were written on a separate page. He had decided to produce two maps: one showing what the city claimed to know, and one showing the routes people actually used.

Adra inspected the work and pointed to the second title.

"How do you know the routes people actually use?"

"The courier told me some."

"Some is not the whole of 'people'."

He felt the sting before he heard the sense. He had made a better map, then given it the same arrogant title in softer ink. Adra went back to her nets while he crossed out the heading.

She showed him a repair in which two lengths of cord entered from different sides. Each bore tension, but neither could be substituted for the other without changing the whole weave. He tried to describe the pattern as a lesson in political representation. She told him that it was a net, and if he wanted to understand it he should finish the knot.

He did. It took longer than a speech.

Later the courier arrived with a bundle of letters sent by households who had used the long upper route. Some were sealed. Others had been left open because the writers could not afford wax or did not care for privacy. No list proved that every household on the far bank had participated. The courier insisted that he had received the bundle from particular doorways, not from a council empowered to represent an entire shore.

Jake laid out the letters without reading them. One bore the same awkward phrase as the note found in the unclaimed sack: *For the person who carries the road away in ink*. He asked whether the courier knew who had written it.

"I know who handed it to me," the courier said. "That is not necessarily the writer."

"What am I supposed to do with it?"

"Ask whether it has found its reader."

Jake showed him the unclaimed letter, still sealed only by its folds. The courier examined the handwriting but would not certify that it came from the same person. Two hands could imitate a style; a single hand could change with weather, pain or haste. He suggested returning it by the route it had come if no intended reader could be named. That journey might take weeks and might fail. It was nonetheless a better answer than opening the paper and claiming its contents as public testimony.

The fifth competition entry was concise: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (home), 142.80**; **The LLC (away), 116.34**. Winner: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (slot 5)**. Margin **26.46**. The banner's Benedict wording did not connect it to Jake or to the separate Benedict Arnold and Eggs Benedict entrants. It did not identify the fictional courier or the writer of the letter.

Adra asked Jake why he looked disappointed. He said he had hoped the letter would tell him how to mend the road. She looked at the map, then at the rope in his hand.

"You may have mistaken a question for instructions."

He folded the maps separately. On the first he wrote *Reports observed from the city*. On the second: *Routes described by named informants, incomplete*. He left the space beyond the last reported light station blank. The marks did not become an Atlas-approved geography merely because the headings were more modest. They had become, at least, honest about their limits.

## VI. Mixon it up for a week and Benedict Arnold — The Unopened Door

The girl announced a second performance without inviting Jake to speak.

She had revised the painted doorway so that the door stood partly ajar. Her mother refused to act the same part again. Not because she was too weak, she said, but because she wanted her daughter to learn that a character could remain absent without the actress having failed anyone. The girl argued; the mother held her ground. In the end the girl took the role herself.

There were fewer spectators at dusk than on the first night. Families had food to prepare, wages to pursue, sick relatives to tend and long roads to walk. Ila supplied a single lantern. The older porter came with his flute and a stool. Nessa brought blankets but warned the people seated beneath the arcade that the shed's hearth remained unsafe and unlit. Bren had not certified it. The shelter's limited dryness was still the only promise the volunteers could make.

Jake sat near the back, holding the sealed letter in the pocket of his mother's coat. The new seam Mara had made pulled neither at his neck nor his shoulder. He had carried the letter all day, considering the urge to become its intended reader by sheer force of curiosity. He had not opened it.

On the little stage the child stood before the painted door and said, "I have a road that reaches every house."

The porter played a wrong note deliberately. She turned toward him.

"Does it reach yours?" he asked.

The girl answered that she did not know where his house was. The audience laughed gently. She looked troubled, then smiled. "It cannot reach a house I have not asked about," she said.

A woman called from the side that some houses preferred to remain unlisted. The girl nodded. She did not try to persuade the woman otherwise. The play continued with a road made of cloth strips, each held by a different volunteer. When one actor let go, the road sagged. The others had to decide whether to lift their ends higher or stop pretending the path was continuous. They stopped. The child moved between the gaps without declaring them bridges.

Her mother watched from the audience. Jake could not know what she felt. He saw only that she remained for the ending, which the daughter had not shared beforehand.

The child approached the painted doorway and placed the sealed paper from her own prop table beside it. She said, "This letter is for someone whose name I have not learned."

"Then it is not mine," replied the actor beyond the door.

"How do you know?"

"I don't."

The girl turned to the audience. She had written no answer. No one called one out. After a long pause she stepped away from the door, leaving the letter unopened. The play ended not with an entrance but with the actor free to remain unseen.

The last competition result of the archived regular season was recorded outside the court: **Mixon it up for a week (home), 158.82**; **Benedict Arnold (away), 119.15**. Winner: **Mixon it up for a week (slot 10)**. Margin **39.67**. The names and numbers were source-exact competitive history, not actor identities, climatic events, or fictional legal verdicts.

Jake entered them with the other five pairings. The archive's fourteen regular-season weeks were now exhausted, and the proposed slot-one association ended **5–9**. That was a record he could count. He could not infer a future playoff bracket, champion or final league disposition from the six scores he had just written. Those records had to be separately recovered.

When the audience left, he asked the girl why she had not given the letter to the actor.

"Because I don't know what it says," she replied.

"You could have written it yourself."

"Then it would only be a thing I wanted someone else to read."

She took down the painted door with her mother's help. The audience had not applauded loudly. It was a smaller performance than the first. Yet the child had resisted the easiest ending, the one in which an answer arrived merely because the story required one.

Jake walked out beneath the unheated arcade, where the blue bird tile lay beside the porter's stool. He left it undisturbed. The courier would depart before morning by the long upper route. There was still time to return the nameless letter without reading it.

### After the Fourteenth Reckoning — Regular Season, Not a Crown

The historical archive verifies **84 of 84 regular-season pairings across fourteen scoring weeks** for 2023. This Chronicle now has fictional draft coverage for all eighty-four. The source does **not** thereby establish the postseason bracket, the champion, a complete transaction register, human-owner identities or any physical venues. Those require separate primary evidence. No champion appears in this fiction, and no future season is anticipated by its characters.

Jake's proposed slot-one competitive record closed **5–9**, contingent on user-attested, not provider-authenticated ownership. In the city that existed only in this draft, his old room remained inventoried under pressure from a creditor. He lived conditionally in Adra's loft and had begun to earn an honest place there by learning work he once considered too small to matter. Adra's roof still required safe repair. His mother's coat carried two sets of visible stitches.

The river had not been reopened. The proper ferry was lost; the punt grounded under a temporary safety line; no passenger crossing was certified. Some laborers had received partial individual payments under disputed concession language, others had not. The city and far-bank households had never signed a common settlement. The route lights were useful to some and threatening to others. The medicine cart still required a stronger load test; the mill had not converted its shortage into abundance. The arcade remained an **unheated shelter**; the shed's hearth was unapproved. These persistent conditions are proposed story continuity, not verified league consequences or Atlas-approved geography.

At dawn Jake gave the unclaimed letter back to the courier, with an account of where it had been found. He did not ask the courier to deliver it to a person neither knew. The courier agreed to carry it toward the place where the bundle had first been gathered and inquire, without opening it, whether anyone recognized its outer marks. There was no promise that the errand would succeed.

In Adra's loft, Jake placed the warped box on its shelf. Beside it he left the two maps and a note reading *These are not the same road.* He checked the bowl beneath the leak. It was empty for now, though the eave was not repaired. Downstairs Adra had begun another net, and he could hear the familiar small sounds of cord pulled across her table.

He went down to learn the next knot. The city did not wait for him to finish.

Everything about the town, river, creditor, letters, ward, grain, rope, worker payments, landlord, map, play, weather, choices, and thoughts is **NONCANON PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not biographical reporting or ESPN history. Jake alone is proposed owner POV. The four separate Benedict banners establish no family relation, and El Niño remains a competition banner, not a character. The archive's home/away ordering proves no geographic location. Historical human-owner verification, independent Author Council, Atlas, Umpire, Founder canon approval and publication remain **HOLD**. Neither postseason result nor champion is invented.

# 2023 Postseason, First Reckoning — The Roof That Held No Sky

**DRAFT / NONCANON — historical Atlas and independent editorial HOLD.** A proposed historical Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER limited viewpoint. Archived ESPN/Flaim labels and scoring results are evidence, not biographies. The episode follows the 2023 regular-season Chronicle without changing it.

The first rain after the fourteenth reckoning came without the courtesy of wind.

It was a thin, steady rain, the kind that did not frighten animals or make a person run for shelter. It hung between the stones and the roofs until every surface surrendered. Jake had seen a storm dismantle a market in minutes. This was worse for the people who needed to sleep beneath the arcade, because the rain persuaded everyone that there was still time.

Adra stood at the rear of her net shop, looking up at a seam where the wall met the eaves. The wooden bowl under the stain had filled nearly to the lip. She emptied it into the gutter and set it back without a word. The shop held more than nets now. A borrowed bedroll lay beside the winding frame; wrapped letters occupied one shelf; and Jake's old box, its lid warped into a permanent question, stood on the highest ledge.

"I said you could stay," she told him. "I did not say the roof could support another household."

"I can move the bedding."

"Move it where?"

He looked toward the street. Rain had made all the usual answers absurd.

She had asked him a different question. Her shop had become the remedy for failures that belonged to other people, and his willingness to help was not a timber he could put under the rafters. He had paid her in repairs, copying and imperfect net work; he had not paid for the additional water that came down their stairs whenever his belongings occupied the driest place.

They carried the bedroll into the upper room and lifted the boxes from the wall. Below them the city was emptying its public arcade. Its roof had been damaged when the old amphitheater steps were taken apart; a season of hurried sheltering had disguised how much load the beams could still carry. No fire could be lit in the neighboring shed. The chimney had never passed a safe test. In the ward the medicines were counted twice a day, but no counting could persuade a covered cart to carry a load its axle had not been proved to bear.

At the arcade a child stood with her painted door beneath one arm, watching men stretch a sheet across the opening. It filled with water and sagged. One of the men made a joke. Nobody laughed after the first seam tore.

Jake had meant to spend the morning entering the first postseason returns. Instead he went back to Adra and asked where she kept the spare wedges.

"Where I can find them when I need them," she said, "which means you will ask before taking any."

He asked. She showed him. The wedges were not enough.

## I. Benedict Coomhersnatch and Arm Chair GM — The Carpenter's Measure

The carpenter would not sign Jake's drawing.

He laid a straightedge against it, marked the angle where Jake had proposed to brace the arcade's first upright, and snapped the charcoal between his fingers.

"You have drawn a sound roof," the carpenter said. "Unfortunately, the one outside is not sound."

Jake had copied the beam dimensions from the city's record. The carpenter had measured the surviving beam itself. Its underside was soft, and the upright had shifted under repeated loading. Neither defect appeared in the old inventory. Jake had written a proposal for shoring an architecture that existed on paper. The carpenter had brought him a shaving of wood that crumbled when touched.

At the arcade, the volunteer builders argued that temporary props could still hold the roof until the sick and their relatives found other shelter. The carpenter agreed that props might hold something. He would not let anyone remain beneath the roof while they found out what that something was.

Jake's impatience made him careless. He said people had already survived a week beneath it.

"Survived is a report about yesterday," the carpenter said. "You are asking me to sign tomorrow."

Adra arrived carrying two clean nets that could be sold for the cost of replacement pegs. She had not offered them as donations. Jake told the carpenter he could use the net frame as a light crossbar. Adra turned to him so suddenly he nearly stepped back.

"That frame is how I earn the price of the next one."

"I meant only until—"

"Until your drawing becomes a roof? It will not."

He felt the shame of someone caught turning another person's livelihood into an available material. Adra took the frame back to her shop. He could not make the phrase *public need* large enough to cover what he had done.

The first settled competitive entry came from the scoring board near the weigh house. **Benedict Coomhersnatch (home), 92.40**; **Arm Chair GM (away), 148.32**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **55.92**. The source classified this as a winners-bracket result, but gave no right to dress the carpenter in one banner's identity or to infer why a competitor had lost.

Jake entered the names and figures on his own paper. The board needed no repair. He could not say the same of the building behind it.

The carpenter eventually offered a different proposal: remove the lowest damaged section of covering entirely, place a guarded line around the unsafe area, and move the sleepers into a smaller, sounder porch beside the ward. This would keep fewer people dry. There was no way to disguise that subtraction.

A woman carrying a sleeping child asked whose porch he meant. The carpenter named the ward. She said her mother could not climb its steps. Jake offered his arm. She refused it, not unkindly, and asked for a lower passage instead. They spent the next hour lifting boards out of the way, one at a time, until she could bring her mother across without asking permission from the dangerous roof.

The carpenter inspected the path before anyone used it. He still did not sign Jake's drawing. Jake put the drawing away.

## II. Mixon it up for a week and Eggs Benedict — The Load Nobody Could Count

The flour had to be moved before the outer sacks soaked through.

At the mill, Sena kept the sacks on raised boards while three porters counted what had come from the upper road. Their tally matched the merchant's ledger until the last cart, where the weight entered at dispatch exceeded what had reached the mill. One porter wanted to call it theft. Another said the rain had taken the difference. Sena lifted a torn seam and rubbed flour between her fingers.

"Water makes it heavier," she said. "If it arrives lighter, water did not carry it away."

Jake had learned to distrust the speed of his own conclusions, but he still felt the old attraction of a simple accusation. He asked who had loaded the cart. Sena gave him three names and no culprit. A broken axle and a torn sack were facts. A man stealing food remained a story they could tell too easily.

An assistant from the creditor's office came with a purchase offer. The creditor would buy the flour at a reduced price, take on the storage risk, and charge the ward a later delivery fee. A mill worker pointed out that the same creditor held the disputed river concession. The assistant did not deny it; he said the offer was valid whether the seller liked the buyer or not. When Jake protested the fee, the assistant asked who would otherwise pay for dry storage.

Sena did not ask Jake to answer. She divided the usable flour from the damp, reserved a measured portion for the ward's established orders, and offered the remainder openly at a stated price. The rejected sacks remained visible. A baker purchased two. A porter who had come hoping for charity took none, because the money he carried was needed for coal. Jake was struck by how little the word *fair* could accomplish without bread.

At the competitive board, **Mixon it up for a week (home), 114.86**; **Eggs Benedict (away), 160.12**. Winner: **Eggs Benedict (slot 12)**. Margin **45.26**. This was a documented winners-bracket victory, one result on a possible title path, not a license to invent a motive, an owner lineage, or a victory over an unnamed later opponent.

Sena asked Jake to write the flour figures in two columns: what had arrived and what could actually be milled. Then she asked him to stop writing and lift the raised boards. His coat caught on a nail. The stitching Mara had put into it held. He tore the nail free with a tool before it caught someone else.

The assistant returned at dusk. Most of the flour was still unsold. He renewed the offer without reducing the fee.

Sena said she had until morning, and if the ward needed the remainder she would deliver the inspected sacks herself by the dry streets. The assistant looked at the sky, where there was no dry street left.

She looked there too. Her refusal was not a triumph. It was a wager measured against hunger, and Jake could see the cost in the way she checked the sacks again after the man departed.

## III. Red Leopards and The LLC — The Price of Timber

Varn brought a length of sound wood to the ruined arcade on a handcart, wrapped against the rain.

The piece was long enough to replace one failed upright, but not the roof. He said it was surplus from a work order that had been cancelled. The carpenter examined both ends, tested the grain and asked for the cancellation notice. Varn had a receipt. It proved the wood had been paid for once; it did not prove Varn had permission to give it away.

Jake asked if he might copy the receipt, and Varn said that was precisely the habit that made people mistake him for a man who owned everything he carried.

The creditor's agent came before the wood had been unloaded. He recognized the timber's mark and said it belonged to a contractor who owed rent on a yard. Varn replied that a disputed rent did not make the timber part of the landlord's body. The agent was patient. He offered to allow its use as security for the yard debt, provided the public shelter accepted responsibility for the amount.

The carpenter laid down his rule. He would not install wood whose ownership might cause it to be taken out the following week. A roof built over sleeping people could not safely be dismantled at an accountant's convenience.

That put the question back where nobody wanted it: who would purchase a beam, with real money, and who would own it afterward? Jake offered the small amount left from his former room's belongings. Adra, who had followed him to make sure he did not volunteer another net frame, heard the amount and shook her head.

"That is the money for your board," she said.

"Which board?"

"The one you broke when you thought my shop was a public supply house."

He could not argue. She had named a debt he actually owed.

The third scored return was fixed beneath the temporary awning: **Red Leopards (home), 149.38**; **The LLC (away), 117.91**. Winner: **Red Leopards (slot 2)**. Margin **31.47**. The board called this a winners-bracket contest. The city could not infer from the result any legal ruling about the wood, nor identify the lender with the LLC banner.

Varn moved the timber back onto the cart. His hands were red from pulling its wet wrapping tight. He asked the carpenter what a safe temporary repair would cost if they purchased all materials rather than borrowing them. The carpenter named the sum. It was high enough to make the group fall silent.

Then the child with the painted door asked whether the porch had to be larger. The carpenter said not if the number of people allowed beneath it were clearly limited. The child asked if people could take turns.

The mother carrying the sleeping child answered before Jake. Some could; the sick could not stand in the rain while somebody else slept.

Varn proposed dividing the work into a small ward porch for those who could not move easily and a covered, elevated store for dry medical goods. The carpenter agreed to price both separately. He did not approve either for use yet. Adra suggested a public subscription with each gift recorded as a gift, not a permanent property claim. Varn offered the first contribution after verifying that the yard had released his own timber.

Jake put away the money he owed Adra. She had asked for repayment, not a gesture.

## IV. The KING of the Octagon Village and The Blew Waffle's — A Door Carried Outside

The girl whose painted door had become a public argument carried it into the market while rain slid from its top edge.

Her mother walked behind with a bundle of clothes, and the porter followed with his flute beneath his coat. The performance court was being cleared for the temporary storeroom. People had suggested that the girl's play could continue in the ward, where the audience could sit dry. The ward's steward had refused. Fever patients were not an audience and needed their sleep.

The girl was furious. Jake heard her say that everybody else was permitted to use the building for important things, while her little door had become an inconvenience.

"Yes," her mother said. "That's what happens when a room is needed."

"You say it like it doesn't hurt."

"I didn't say it didn't."

She set the door against a market pillar and watched the rain wash a pale line through its paint. The child tried to cover the image with her sleeve. Jake offered to carry the door into Adra's shop, but the child asked whether he intended to keep it there. When he said no, she asked why her play should be housed in someone else's workroom at all.

The fourth settled archive line recorded **The Blew Waffle's (home), 110.94**; **The KING of the Octagon Village (away), 118.70**. Winner: **The KING of the Octagon Village (slot 3)**. Margin **7.76**. It belonged to the losers-consolation ladder. Its placement mattered: this was neither a championship semi-final nor a title claim. The actors under the market pillar were fictional people, not persons certified by those banners.

The child asked Jake to help lift the door onto a low wooden platform so it would not rot in the puddles. He obeyed without advising her how to finish the story. The porter took out his flute, tested three notes and stopped when she shook her head.

Instead of reciting, she asked the people sheltering under the market eaves to tell her which part of the door they could see. A man answered that the hinges were rotten. A woman said she could see only the lower paint. A young porter said the whole thing was leaning.

The girl moved back. "It looks straight from where I stand."

"No one said you had to believe us," the porter answered.

She turned the door until all three could see its face. It had stopped looking like an entrance. It looked like a thing made by a child, carried in difficult weather, with a story still waiting for room to happen.

Her mother laid the clothes under the platform to keep them dry. Jake helped, and the girl thanked him as one might thank somebody who had held the end of a heavy board, not somebody who had saved a play.

At twilight she performed nothing. The porter put his flute away. The door survived the rain, and that, on this particular day, was a complete enough undertaking.

## V. Benedict Kenobi and Benedict Arnold — What Jake Could Hold

His personal return was waiting on the board when he came back from the market.

**Benedict Kenobi (home), 137.98**; **Benedict Arnold (away), 131.86**. Winner: **Benedict Kenobi (slot 1)**. Margin **6.12**. The source classified the result as losers-consolation. The slot-one connection to Jake was a user-attested literary candidate, not authenticated historical owner identity; the separate Benedict banners were not evidence of a shared family or person.

He felt a flicker of pleasure before embarrassment overtook it. He had wanted the entire season to count for more than the number of times he prevailed. Yet he had looked for his own line first, and now he wished somebody had watched him find it.

There was nobody beside him. The carpenter was marking lumber, Adra was at work, the girl had gone home, and the creditor's agent was checking an invoice on the far side of the court. No one had turned to witness Jake's small result. It was his, if the owner line ever held true, and that was enough.

He returned to Adra with the money he had promised for the damaged board. She counted it, placed it in a jar and handed him a bent nail from the broken frame.

"You can keep this," she said.

"To remember what I did?"

"To pull the next one before it tears your coat."

She showed him how to set a small brace below the eaves, where a joint had loosened. The brace would not repair the entire roof. It could stop one board from falling while a proper carpenter inspected the timber. They placed a bucket under the drip and climbed down before dusk. Adra checked his work twice. He did not ask her to certify it for anyone else.

Later the creditor's agent came to the door. He needed a copy of the workers' individual receipts from the twelfth and fourteenth weeks. Jake had them, with certain private explanations deliberately omitted. The agent said the missing lines could make the record appear incomplete. Jake replied that the payment terms were present, the signatories could be identified, and the reasons a hungry man chose to sign were not the creditor's property.

The agent asked whether a fair record could leave something out.

"Only if it says what it is leaving out," Jake answered.

He was not certain that was a sufficient answer. The agent might have a legitimate need to know whether the receipts had been obtained under duress. A worker could also have a legitimate wish not to see his father's illness filed as a public motive. Jake offered to ask the worker what could be recorded, and to add any authorized correction without filling the blank himself.

The agent accepted the offer. He did not withdraw the concession claim.

When the door closed, Adra asked whether Jake would sleep upstairs. Jake said he would, if she still wanted the arrangement.

"I want you to fix that brace properly tomorrow," she said. "And I want my table clear before supper."

Those were terms he could meet. He met them.

## VI. El Niño — The Figure Without a Pair

The sixth entry had arrived with only one side filled in.

The archive for scoring Week 15 gave **El Niño (slot 9), 138.32**, winners-bracket, with no opposing side and **winner UNDECIDED**. Jake copied the number and left the opposite column blank. He did not call it a victory, a loss, a bye, or evidence of a semifinal already achieved; the source alone did not grant those inferences.

A boy carrying messenger envelopes asked whether a blank meant the other person was afraid.

"It can mean we do not know," Jake said.

"But someone knows."

"Possibly. Not on this sheet."

The boy inspected the list as if the paper had betrayed him. Jake remembered feeling the same anger when the courier had refused to certify a letter's author from its handwriting. A blank demanded more discipline than a number. It required a person to leave a tempting story unwritten.

At the ward entrance, Sena arrived with the flour she had held from the creditor's purchase. The sacks were fewer than she had hoped. The flour had been inspected; the mill could not make it go further by treating wet grain as healthy. Jake helped carry them inside along the newly cleared low passage. He was not carrying a league result into the ward. He was carrying a sack heavy enough to strain his old shoulder, and somebody had to tell him where to put it.

He thought the agent might return with another offer and he feared Sena would be forced to accept it. She had not asked him to determine how that choice should be narrated. He set the sack on the dry boards and asked which ones should be moved next.

## VII. Two Dreaded Snake — The Other Blank

At the last board the record was again incomplete: **Two Dreaded Snake (slot 6), 88.04**, losers-consolation, without an opponent and with **winner UNDECIDED**. Nothing about the name established the physical appearance or human ownership of any later serpent figure. Jake marked it as an unpaired scoring record, not a decided encounter.

There had once been a time when a partial return would have sent him searching for a person to blame. Now he thought of the wet shelf in Adra's shop and the inadequate timber at the arcade. A broken thing could be described faithfully without anyone claiming to have fixed it.

The ferryman's daughter was tying a second safety loop to the grounded punt when he passed the landing. He asked whether she wanted a hand. She asked whether he had learned to tie the knot Adra showed him. He made it on a dry length of rope while she watched.

"Better," she said. "Not for a boat yet."

A lantern burned on the far bank. Jake could not know who had lit it or why. The courier was somewhere on the long upper route with the unopened letter; he might never find its writer. The river's concession remained disputed, its proper ferry lost, its punt grounded and its passenger crossing closed. Nothing in the first postseason scoring returns altered those conditions.

He spent the evening helping move the children's bedding from the unsafe arcade to the smaller porch. One woman chose to take her child home rather than accept the offered place; another could not travel and used it. Neither choice stood for every household in the Commonwealth.

Adra had kept a place for his supper. He arrived late and did not describe himself as indispensable. He washed the mud from his boots before he entered.

### After the First Postseason Reckoning

The archived 2023 Week 15 packet contains **seven scoring records, five decided pairings and two single-sided UNDECIDED entries**. The three winners-bracket decided fixtures and two losers-consolation decided fixtures are separately represented above. The unpaired El Niño and Two Dreaded Snake scores are preserved without fabricated opponents or outcomes.

In the invented city, the arcade's unsafe roof is partly isolated, the ward porch serves fewer people than hoped, Adra's tool and timber remain her property, and Sena's grain reaches the ward without curing the shortage. The river crossing does not reopen. Jake repays Adra for a board he damaged, learns to ask before borrowing labor, and makes a limited personal victory without treating it as communal triumph. These are **NONCANON PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**, not league facts or claims about real owners.

# 2023 Postseason, Second Reckoning — The Fire That Would Not Travel

**DRAFT / NONCANON — historical Atlas and independent literary HOLD.** Proposed Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER limited viewpoint, historical slot-one identity unverified. The competition entries are sourced archival facts; the labor, ward, river, city, conversations and interior life are proposed fiction.

A wheel broke on the dry side of the gate.

That was what made the failure so difficult to forgive. The cart had not reached the disputed crossing, had not challenged the river, had not even left the ward's own yard. Its wheel simply leaned away from the axle when the porters lifted one side to test the load, and a bag of sand fell into the mud.

The cart had been built to carry medicines and other supplies along the upper road. No sick person was riding it; no passenger passage had been approved; nobody had ordered the ferryman's daughter to bring the grounded punt into service. Jake had made certain of all those restrictions in the trial instructions. He had mistaken their clarity for an assurance that the vehicle would work.

Bren, the foundry worker, held the wheel while the others pulled the axle free.

"The hole is wider than the pin," he said.

Jake knelt to see. What he had thought was a fixed sleeve was a patched one, worn oval by earlier loads. The cartwright had warned them the old axle might need replacing. Jake had entered the warning in his notes but left the test on the schedule because the ward needed transport.

He remembered the carpenter's straightedge against his drawing, the disintegrating beam, the neat imaginary roof. He had not learned enough from either.

A porter stood in the rain looking at the spilled sand. He asked whether Jake planned to copy the failed test into another ledger.

"I do," Jake said.

"Will the ledger carry the bag to the ward?"

Jake picked up the bag. The porter took the other handle without accepting his apology. Together they lifted it onto the dry stones.

The first semifinal scores would arrive that evening. They would be true whether or not the cart moved. Jake disliked that separate certainty. He wanted a world in which getting the numbers right somehow taught wood and iron to behave.

## I. El Niño and Arm Chair GM — The Measured Failure

The first semifinal return arrived while Bren was heating a replacement pin.

**El Niño (home), 148.24**; **Arm Chair GM (away), 159.52**. Winner: **Arm Chair GM (slot 11)**. Margin **11.28**. This was a winners-bracket semifinal, a certified advancement in the archived contest. The El Niño banner was not a human official, a storm that caused the axle to fail, or a voice any person could consult.

The ward clerk asked Jake to read the numbers aloud. The clerk kept a separate slate for competition returns, because people sheltering at the ward had asked for news of the postseason. The two records belonged on different walls: one held a contest's outcome, the other the quantities of flour, water and fuel actually available to the sick.

After he spoke, Jake went back to the foundry. Bren had put the cracked sleeve and the new iron on a bench, but he had not begun fitting them together.

"I'll pay for it," Jake said.

"With what?"

"I can work."

"Then work when the metal is ready. Your need to feel useful won't change its temperature."

Bren's voice was neither cruel nor tender. He had a queue of other work: a mill hinge, a stove damper that had failed inspection, tools promised to a stone crew. The ward cart was urgent, but it was not the only demand on the forge. Jake began carrying scrap to the sorting bench. He was angry to be doing such a small thing and then angry at himself for being angry.

A woman with a wrapped hand came in to collect a repaired latch. She waited without asking to move ahead of the ward's cart. Bren told her the latch was ready, and she produced two coins. He gave her the finished piece. She asked what had happened to the wheel.

"Old iron," Bren said.

"I have some," she answered. "Not the right shape."

"No."

She left the coins anyway and took her latch. Jake asked whether Bren would count them toward the cart. Bren said the coins were payment for work already completed. He would not take them twice.

When the pin had cooled enough to inspect, its head was sound but the sleeve remained defective. Bren insisted on replacing both. That meant another day and another purchase. The clerk who had brought the semifinal result waited by the door, looking from the wheel to Jake's notebook. The result was final; the repair was not. Jake could live with only one of those truths at a time.

## II. Red Leopards and Eggs Benedict — The Red Line on the Floor

The second semifinal return was even less ambiguous: **Red Leopards (home), 149.84**; **Eggs Benedict (away), 214.52**. Winner: **Eggs Benedict (slot 12)**. Margin **64.68**. The source established the other winners-bracket finalist. The points could not tell Jake anything about the competitor's private reasoning, owner, costume, home territory or political intention.

At the ward, Nessa had stretched a red cord across the floor beneath the cracked beam that no one was allowed to approach. A man carrying blankets stepped over it to reach a warmer corner. Nessa stopped him.

"It's dry there," he said.

"It's dangerous there."

"It's dangerous outside."

She knew this, but the cord stayed. Jake had written a notice in careful letters explaining the load risk. The man had not read it. He asked Nessa whether she had somewhere else for his wife, whose breath had been shallow all morning.

Nessa led them to a lower room that smelled of damp stone and clean vinegar. It was smaller and colder but had a sound ceiling. They were able to move the bedroll there. Jake helped shift the blankets, trying to keep his coat away from the bowl of water balanced on the frame. He looked again at the red cord and saw that it was not a commandment. It was the edge of what the building could safely offer.

Later the creditor's agent arrived with a delivery note. The agent had found a supplier willing to provide proper axle iron immediately, but only at a price that included security against the disputed landing accounts. Jake expected Nessa to refuse. She unfolded the paper and read it slowly.

"Why would the landing secure a cart that may never cross the river?" she asked.

"Because your buyers have no other collateral."

"That isn't an answer about the cart."

"It is an answer about the loan."

For the first time Jake saw how an institution could be both plainly self-serving and brutally attentive to risk. The agent had come with a usable offer. Its terms might leave the city owing more than the repair would save. Neither fact erased the other.

Nessa asked whether the ward could purchase the sleeve alone without the financing. The agent said the supplier would sell it for cash. She asked for the price and an invoice without any claim on the landing. He wrote both, and she carried them to the ward's small council.

Jake was not invited to the decision. He stood outside with a clean water pail and watched people go in and out carrying evidence of their own. Someone inside was asking whether the medicines could be sent in smaller loads on foot. Someone else said the foot carriers were already exhausted. He could hear questions, not answers.

The eventual decision was narrow: solicit separate gifts for the sleeve, transport the lighter medicine packets by hand for another day, and leave the creditor's proposal unaccepted. No law changed. The ward still lacked iron. Jake thought of the two semifinal scores, which had closed the paths of two competitors in a single evening. Civic needs did not end so cleanly.

## III. Mixon it up for a week and Benedict Coomhersnatch — The Weight of Names

The old courier came back by the upper road with a swollen ankle and no answer about the letter.

He had taken the unclaimed paper to three places where it might have entered his bundle. At one house a woman recognized the outside mark but not the handwriting. At another a child said the folded corner looked familiar. At the third a man wanted the courier to open the letter so they could settle the matter quickly. The courier refused.

Jake offered him the stool near Adra's table. The courier sat, loosened his boot, and placed the still-folded letter between them. No seal had been broken.

"I told the man you asked me not to open it," the courier said. "He answered that perhaps you had no right to decide."

"Perhaps I didn't."

"Then who did?"

They regarded the paper. An address might be inside, but they had no consent to search it. They could return it to a place that was not necessarily its source, or keep it where the claimant might never come. It was an ordinary impossibility, made heavy by the roads that people had to walk to express it.

Adra brought the courier hot water and asked whether his ankle could bear another trip. He said it could after rest. She answered that rest was not a price she would accept in exchange for a quicker letter.

The third decided result was **Mixon it up for a week (home), 121.48**; **Benedict Coomhersnatch (away), 132.02**. Winner: **Benedict Coomhersnatch (slot 5)**. Margin **10.54**. The archive placed it in the winners-consolation ladder, distinct from the championship semifinals. The similar Benedict names did not make the courier, the writer, or Jake an authenticated relation of any rival banner.

Jake copied the figures while the courier rested. A page from the old upper-road map lay beneath the score sheet. It showed light stations with blank space beyond the last confirmed marker. He had once considered that blank a defect to be corrected. Now he asked the courier to add only the places he had walked, with the knowledge that even his testimony would not exhaust every possible path.

The courier marked two lanes, then stopped. He had met a young woman who asked that the location of her household not be included in any public map. She could travel without wanting her doorway marked. Jake left a gap. The upper route became less comprehensive and more trustworthy.

The letter remained with the courier for one more attempt, but not as a demand that he walk before he healed. Adra offered him the corner near the fireless upper room, dry if not warm. He slept there for a few hours, his boots on the floor and the letter beneath his hand.

## IV. The LLC — A Number Without Judgment

The fourth entry in the archival Week 16 record was **The LLC (slot 4), 140.52**, winners-consolation, with no opponent row and **winner UNDECIDED**. It did not establish a win, loss, bye, advance, or an offscreen participant. Jake left the other side empty.

The creditor's agent visited the foundry with a message from the ward. No one had gathered sufficient cash for the replacement sleeve. He had not withdrawn the original financing offer; he had brought a second paper showing the supplier's current price without credit.

"You can make both offers at once?" Jake asked.

"One is for money," the agent said. "The other is for a promise."

"A promise that includes land the ward does not own."

"I did not draw the ward's account."

"Someone did."

The agent folded the paper. Jake knew very little about the man whose work consisted of bringing bad choices from one room into another. The agent might go home to people he loved or to a room as bare as Jake's former lodging. Nothing about either possibility could be asserted from the words *creditor's agent*. Jake was tired of inventing the human being he needed for his argument.

Bren emerged holding the damaged sleeve. He showed the agent where metal had worn away. The agent looked closely, then named a merchant who bought old iron. Bren thanked him for the information without promising to sell. The scene ended without friendship or settlement.

Jake wrote down the supplier's unfinanced price, not the agent's biography. Beside it he left a blank where the source of payment would go.

## V. The KING of the Octagon Village and Benedict Kenobi — The Defeat That Did Not Stay on the Board

He learned of his own loss from a child who asked whether the figures had been copied correctly.

**The KING of the Octagon Village (home), 181.20**; **Benedict Kenobi (away), 129.70**. Winner: **The KING of the Octagon Village (slot 3)**. Margin **51.50**. The archive marked it as losers-consolation, not a title contest. Jake's connection to slot one remained a literary, user-attested possibility rather than provider proof.

He felt no noble resignation. He felt the familiar little heat of wanting the number changed, and beneath it a childish wish to find a larger, more important contest in which he had won. He looked toward the ward and hated himself a little for treating other people's danger as a more flattering kind of victory.

Adra was planing a new edge for the net frame he had damaged. He told her about the result. She asked whether he wanted her to say she was sorry.

"I don't know."

"Then I won't."

He helped hold the wood steady. The plane made a long strip, thin as a letter ribbon, and left behind a surface smooth enough to take a new peg. The sound reminded him of a door closing softly in a room he no longer owned.

He asked if she had ever kept a piece of work because she had spent too long on it to see that it was wrong.

"Yes," she said.

"What did you do?"

"Sold it cheaper, if it was safe. Took it apart if it wasn't."

"And if somebody had already paid?"

"I told them what I had done."

She did not ask him to compare his losses to her workmanship. He did that himself, and the comparison made him feel foolish. The tally was a tally. The frame was a frame. Neither deserved to be turned into proof of an entire man's character.

They finished the repair late. Adra inspected the new peg, shook it once and put it into use. The cart at the foundry still waited for a sleeve. Jake's competition outcome had changed nothing about its metal. The day offered him no excuse to disappear into disappointment.

## VI. The Blew Waffle's and Two Dreaded Snake — The Rope Not for Crossing

At the lower landing, the ferryman's daughter had built a small rig between two posts. The rope did not span the river. It ran parallel to the near-bank stones, a short distance above ground, and allowed one person to pull a crate out of the mud without bearing its entire weight.

She asked Jake to try it with a box of stones, not with medicine and not with a person. He took the rope and leaned backward. The box shifted and stopped. He pulled harder. The rope cut into his palm. She told him to stand straighter and use his legs.

The crate moved. He could see the value immediately, and then the danger: a clever way to lift a load could become an excuse to move a greater load than the supports would tolerate.

"What does it hold?" he asked.

"Today, this box," she said. "Tomorrow, if I have measured and tested the anchors, perhaps another box. That's the whole answer."

Jake wanted her to bring the rig to the ward. She said it might be useful for short lifts but would not repair the cart axle or approve the disputed road. Her father had lost a ferry, and people had begun speaking of every new knot as though it were a replacement vessel. She would not let his loss make them careless about things that remained.

The sixth result was **The Blew Waffle's (home), 124.38**; **Two Dreaded Snake (away), 161.98**. Winner: **Two Dreaded Snake (slot 6)**. Margin **37.60**. This was a losers-consolation ladder result, no more and no less. The archived banner did not confer a three-headed body, a coastal homeland, or a real-world owner's private behavior upon the imagined shore.

Jake coiled the rope after the trial. The daughter kept the rig installed only for further unloaded inspection. No cargo moved along the river and no passenger crossing was permitted. He was disappointed and relieved in equal measure, an uncomfortable combination he had come to recognize as the beginning of caution.

The daughter pointed toward the ward cart's broken wheel leaning against the foundry wall. "Can he fix that without another promise about the river?"

"Bren says he can. We need the iron."

"Then get iron. Don't ask the river to become a wheel."

## VII. Benedict Arnold — The Last Unpaired Entry

The remaining score line read **Benedict Arnold (slot 8), 95.30**, losers-consolation, **winner UNDECIDED** with no opponent recorded in that row. Jake entered it as an unpaired archive observation. He did not infer a result, a match, or a person's motives from its solitary appearance.

That evening Adra brought him an invoice paid in small amounts. Varn had found two household buyers for a portion of the repaired netting, and one had paid extra for a stronger border. She set the proceeds beside the sum still owed for the new frame.

"This is what a shop looks like," she said. "Sometimes the money comes in. Sometimes it doesn't. The roof still leaks."

Jake had never heard such a mercilessly ordinary description of courage.

The ward sent word that Bren could use a narrow batch of scrap iron already belonging to the cartwright, provided it passed his inspection and the cartwright approved the work. Jake hurried to the foundry, where Bren was already examining it. The iron would be enough for a sleeve if its grain held. It was not enough for every other broken thing on the list.

Jake helped hold the lamp. Bren found a hairline defect in one piece and set it aside. The rest could be forged, but only the following morning.

"Does that mean the cart is saved?" Jake asked.

"It means I can begin making a sleeve."

He wanted more. The ward wanted more. But Bren did not give either of them a future he could not certify.

The courier rested upstairs in Adra's shop. The child with the painted door was drawing a new hinge. Nessa had cleared another room beneath a safe ceiling. Sena had delivered the flour without inventing a second supply. The creditors still claimed the landing. The proper ferry was lost, the punt grounded, the upper-road passage conditional and the unheated shelter restricted.

And on the other side of the city's unrelated competitive ledger, two finalists were now known. The world seemed to Jake full of doors that opened for one purpose and stayed closed for another.

### After the Second Postseason Reckoning

Archival Week 16 contains **seven scoring records, five decided pairings and two single-sided UNDECIDED entries**. The two winners-bracket semifinal outcomes, one winners-consolation result and two losers-consolation results are preserved individually. The unpaired LLC and Benedict Arnold figures have **no invented opponents or winners**.

The fictional ward cart remains unusable pending a tested new sleeve. The foundry has material for a possible repair, not a certified road solution. No passenger crossing has opened; no legal settlement or wage reconciliation has been invented; the unheated shelter remains restricted, and the creditor's financing offer is unaccepted. The unopened letter remains with a resting courier rather than becoming an all-knowing document. These are **NONCANON PROPOSED FICTION / HISTORICAL ATLAS HOLD**.
